S_{0}=\left\{\bm{P}\in\{\bm{I},\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z}\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{\bm{I}^{\otimes n}\}\,\,|\,\,\alpha_{\bm{P}}(\bm{H})\neq 0\,\,\text{or}\,\,\alpha_{\bm{P}}(\bm{O})\neq 0\right\},

(C.11)

and
S_{E}
be the set of Pauli operator
\bm{P}
with non-zero Pauli coefficients
\alpha_{\bm{P}}
in the Pauli decompositions of
\bm{h}_{a}
for some
a
,

S_{E}=\left\{\bm{P}\in\{\bm{I},\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z}\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{\bm{I}^{\otimes n}\}\,\,|\,\,\exists 1\leq a\leq m,\alpha_{\bm{P}}(\bm{h}_{a})\neq 0\right\}.

(C.12)

Because
\bm{H}
is a local Hamiltonian and
\bm{O}
is a local observable, we have
|S_{0}|=\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
Because
\bm{h}_{a}
is a local observable, we have
|S_{E}|=\mathcal{O}(m)=\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
We then define,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\Big\{\bm{P}^{\prime}\in\{\bm{I},\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z}\}^{\otimes n}\setminus\{\bm{I}^{\otimes n}\}\,\,|\,\,\exists\bm{Q}\in S_{0},\bm{P}\in S_{E},\Tr(\bm{P}^{\prime}[\bm{Q},\bm{P}])\neq 0\Big\}\cup S_{0}.

(C.13)

Because
|S_{0}|=\mathrm{poly}(n)
and
[\bm{Q},\bm{P}]
is another Pauli observable up to a phase, we have
|S|\leq|S_{E}||S_{1}|=\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
The union bound yields the following probabilistic statement,

1-\Pr_{\ket{\psi}}\left[\left|\bra{\psi}\bm{Q}\ket{\psi}\right|<\frac{1}{2^{n/3}},\,\,\forall\bm{Q}\in S\right]\leq 2|S|\exp\left(-\frac{2^{n/3}}{10}\right)\leq\frac{\mathrm{poly}(n)}{2^{2^{n/3}/10}}<\frac{1}{2^{2^{n/4}}},

(C.14)

where the last inequality holds for any large
n
since
2^{2^{n/3}/10-2^{n/4}}
grows much faster than any polynomial of
n
.
We condition on the event for the random state
\ket{\psi}
that

\left|\bra{\psi}\bm{Q}\ket{\psi}\right|<\frac{1}{2^{n/3}}\quad\text{for all}\quad\bm{Q}\in S,

(C.15)

referred to as event
E^{*}
. We can obtain the following from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\left|\bra{\psi}[\bm{H},\bm{h}_{a}]\ket{\psi}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\bm{Q},\bm{P}\in\{\bm{I},\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z}\}^{\otimes n}}\left|\alpha_{\bm{Q}}(\bm{H})\right|\left|\alpha_{\bm{P}}(\bm{h}_{a})\right|\left|\bra{\psi}[\bm{Q},\bm{P}]\ket{\psi}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{\bm{Q},\bm{P}\in\{\bm{I},\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z}\}^{\otimes n}}\alpha_{\bm{Q}}^{2}(\bm{H})\alpha_{\bm{P}}^{2}(\bm{h}_{a})}\sqrt{\frac{|S|}{2^{2n/3}}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{poly}(n)}{2^{n/3}},

(C.16)

where the second inequality uses the conditioning on event
E^{*}
and
[\bm{Q},\bm{P}]\neq 0\implies\bm{Q},\bm{P}\neq\bm{I}^{\otimes n}
, and the third inequality uses
|S|=\mathrm{poly}(n)
and Eq. (
C.8
), (
C.10
).
Similarly, we also have

\left|\bra{\psi}[[\bm{H},\bm{h}_{a}],\bm{h}_{a^{\prime}}]\ket{\psi}\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{poly}(n)}{2^{n/3}}.

(C.17)

Using Eq. (
C.9
) instead of Eq. (
C.8
), we can similarly obtain

\left|\bra{\psi}\bm{O}\ket{\psi}-\alpha_{I^{\otimes n}}(\bm{O})\right|=\left|\bra{\psi}\bm{O}\ket{\psi}-\frac{\Tr(O)}{2^{n}}\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{poly}(n)}{2^{n/3}}<\frac{1}{2^{n/4}}\leq\epsilon

(C.18)

for any large problem size
n
since
2^{n/3}
grows much faster than any polynomial in
n
.
We now show that
\ket{\psi}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under local unitary perturbations.
To establish this claim, from Def.
5
, we need to prove that

\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})^{\dagger}\bm{H}\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})\geq\bra{\psi}\bm{H}\ket{\psi}-\epsilon\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1},\,\,\text{for each}\quad{\bm{\alpha}}\in T_{\psi},\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\delta

(C.19)

for some
\delta>0
.
Recall from Lemma
C.1
based on Taylor’s theorem (Prop.
D.1
), we have

\displaystyle\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})^{\dagger}\bm{H}\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=\bra{\psi}\bm{H}\ket{\psi}-\mathrm{i}\bra{\psi}\left[\bm{H},\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{h}_{a}\right]\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a=1}^{m}\sum_{a^{\prime}=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\alpha_{a^{\prime}}\exp_{\psi}(\eta\hat{\bm{\alpha}})^{\dagger}[[\bm{H},\bm{h}_{a}],\bm{h}_{a^{\prime}}]\exp_{\psi}(\eta\hat{\bm{\alpha}}),

(C.20)

for some
0\leq\eta\leq\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}
.
For the linear term, from Eq. (
C.16
) bounding
|\bra{\psi}[\bm{H},\bm{h}_{a}]\ket{\psi}|
, we have

\left|-\mathrm{i}\bra{\psi}\left[\bm{H},\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{h}_{a}\right]\ket{\psi}\right|\leq\sum_{a=1}^{m}|\alpha_{a}|\frac{\mathrm{poly}(n)}{2^{n/3}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{poly}(n)}{2^{n/3}}\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1},

(C.21)

where the last inequality uses
m=\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
For the quadratic residual term, we have

\left|\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a=1}^{m}\sum_{a^{\prime}=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\alpha_{a^{\prime}}\exp_{\psi}(\eta\hat{\bm{\alpha}})^{\dagger}[[\bm{H},\bm{h}_{a}],\bm{h}_{a^{\prime}}]\exp_{\psi}(\eta\hat{\bm{\alpha}})\right|\leq 2\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathrm{poly}(n)\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert^{2}_{1}.

(C.22)

Together, we can combine the inequalities to get

\displaystyle\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})^{\dagger}\bm{H}\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})

\displaystyle\geq\bra{\psi}\bm{H}\ket{\psi}-\frac{\mathrm{poly}(n)}{2^{n/3}}\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}-\mathrm{poly}(n)\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\bra{\psi}\bm{H}\ket{\psi}-0.5\epsilon\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}-\mathrm{poly}(n)\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}^{2},

(C.23)

where the second inequality holds for any large problem size
n
since
\epsilon\geq 1/2^{n/4}
decays much slower than
\mathrm{poly}(n)/2^{n/3}
.
For any
\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert<\delta:=0.5\epsilon/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, we have

\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})^{\dagger}\bm{H}\exp_{\psi}({\bm{\alpha}})\geq\bra{\psi}\bm{H}\ket{\psi}-0.5\epsilon\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}-0.5\epsilon\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}=\bra{\psi}\bm{H}\ket{\psi}-\epsilon\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1},

(C.24)

which shows that
\ket{\psi}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under local unitary perturbations.
Finally, because the event
E^{*}
occurs with probability at least
0.99
, by combining Eq. (
C.18
) and the above, we establish the claim that, with high probability, a random
n
-qubit state
\ket{\psi}
sampled uniformly is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under local unitary perturbations and
\bra{\psi}\bm{O}\ket{\psi}
is
\epsilon
-close to
\Tr(O)/2^{n}
.
∎

Appendix D
Characterizing local minima under thermal perturbations

In this appendix, we characterize local minima under thermal perturbations.
In particular, we will focus on the gradients of the energy landscape, conditions of local minima, and conditions on the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
that ensure approximate local minima are approximate global minima, i.e., there are no suboptimal local minima in the energy landscape.

D.1
Energy gradients

The energy landscape is much more nontrivial when defined under thermal perturbations.
We can study the energy landscape by looking at the energy gradients.
Recall the exponential map
\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}
in Eq. (
B.25
) and consider the one-dimensional function

g(t)=\Tr\left(\bm{H}\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}(t\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})\right)=\Tr\left(\bm{H}\exp\left(\sum_{a=1}^{m}t\hat{\alpha}_{a}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}\right)({\bm{\rho}})\right)

(D.1)

for
0\leq t\leq\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1},\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}={\bm{\alpha}}/\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}
.
We have the following derivatives,

\displaystyle\frac{dg}{dt}(t)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(\bm{H}\sum_{a}\hat{\alpha}_{a}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{a=1}^{m}t\hat{\alpha}_{a}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}\right)({\bm{\rho}})\right]\right),

(D.2)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}g}{dt^{2}}(t)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(\bm{H}\sum_{a}\sum_{a^{\prime}}\hat{\alpha}_{a}\hat{\alpha}_{a^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{a^{\prime}}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}\left[\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{a=1}^{m}t\hat{\alpha}_{a}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}\right)({\bm{\rho}})\right]\right]\right).

(D.3)

Recall Taylor’s theorem with the Lagrange form of the remainder from standard single-variate calculus.
By applying Taylor’s theorem in Prop.
D.1
to
g(t)
, we can obtain Prop.
D.2
.

Proposition D.1

(Taylor’s theorem)
.

Let
g(t):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be twice differentiable on the open interval between
0
and
t
and
g^{\prime}(t)
continuous on the closed interval between
0
and
t
. Then

g(t)=g(0)+g^{\prime}(0)t+\frac{1}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(\eta)t^{2},

(D.4)

for some real number
\eta
between
0
and
t
.

Proposition D.2

(Taylor’s theorem for thermal perturbations)
.

Given an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
, parameters
\beta,\tau\geq 0
,
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
, and an
n
-qubit state
{{\bm{\rho}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{2^{n}}
.

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\bm{H}\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}({\bm{\alpha}})\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})+\sum_{a}\alpha_{a}\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}])

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a}\sum_{a^{\prime}}\alpha_{a}\alpha_{a^{\prime}}\Tr\left(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{\prime}}[\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}(\eta\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})]]\right)

(D.5)

for some
0\leq\eta\leq\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}
.

We define the energy gradients as follows. We separately consider a positive and a negative energy gradient. The motivation of the definition is that the positive (negative) energy gradient should determine the direction of the thermodynamics that causes the energy of the state to increase (decrease).
Because our goal is to understand local minima, we will focus on the negative energy gradient. When one studies local maxima, one will focus on the positive energy gradient.

Definition 8

(Energy gradients of a state under thermal perturbations)
.

Given an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
, and parameters
\beta,\tau\geq 0
, the gradients of an
n
-qubit state
{{\bm{\rho}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{2^{n}}
under thermal perturbations with inverse temperature
\beta
, time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}^{+}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\max\left(+\Tr\left(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}]\right),0\right)\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a},

(positive energy gradient)

(D.6)

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\max\left(-\Tr\left(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}]\right),0\right)\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a},

(negative energy gradient)

(D.7)

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\Tr\left(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}]\right)\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a},

(energy gradient)

(D.8)

where
\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a}
is the unit vector along the
a
-th coordinate.

Since the set of jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
will be fixed, we will sometimes drop the dependence on
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
for notational simplicity.
The positive/negative energy gradient belongs to the tangent space
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
, but the energy gradient

{\bm{\nabla}}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})={\bm{\nabla}}^{+}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})-{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})

(D.9)

may not be in the tangent space due to negative values. So, in general, one could not move in the direction of the energy gradient.
However, one could move in the direction of the positive or negative energy gradient. It is instructive to think about the Heisenberg picture and define the
energy gradient operator
.

Definition 9

(Energy gradient operator)
.

Given an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
, inverse temperature
\beta\geq 0,
and time scale
\tau\geq 0
, the energy gradient operators under thermal perturbations is

\sum_{a=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[\bm{H}]\,\,\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a},

(D.10)

which is a vector of
n
-qubit Hermitian operators.

We can provide an upper and lower bound to the energy gradients by combining Prop.
F.2
and Prop.
F.3
to obtain the following proposition.

Proposition D.3

(Bound on the energy gradients)
.

Given an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
, inverse temperature
\beta\geq 0,
and time scale
\tau\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}(\bm{H})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}

(D.11)

for all
a=1,\ldots,m
.

The
\beta,\tau\rightarrow\infty
limit (zero temperature heat bath with an infinite time scale) recovers the Davies’ generator
\mathcal{L}^{\infty,\infty,\bm{H}}_{a}
.
The Davies’ generator takes an energy eigenvector
\lvert\psi_{j}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{j}\rvert
of
\bm{H}
to energy eigenvectors with equal or lower energy, i.e., for any
t\geq 0
,

\bra{\psi_{k}}\exp\left(t\mathcal{L}^{\infty,\infty,\bm{H}}_{a}\right)\left(\lvert\psi_{j}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{j}\rvert\right)\ket{\psi_{k}}=0\quad\text{for any}\quad j,k\quad\text{such that}\quad E_{k}>E_{j}.

(D.12)

We can use the above to obtain the following proposition.

Proposition D.4

(Vanishing positive energy gradient)
.

For
\beta=\tau=\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\infty,\infty,\bm{H}{\dagger}}_{a}[\bm{H}]\preceq 0,\quad\text{for each}\quad a.

(D.13)

Hence, the positive energy gradient vanishes
{\bm{\nabla}}^{+}_{\infty,\infty}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})=0
and
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\infty,\infty}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})=-{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\infty,\infty}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})
for all Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
and state
{\bm{\rho}}
.

This proposition illustrates that thermal perturbations induced by a zero-temperature heat bath with an infinite time scale should only absorb energy from the quantum system and not cause the energy to increase. Hence, the positive energy gradient must vanish.

D.2
A sufficient condition and a necessary condition of local minima

Using the negative gradient, we can show a necessary condition and a sufficient condition for local minima under thermal perturbations.
They differ only slightly (
<
vs
\leq
).
From the conditions, we can see that local minima are well characterized by the negative energy gradient.
Recall that
\left\lVert{\bm{x}}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i}|x_{i}|
is the
\ell_{\infty}
norm for a finite-dimensional vector
{\bm{x}}
.

Lemma D.1

(A sufficient condition for local minima under thermal perturbations)
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
, and parameters
\beta,\tau\geq 0
, an
n
-qubit state
{\bm{\rho}}
with a small negative energy gradient,

\left\lVert{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})\right\rVert_{\infty}<\epsilon,

(D.14)

is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{{\bm{\rho}}}
of the
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with inverse temperature
\beta
, time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
.

Proof.

Consider
C_{L}=\max_{a}\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}\right\rVert_{1-1}>0
and
C_{H}=\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}
.
Given
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{a}\sum_{a^{\prime}}\alpha_{a}\alpha_{a^{\prime}}\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{\prime}}[\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[\bm{\sigma}]])\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}^{2}C_{H}\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}^{2},

(D.15)

for any state
\bm{\sigma}
.
Let
\epsilon_{0}:=\left\lVert{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})\right\rVert_{\infty}<\epsilon
.
From
\alpha_{a}\geq 0
and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\sum_{a}\alpha_{a}\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}])\geq-\sum_{a}\left|\alpha_{a}\right|\max\left(-\Tr\left(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{i}[{\bm{\rho}}]\right),0\right)\geq-\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}\epsilon_{0}.

(D.16)

Together, Taylor’s theorem for thermal perturbations (Prop.
D.2
) implies

\Tr\left(\bm{H}\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}({\bm{\alpha}})\right)\geq\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})-\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}\epsilon_{0}-\frac{\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert^{2}_{1}}{2}C_{L}^{2}C_{H},

(D.17)

for any
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
.
From the above, we see that for any
\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}<\delta:=\frac{2(\epsilon-\epsilon_{0})}{C_{L}^{2}C_{H}}
,

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\bm{H}\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}({\bm{\alpha}})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})-\epsilon\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}\left((\epsilon-\epsilon_{0})-\frac{C_{L}^{2}C_{H}}{2}\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})-\epsilon\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}.

(D.18)

So,
{\bm{\rho}}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with inverse temperature
\beta
, time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
.
∎

Lemma D.2

(A necessary condition for local minima under thermal perturbations)
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
, and parameters
\beta,\tau\geq 0
, an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{{\bm{\rho}}}
of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with inverse temperature
\beta
, time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
satisfies

\left\lVert{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon,

(D.19)

which differs only slightly from the condition in Eq. (
D.14
).

Proof.

Recall that
{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
.
Let
a^{*}=\argmax_{a}\left({\nabla}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})_{a}\right)
.
If the negative energy gradient vector
\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}_{a^{*}}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[{\bm{\rho}}])
is zero, then the claim holds.
Hence, we only need to consider the case when the negative energy gradient vector is nonzero.
In this case,

0<\left\lVert{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})\right\rVert_{\infty}={\nabla}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})_{a^{*}}=-\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}_{a^{*}}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[{\bm{\rho}}]).

(D.20)

Consider
\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}:=\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a^{*}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
, which satisfies
\left\lVert\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{1}=1
. We have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Tr(\bm{H}\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}(t\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}))-\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})}{t}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}_{a^{*}}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[{\bm{\rho}}])

\displaystyle=-\left\lVert{\bm{\nabla}}^{-}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(D.21)

At the same time, for any
t>0
, we also have

\frac{\Tr(\bm{H}\exp^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}_{\bm{\rho}}(t\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}))-\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})}{t}\geq-\epsilon\left\lVert\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}\right\rVert_{1}=-\epsilon.

(D.22)

Together, we obtain the desired claim.
∎

D.3
Hamiltonians without suboptimal local minima

An important concept in classical optimization is to understand when all local minima are global minima.
For example, in convex optimization, checking the convexity of the objective function
h({\bm{x}})
ensures that all local minima are global minima.
When all local minima are global minima, it is commonly referred to as having no suboptimal local minima in the optimization landscape.
For optimizing quantum Hamiltonians, we can define a similar concept.
Let us begin with a definition of approximate global minimum.

Definition 10

(Approximate global minimum of Hamiltonians)
.

Given
\epsilon,\delta>0
and an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
with minimum energy
E_{0}
.
Let
\bm{P}_{G+\epsilon}(\bm{H})
be the projector to the subspace of energy eigenstates of
\bm{H}
with energy at most
E_{0}+\epsilon
.
An
n
-qubit state
{\bm{\rho}}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate global minimum of
\bm{H}
with failure probability
\leq\delta
if
\Tr(\bm{P}_{G+\epsilon}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\delta
.

Definition 11

(No suboptimal local minima)
.

Given
\epsilon>0
.
We say an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
has no suboptimal
\epsilon
-approximate local minima with failure probability
\delta
if any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of
\bm{H}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate global minimum of
\bm{H}
with failure probability
\leq\delta
, i.e.,
\Tr(\bm{P}_{G+\epsilon}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\delta
.

While the above definitions apply to any Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
, in this work, we will focus on Hamiltonians with a gap
\Delta>0
between the minimum energy and the second minimum energy, also known as the spectral gap.
By definition of
\bm{P}_{G+\epsilon}(\bm{H})
and spectral gap
\Delta
, we have

\epsilon<\Delta\implies\bm{P}_{G+\epsilon}(\bm{H})=\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}).

(D.23)

As we will almost always consider
\epsilon<\Delta
, any
\epsilon
-approximate global minimum is an
0
-approximate global minimum or
exact
global minimum.

Convexity implies all local minima are global in classical optimization. In the following lemma, we present a sufficient condition for ensuring that all local minima are global in quantum systems.
As we can see, all we need is to check the negative gradient operator is sufficiently positive in the non-ground-state space
\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{G}
.
We will refer to this as the
negative gradient condition
.

Lemma D.3

(A sufficient condition ensuring all local minima are global)
.

Given
\epsilon,\delta>0
, an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
, and parameters
\beta,\tau\geq 0
.
Let
\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H})
be the projection onto the ground state space of
\bm{H}
.
If there exists
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
with
\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}=1
, such that the negative gradient operator satisfies

\text{(negative gradient condition):}\quad-\sum_{a}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]\succeq\frac{2\epsilon}{\delta}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}))-\epsilon\bm{I},

(D.24)

then any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of the
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with inverse temperature
\beta
, time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
is an exact global minimum with failure probability
\leq\delta
. That is,
\Tr(\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\delta
.

Proof.

From the necessary condition for local minima in Lemma
D.2
, any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of the
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with inverse temperature
\beta
, time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
satisfies

-\Tr(\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]{\bm{\rho}})\leq\epsilon\quad\text{for each}\quad a=1,\ldots,m.

(D.25)

Hence, from Eq. (
D.24
), we have

\epsilon\geq-\sum_{a}\alpha_{a}\Tr(\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]{\bm{\rho}})\geq\frac{2\epsilon}{\delta}(1-\Tr(\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}}))-\epsilon.

(D.26)

This immediately implies that
\Tr(\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\delta
.
∎

Appendix E
Complexity of finding a local minimum in quantum systems

In this appendix, we formally present the main results of this paper shown earlier in
Section

2
regarding the computational complexity of finding a local minimum in quantum systems.
We separate the results into two parts.
First, we look at the problem of finding a local minimum under local unitary perturbations (Def.
7
), showing that the problem is classically trivial to solve.
Next, we look at the problem of finding a local minimum under low-temperature thermal perturbations (Def.
6
). We will see that this problem is quantumly easy but classically hard to solve, establishing a promising candidate problem for quantum advantage.

E.1
Finding a local minimum under local unitary perturbations

We begin with the first main result stating the problem of a local minimum under local unitary perturbations is classically trivial.
The main issue is that there is a large barren plateau (which consists of many local minima with high energy) in the quantum optimization landscape.
Hence, a classical algorithm can efficiently estimate the properties of a single local minimum.

Theorem 5

(Classically easy to find a local minimum under local unitary perturbations; Restatement of Theorem
1
)
.

Consider a large problem size
n
.
There is a trivial classical algorithm that guarantees the following.
Given error
\epsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, an
n
-qubit local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
with
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathrm{poly}(n)
,
m
local Hermitian operators
\{\bm{h}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{m}
with
m=\mathrm{poly}(n)
and
\left\lVert\bm{h}_{a}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
, and a local observable
\bm{O}
with
\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
.

The classical algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}(1)
and outputs a real value
v\in[-1,1]
, such that
v
is
\epsilon
-close to
\bra{\psi}\bm{O}\ket{\psi}
for an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
\ket{\psi}
of the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under local unitary perturbations generated by
\{\bm{h}^{a}\}_{a}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
C.1
given in Appendix
C
characterizing local minima of
\bm{H}
under local unitary perturbations, with high probability, a state
\ket{\psi}
sampled uniformly at random from the space of pure states is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
\ket{\psi}
of the local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under local unitary perturbations, and
\bra{\psi}\bm{O}\ket{\psi}
is
\epsilon
-close to
\Tr(\bm{O})/2^{n}
.
Hence, there exists an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
\ket{\psi}
of
\bm{H}
under local unitary perturbations, and
\bra{\psi}\bm{O}\ket{\psi}
is
\epsilon
-close to
\Tr(\bm{O})/2^{n}
.

This characterization of local minima gives rise to the following trivial classical algorithm.
Given a local observable
\bm{O}
, represented by the subset
S
of qubits
\bm{O}
acts on with
|S|=\mathcal{O}(1)
and a
2^{|S|\times|S|}
Hermitian matrix
\bm{O}^{*}
.
A classical algorithm can compute
\Tr(\bm{O})/2^{n}
efficiently by computing the trace of
\bm{O}^{*}
and dividing by
2^{|S|}
. This trivial classical algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
∎

E.2
Finding a local minimum under thermal perturbations

We now turn to the second main result of this work, which shows that finding a local minimum under low-temperature thermal perturbations is easy with a quantum computer.
This is in contrast to the task of finding the ground state (global minimum), which is hard on quantum computers.
The formal statement is given below in Theorem
6
.

Theorem 6

(Quantumly easy to find a local minimum under thermal perturbations; Restatement of Theorem
2
)
.

Let
n
be the problem size.
There is a
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time quantum algorithm that guarantees the following.
Given error
\epsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, inverse temperature
0\leq\beta\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)
, time scale
\tau=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, an
n
-qubit local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
with
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathrm{poly}(n)
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{m}
with
m=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, and a local observable
\bm{O}
with
\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
.

The quantum algorithm outputs a real value
v\in[-1,1]
, such that
v
is
\epsilon
-close to
\Tr(\bm{O}{\bm{\rho}})
for an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with an inverse temperature
\beta
, a time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
.

Proof idea.

We consider a version of gradient descent, which we refer to as
Quantum thermal gradient descent
, that mimics how Nature cools the quantum system when the system is interacting locally and weakly with a low-temperature heat bath.
The algorithm starts with an arbitrary initial state
{\bm{\rho}}_{0}
.
For each step
t=0,1,2,\ldots
, the algorithm considers the current state
{\bm{\rho}}_{t}
and proposes the next state
{\bm{\rho}}_{t+1}
.
The tangent space
T_{{\bm{\rho}}_{t}}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
at
{\bm{\rho}}_{t}
is high dimensional with many possible directions/dynamics depending on the system-bath interaction.
The algorithm chooses a direction that lowers the energy as fast as possible by computing the gradient of the energy and proposes
{\bm{\rho}}_{t+1}
by performing gradient descent.
As long as the current state
{\bm{\rho}}_{t}
is not an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations, the energy will decrease by a sufficiently large amount

\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}_{t+1})<\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}_{t})-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}.

(E.1)

Because the energy is bounded from below, there are, at most, a polynomial number of steps
t\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)
until the algorithm arrives at an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations.
The detailed proof of Theorem
6
is given in Appendix
G
.
∎

Finally, we turn to the third main result establishing the difficulty of finding a local minimum under thermal perturbations using a classical computer.
To establish this result, we consider a class of geometrically local Hamiltonians
\{\bm{H}_{C}\}_{C}
on 2D lattices.
Each Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
corresponds to a 2D circuit
\bm{U}_{C}=\bm{U}_{T}\cdots\bm{U}_{2}\bm{U}_{1}
acting on
n
qubits with
T=2t_{0}+L=\mathrm{poly}(n)
gates as constructed in Fig. 1 of
[
43
]
with the additional padding to the construction in
[
43
]
such that the first and last
t_{0}=cL^{2}
gates being the identity gates for a constant
c=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
The construction in
[
43
]
has the property that each gate of the 2D circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
is geometrically adjacent to the subsequent gate.

Given the 2D circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
on
n
qubits with
T
gates.
The geometrically local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
acts on
n+T
qubits on a 2D lattice and has a highly-entangled unique ground state that encodes the quantum computation based on the 2D circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
,

\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\sqrt{\xi_{t}}\big(\bm{U}_{t}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}\ket{0^{n}}\big)\otimes\ket{0^{t}1^{T-t}},\qquad\text{where}\quad\xi_{t}:=\frac{1}{2^{T}}\binom{T}{t}.

(E.2)

We present the detailed construction of the 2D Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
in
Definition

14
in Appendix
J
.
We have the following proposition for estimating single-qubit observables on the ground state of
\bm{H}_{C}
.

Proposition E.1

(
\mathsf{BQP}
-hardness for estimating properties of the ground state of
\bm{H}_{C}
)
.

If there is a classical algorithm that can estimate any single-qubit observable on the unique ground state of the geometrically local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
in time polynomial in the number of qubits in
\bm{H}_{C}
to error
1/4
for any
\bm{H}_{C}
in the class, then
\mathsf{BPP}=\mathsf{BQP}
.

Proof.

Consider the single-qubit observable
\bm{Z}_{j}
and let
T_{j}
be the last time that qubit
j
is acted by a gate in the circuit
C
.
The ground state expectation of
\bm{Z}_{j}
is

\displaystyle\braket{\eta_{\bm{0}}|\bm{Z}_{j}|\eta_{\bm{0}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=T_{j}+1}^{T}\xi_{t}\braket{0^{n}|\bm{U}_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\bm{U}_{t}^{\dagger}\bm{Z}_{j}\bm{U}_{t}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}|0^{n}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T_{j}}\xi_{t}\braket{0^{n}|\bm{U}_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\bm{U}_{t}^{\dagger}\bm{Z}_{j}\bm{U}_{t}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}|0^{n}}

\displaystyle=\braket{0^{n}|\bm{U}_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\bm{U}_{T}^{\dagger}\bm{Z}_{j}\bm{U}_{T}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}|0^{n}}P_{t>T_{j}}+\epsilon_{j},

(E.3)

where

\displaystyle\quad P_{t>T_{j}}:=\sum_{t>T_{j}}\xi_{t},\quad\epsilon_{j}:=\sum_{t\leq T_{j}}\xi_{t}\braket{0^{n}|\bm{U}_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\bm{U}_{t}^{\dagger}\bm{Z}_{j}\bm{U}_{t}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}|0^{n}}.

(E.4)

We have used the fact that
U_{t}
for
t>T_{j}
acts like identity on the
j
-th qubit.
Note that

|\epsilon_{j}|\leq 1-P_{t>T_{j}}=:P_{t\leq T_{j}}.

(E.5)

We can make
\epsilon_{j}
arbitrarily small using a tail bound on the binomial distribution.
Given any circuit, one could always pad more identity gates to form an
L
-gate circuit, such that the last 3/4 of the
L
gates are identity.
Recall that
T=2t_{0}+L=2cL^{2}+L
. Then
T_{j}\leq cL^{2}+L/4
and we have
|\epsilon_{j}|\leq P_{t\leq T_{j}}\leq P_{t\leq cL^{2}+L/4}
.

Using Hoeffding’s inequality, we can bound the probability of sampling a time
t
, such that
t\leq cL^{2}+L/4
, according to the Binomial distribution
\{\xi_{t}\}_{t=0}^{T}
. This yields,

|\epsilon_{j}|\leq\exp\left[-2T\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{cL^{2}+L/4}{2cL^{2}+L}\right)^{2}\right]=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{L}{8+16cL}}.

(E.6)

By choosing a small constant
c\leq 1/(16\ln 18)-1/(2L)
, we have
|\epsilon_{j}|\leq 1/18
and
P_{t>T_{j}}\geq 17/18
.
Because of the bounds on the error
|\epsilon_{j}|
and the probability
P_{t>T_{j}}
, a classical algorithm satisfying the assumption of the proposition can determine whether

\bra{0^{n}}\bm{U}_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\bm{U}_{T}^{\dagger}Z_{j}\bm{U}_{T}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}\ket{0^{n}}>1/3\quad\mathrm{or}\quad\bra{0^{n}}\bm{U}_{1}^{\dagger}\cdots\bm{U}_{T}^{\dagger}Z_{j}\bm{U}_{T}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}\ket{0^{n}}<-1/3,

(E.7)

for any 2D circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
with
T=2t_{0}+L=2cL^{2}+L
gates, where the first
t_{0}
and and the last
(3/4)L+t_{0}
gates are identity.
Because one could think of the circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
as having
L/4
gates for any
L=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, this immediately implies that a polynomial-time classical algorithm can decide whether the expectation value of
Z_{j}
on the output state is
>1/3
or
<-1/3
for any polynomial-size 2D circuit where all consecutive gates are adjacent in the 2D geometry.

A 2D circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
such that any gate is adjacent to the subsequent gate can be constructed from any quantum circuit without the 2D constraint, such that a single-qubit observable
Z_{i}
on the output of the original quantum circuit corresponds to a single-qubit observable
Z_{j}
on the output of the 2D circuit.
As a result, any polynomial-time classical algorithm that can determine whether the expectation value of
Z_{j}
on
\bm{U}_{t}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}\ket{0^{n}}
is greater than
1/3
or smaller than
-1/3
can be used to simulate any polynomial-time quantum algorithm for solving decision problems in classical polynomial time. Hence,
\mathsf{BPP}=\mathsf{BQP}
.
∎

Using a series of mathematical techniques presented in Appendix
H
for characterizing whether all local minima are global minima in a many-body Hamiltonian, we prove that all local minima
\bm{H}_{C}
are close to the unique ground state
\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}
in Theorem
7
.
This theorem is the most involved technical contribution of this work.
Intuitively, one can think of the energy landscape of the 2D Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
over the space of
n
-qubit density matrices under low-temperature thermal perturbations to have a good bowl shape.
This is in stark contrast to the energy landscape under local unitary perturbations, where the landscape always contains an overwhelmingly large barren plateau causing the problem of finding local minima to be classically easy.
Furthermore, this theorem shows that a low-temperature cooling can always find a state close to the ground state irrespective of where we initialize the state in the exponentially large quantum state space.

Theorem 7

(All local minima are global in
\mathsf{BQP}
-hard Hamiltonians; Restatement of Theorem
3
)
.

Let
\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}_{C})=\lvert\eta_{\bm{0}}\rangle\!\langle\eta_{\bm{0}}\rvert
be the ground state of the 2D Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
acting on
n+T=\mathrm{poly}(n)
qubits.
There is a choice of
m=\poly(n)
two-qubit jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
satisfying the following.

Given
0<\delta<1
. For any small error
\epsilon=1/\poly(n,1/\delta)
, any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of
\bm{H}_{C}
under thermal perturbations with a large inverse temperature
\beta=\poly(n,1/\delta)
, a large time scale
\tau=\poly(n,1/\delta)
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
is an exact global minimum with high probability, i.e., we have
\Tr(\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}_{C}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\delta
.

The proof of Theorem
7
is given in Appendix
J
.
To show that the landscape has a good bowl shape, we utilize the negative gradient condition given in Appendix
D.3
.
However, the negative energy gradient operator is not easy to study.
To establish this strong claim, we give a series of techniques in Appendix
H
for characterizing negative energy gradient operator in few-qubit systems, in commuting Hamiltonians, and in perturbed Hamiltonians.
These technical tools can also be used to understand the energy landscape in other interacting many-body Hamiltonians.

While finding a local minimum under local unitary perturbations is classically easy,
the characterization of the energy landscape in these
\mathsf{BQP}
-hard Hamiltonians
\bm{H}_{C}
implies that finding a local minimum under thermal perturbations is
universal for quantum computation
and is hence classically hard if
\mathsf{BPP}\neq\mathsf{BQP}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{BPP}=\mathsf{BQP}
implies that all single-qubit measurements of all polynomial-size quantum circuits can be simulated in polynomial time on a classical computer.
Since one expects some quantum circuits to be hard to simulate on a classical computer, Theorem
8
implies that finding a local minimum under thermal perturbations is classically hard.

Theorem 8

(Classically hard to find a local minimum under thermal perturbations; Restatement of Theorem
4
)
.

Let
n
be the problem size.
Suppose there is a
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time classical algorithm guaranteeing the following.
Given error
\epsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, inverse temperature
0\leq\beta\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)
, time scale
0\leq\tau\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)
, an
n
-qubit local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
with
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathrm{poly}(n)
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{m}
with
m=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, and a single-qubit observable
\bm{O}
with
\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
.

The classical algorithm outputs a real value
v\in[-1,1]
, such that
v
is
\epsilon
-close to
\Tr(\bm{O}{\bm{\rho}})
for an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with an inverse temperature
\beta
, a time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
.
Then
\mathsf{BPP}=\mathsf{BQP}
.

Proof.

Assuming the existence of a polynomial-time classical algorithm that satisfies the properties stated in the theorem.
Apply this classical algorithm to the 2D Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
considered in Theorem
7
with a sufficiently small approximation error
\epsilon
, such that any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of
\bm{H}_{C}
under thermal perturbations with polynomially-large
\beta
,
\tau
and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
is an exact global minimum with high probability, i.e.,

\bra{\eta_{\bm{0}}}{\bm{\rho}}\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}=\Tr(\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}_{C}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\frac{1}{16^{2}},

(E.8)

where
\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}
is the unique ground state of
\bm{H}_{C}
.
We further consider
\epsilon
to be small enough such that

\epsilon<\frac{1}{8}.

(E.9)

Let
{\bm{\rho}}
be an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations.
Consider the observable
\bm{O}_{j}=\bm{Z}_{j}
from the proof of Proposition
E.1
.
Using the Fuchs–van de Graaf inequalities, we have

\left\lVert{\bm{\rho}}-\lvert\eta_{\bm{0}}\rangle\!\langle\eta_{\bm{0}}\rvert\right\rVert_{1}\leq\frac{1}{8}.

(E.10)

Because the classical algorithm can estimate
\Tr(\bm{O}_{j}{\bm{\rho}})
to error
\epsilon
, from Eq. (
E.9
) and (
E.10
), the classical algorithm can estimate
\braket{\eta_{\bm{0}}|\bm{O}_{j}|\eta_{\bm{0}}}
to error
1/4
in time polynomial in the number of qubits in
\bm{H}_{C}
.
From Prop.
E.1
, this implies that
\mathsf{BPP}=\mathsf{BQP}
.
∎

In the following, we use the previous theorem to show that quantum machines can improve over any efficient classical algorithm that variationally optimizes a classical ansatz that can efficiently predict local properties by performing low-temperature cooling.
Examples of the classical ansatz include tensor networks with efficient tensor contraction algorithms and neural-network quantum states with fast sampling algorithms.
This result provides a physically-relevant problem that yields an advantage in minimizing the energy of a geometrically-local Hamiltonian.

Corollary E.1

(Quantum advantage over variationally optimized classical ansatz)
.

Under the conjecture that
\mathsf{BPP}\neq\mathsf{BQP}
, there exists a class of
n
-qubit geometrically-local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
on a two-dimensional lattice with
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(n)
that satisfies the following.
Given any classical ansatz of
n
-qubit state
{\bm{\rho}}
that can estimate the expectation value of single-qubit observables to
1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
error in
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time on classical computers, any
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time classical algorithm for minimizing the energy
\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})
using the classical ansatz, and samples of the state
{\bm{\rho}}
represented by the optimized classical ansatz.
A quantum machine can find a state
{\bm{\rho}}^{\#}
with strictly lower energy than
{\bm{\rho}}
in
\mathrm{poly}(n)
time by running a quantum thermal gradient descent based on low-temperature cooling.

Proof.

The central claim is that the state
{\bm{\rho}}
found by an efficient classical algorithm cannot be an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum under low-temperature thermal perturbations.
We establish this claim by contradiction. Suppose that the classical ansatz for
{\bm{\rho}}
found by the efficient classical algorithm is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum.
Then the classical algorithm can use the classical ansatz to predict the expectation values of single-qubit observables of an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of
\bm{H}
to
\epsilon
error. From Theorem
8
, this implies that
\mathsf{BPP}=\mathsf{BQP}
, which is a contradiction.

Because
{\bm{\rho}}
is not an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum under low-temperature thermal perturbations, a quantum machine can use samples of
{\bm{\rho}}
to initialize at the state
{\bm{\rho}}
and perform one gradient descent step based on low-temperature cooling.
From Lemma
D.2
on the necessary condition for local minima, there exists
a\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
such that
\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}])<-\epsilon
.
From Lemma
G.1
on cooling by gradient descent, one can show that a single gradient descent step yields a state
{\bm{\rho}}^{\mathrm{(next)}}
with a strictly lower energy than the state
{\bm{\rho}}
.
Hence, one establishes the desired claim.
∎

Appendix F
Details of thermal Lindbladians

In the rest of the appendices, we give the full detailed proofs of Theorems
6
and
7
that are central to establishing the computational complexity of finding local minima under thermal perturbations in the previous appendix.
To that end, we need to provide the techincal details of thermal Lindbladians that generate such perturbations.

We have previously presented a high-level introduction to thermal Lindbladians in Appendix
A.3
. This has been sufficient for defining local minima and analyzing some basic properties, but not enough for proving Theorems
6
and
7
.
In this appendix, we present the exact form of thermal Lindbladians, their properties, and the algorithmic primitives for simulating quantum thermodynamics.

F.1
Exact form

The exact form of the thermal Lindbladian depends on a few physical concepts due to the microscopic derivation from a system-bath interaction
[
41
]
. For each jump
\bm{A}^{a}
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}({\bm{\rho}}):=-\mathrm{i}[\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a},{\bm{\rho}}]+\underset{:=\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}]}{\underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\Big[\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega){\bm{\rho}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega),{\bm{\rho}}\}\Big]\mathrm{d}\omega}},

(F.1)

where
\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}
is the purely dissipative part of the thermal Lindbladian. Implicitly, the operator
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)
also depends on the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
and the time scale
\tau.
We now unpack the physical concepts that form the building blocks of this expression.

Transition weight.

At a fixed inverse temperature
\beta
, the
transition weight

\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
tells us how strong the rate of a transition/jump should be, depending on the energy difference
\omega
.
In particular, the transition weight satisfies the following
Kubo-Martin-Schwinger (KMS) condition
and convenient normalization

\displaystyle\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)/\gamma_{\beta}(-\omega)=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega}\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\leq 1\quad\text{for any}\quad\beta\geq 0\quad\text{and any}\quad\omega\in\mathbb{R},

(F.2)

which is reminiscent of how detailed balance is enforced in classical Markov chains. We remark that any
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
obeying the above KMS condition and normalization also satisfies the following tail bound

\max_{\omega\geq\Delta}\omega\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\leq\max_{\omega\geq\Delta}\omega\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega}=\frac{1}{\beta}\max_{x\geq\beta\Delta}x\mathrm{e}^{-x}\leq\frac{1}{\beta}\max_{x\geq\beta\Delta}\mathrm{e}^{-x/2}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta/2}}{\beta}.

(F.3)

For concreteness, we usually adopt the common choice of
\gamma_{\beta}
corresponding to
Glauber dynamics
, with a cut-off frequency
\Lambda_{0}
to regulate the inverse Fourier transform:

\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2+\ln(1+\beta\Lambda_{0})}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}.

(F.4)

In the zero temperature regime (
\beta=\infty
), the function
(1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega})^{-1}
gives a step function (one for negative
\omega
and zero for positive
\omega
).
Based on the choice of bath phenomenology, there are plenty of options for the transition
weight, such as ohmic heating
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\omega_{0}}\frac{\omega\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega}}
.
However, for simplicity, we will stick to the Glauber dynamics.
Unless otherwise stated, we will also choose the cut-off frequency to be

\Lambda_{0}=1,

(F.5)

since each local jumps
\bm{A}^{a}
changes the energy
by at most
\mathcal{O}(1)
for our usage (and is generally true for local Hamiltonians with bounded degree interaction graph and bounded-norm terms).
We do not expect our main conclusion to change under other reasonable choices of
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
.

Operator Fourier transform.

Given a jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}
, we consider the
operator Fourier Transform

[
42
]
for the Heisenberg-evolved jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}(t)
characterized by a time scale
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
of the heat bath

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\int_{-\tau/2}^{\tau/2}\underset{=:\bm{A}^{a}(t)}{\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathrm{d}t.

(F.6)

The operator
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)
corresponds to matrix elements in
\bm{A}^{a}
that induce jumps between energy eigenstates with an energy difference approximately
\omega\pm\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\tau})
.
The bigger
\tau
is, the more precise
\omega
corresponds to the true energy difference; see Appendix
K
for further details. Physically,
\tau
is related to microscopic parameters of the bath (the bath correlation time and the weak-coupling strength
[
41
]
), but our discussion only requires the single time scale
\tau
that sets the Fourier transform energy uncertainty.

Lamb-shift.

The interaction with the heat bath induces an additional correction term in the coherent Hamiltonian dynamics of the
n
-qubit system, known as the
Lamb-shift
.
Given a jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}
, we have the following Lamb-shift Hamiltonian that depends on the bath correlation function
c_{\beta}(t)
and the time scale
\tau

\displaystyle\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a}:=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}\tau}\int_{-\tau/2}^{\tau/2}\int_{-\tau/2}^{\tau/2}\textrm{sgn}(t_{1}-t_{2})c_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1})\bm{A}^{a}(t_{2})\bm{A}^{a}(t_{1})\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(F.7)

While the Lamb-shift term is physically important, for our purposes, it is largely treated as a source of error; the energy gradient contribution comes from the dissipative part
\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}
.

Bath correlation function.

In the Lamb-shift term, the bath correlation function
c_{\beta}(t)
is the Fourier transform of the transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
,

c_{\beta}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\mathrm{e}^{+\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathrm{d}t.

(F.8)

The prefactor in Eq. (
F.4
) is chosen such that (see
Proposition

F.1
)

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{c_{\beta}(t)}\right|\mathrm{d}t\leq 1.

(F.9)

This normalization sets the strength of
\left\lVert\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a}\right\rVert
to be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Absolute zero
\beta=\infty
.

It is instructive to consider the case of zero temperature
\beta=\infty
and infinite time scale
\tau=\infty
.
In this case, the transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
is a step function (
1
for
\omega<0
and
0
for
\omega>0
) and
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)
measures the energy difference perfectly. Thus, all heating transitions (
\ket{E}\rightarrow\ket{E+\omega}
for
\omega>0
) are forbidden, and all cooling transitions (
\ket{E}\rightarrow\ket{E+\omega}
for
\omega<0
) will remain.
Hence, in the case when
\beta=\tau=\infty
, the thermal Lindbladian only lowers the energy. This matches our physical intuition that a zero-temperature bath only absorbs energy from the system.

Multiple jumps.

The thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
considers merely a single jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}
in the system-bath interaction.
When there are multiple jump operators, the total thermal Lindbladian is a weighted sum of the individual thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
,

\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}},

(F.10)

where
\alpha_{a}\geq 0
is a nonnegative weight.

Again, the interaction strength vector
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
weights the contribution of each thermal Lindbladian. Thus, the total equation of motion under multiple jumps reads

\displaystyle\frac{d{\bm{\rho}}}{dt}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}[\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}}]+\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}({\bm{\rho}})

(F.11)

\displaystyle={-\mathrm{i}\left[\bm{H}+\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a},{\bm{\rho}}\right]}+\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}({\bm{\rho}}),

(F.12)

which consists of a coherent part and a purely dissipative part.

Calculation for normalization of
c_{\beta}(t)
.

We now give a supplemental calculation that shows our choice of
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
in
Eq.

F.4
satisfies the condition in
Eq.

F.9
.

Proposition F.1
.

For

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\omega):=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}},

(F.13)

we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1}\leq{2+\ln(1+\beta\Lambda_{0})}.

(F.14)

Proof.

We want to bound the 1-norm of
f(t)
in the time domain. To do so, we bound the moments in the time domains

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\pi}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{f}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\omega=\Lambda_{0}\sqrt{2\pi}.

(F.15)

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\pi}\left\lVert tf(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\hat{f}\right\rVert_{1}=2\cdot\left\lVert\hat{f}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2.

(F.16)

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\pi}\left\lVert t^{2}f(t)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\omega^{2}}\hat{f}\right\rVert_{1}\leq 4\cdot\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\hat{f}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq T.

(F.17)

The second line uses the fact that
\hat{f}
is increasing and then decreasing (from
-\infty
to
\infty
). The third line evaluates the derivative

\displaystyle 4\left|{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle=4\left|{\frac{-\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\omega/\Lambda_{0}^{2}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\beta\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}{(1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega})^{2}}}\right|

(F.18)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{e}}\Lambda_{0}}+\beta\right)=:T.

(since
\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}/2}x\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{e}}}
)

Thus, we may partition into three integrals to optimize the bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\left|{t}\right|\leq\Lambda_{0}^{-1}}+\int_{T\geq\left|{t}\right|\geq\Lambda_{0}^{-1}}+\int_{\left|{t}\right|\geq T}\right)\left|{f(t)}\right|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\left|{t}\right|\leq\Lambda_{0}^{-1}}\Lambda_{0}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{T\geq\left|{t}\right|\geq\Lambda_{0}^{-1}}\frac{2}{\left|{t}\right|}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\left|{t}\right|\geq T}\frac{T}{t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2+\frac{4}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\ln(\Lambda_{0}T)+\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2+2\sqrt{2\pi}+4\ln(\frac{4}{\sqrt{\mathrm{e}}}+4\beta\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2+2\sqrt{2\pi}+8\ln(2)+4\ln(1+\beta\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\leq\sqrt{2\pi}(2+\ln(1+\beta\Lambda_{0})).

where in the last line, we used
1/\sqrt{\mathrm{e}}\leq 1
among other numerical bounds.
∎

F.2
Properties of thermal Lindbladians

From the exact forms of the thermal Lindbladians, we have the following propositions.

Proposition F.2

(Norm for the dissipative part
[
42
]
)
.

Any purely dissipative Lindbladian
\sum_{a}\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}
defined in Eq. (
F.1
) for any set of jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{m}
and any transition weight satisfying Eq. (
F.2
) have bounded superoperator norms

\displaystyle\left\|{\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}}\right\|_{\infty-\infty}=\left\|{\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}}\right\|_{1-1}\leq 2\left\lVert\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert.

(F.19)

The first equality is the duality between the
1-1
and
\infty-\infty
superoperator norms.

Proposition F.3

(Properties of the Lamb-shift term
[
42
]
)
.

The sum of Lamb-shift term (
F.7
) for any set of jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{m}
under a normalized bath correlation function
c_{\beta}(t)
given by Eq. (
F.9
) satisfies that
8
8

8

Implicitly, the Lamb-shift term has units of energy yet do not scale with
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert
.

\displaystyle\left\|{\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert

(F.20)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}[\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a},\bm{H}]\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}\left\lVert\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\right).

(F.21)

For large enough
\tau
, the Lamb-shift term almost commutes with the Hamiltonian.

From Prop.
F.2
and Prop.
F.3
, we have the following norm bound for thermal Lindbladians.

Proposition F.4

(Norm of thermal Lindbladians)
.

Given a Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
, an inverse temperature
\beta\geq 0
, a time scale
\tau\geq 0
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{m}
, a transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
satisfying Eq. (
F.2
), a normalized bath correlation function
c_{\beta}(t)
satisfying Eq. (
F.9
). The associated thermal Lindbladian
\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
has bounded superoperator norms

\displaystyle\left\|{\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}}\right\|_{\infty-\infty}=\left\|{\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}}\right\|_{1-1}\leq 3\left\lVert\sum_{a=1}^{m}\alpha_{a}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert,

(F.22)

which is controlled by the interaction strength vector
{\bm{\alpha}}
under the normalization of
\bm{A}^{a}
in Eq. (
A.4
).

F.3
Algorithmic primitives for simulating thermal Lindbladians

In this subsection, we review existing algorithmic primitives for simulating thermal Lindbladians
[
42
]
, estimating energy and expectation value of observables using block-encoding and quantum singular value transform (QSVT). See
[
72
]
for a tutorial on block encoding and QSVT.
We begin with a definition of a block-encoding for Hermitian matrices, i.e., observables.

Definition 12

(Block-encoding for Hermitian matrices)
.

We say that a unitary
\bm{U}
is a block-encoding for a Hermitian matrix
\bm{O}
if

\displaystyle(\bra{0^{d}}\otimes\bm{I})\cdot\bm{U}\cdot(\ket{0^{d}}\otimes\bm{I})=\bm{O}\quad\text{for}\quad d\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

(F.23)

Recall the following result stating that expectation values can be estimated using block-encoding.

Proposition F.5

(Measuring observable using block-encoding)
.

Given a block-encoding
\bm{U}_{\bm{O}}
for a Hermitian matrix
\bm{O}
and samples of a state
{\bm{\rho}}
.
One could estimate
\Tr(\bm{O}{\bm{\rho}})
to small error
0<\epsilon<0.5
using only
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{2})
queries to the unitary
\bm{U}_{\bm{O}}
.

Proof.

Consider
\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes{\bm{\rho}}
and apply the Hadamard test to sample
\Tr[\bm{U}\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes{\bm{\rho}}]=\Tr[\bm{O}{\bm{\rho}}]
9
9

9

We thank Yu Tong for discussions on this argument.
.
∎

Linear combinations of unitaries allow us to make efficient block-encodings of Hamiltonians presented as a sum of local terms. This fact results in the following proposition.

Proposition F.6

(Block-encoding for Hamiltonian; see
[
73
,
74
,
72
]
)
.

Any
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
has an efficient block-encoding
\bm{U}_{\bm{H}/\lambda_{1}}
for some scalar
\lambda_{1}
being the 1-norm of Pauli expansion coefficients.

From
[
42
]
, we have the following for the Lamb-shift term
\bm{H}_{LS}
from Eq. (
F.7
). Conveniently, the Lamb-shift term is already normalized (Proposition
F.3
).

Proposition F.7

(Block-encoding for Lamb-shift term; see
[
42
]
)
.

The Lamb-shift term
\bm{H}_{LS}
has an efficient block-encoding
\bm{U}_{LS}
.

We define the block-encoding for a Lindbladian without the coherent commutator term
-\mathrm{i}[\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}}]
.

Definition 13

(Block-encoding for Lindblad operators
[
42
]
)
.

Given a purely irreversible Lindbladian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[{\bm{\rho}}]:=\sum_{j\in J}\left(\bm{L}_{j}{\bm{\rho}}\bm{L}_{j}^{{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{L}_{j}^{{\dagger}}\bm{L}_{j}{\bm{\rho}}-\frac{1}{2}{\bm{\rho}}\bm{L}_{j}^{{\dagger}}\bm{L}_{j}\right),

(F.24)

we say that a unitary
\bm{U}
is a block-encoding for Lindblad operators
\{\bm{L}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
if
10
10

10

In the first register, we could use any orthonormal basis, sticking to computational basis elements
\ket{j}
is just for ease of presentation. Intuitively one can think about
b
as the number of ancilla qubits used for implementing the operators
\bm{L}_{j}
, while typically
a-b\approx\log|J|
.

\displaystyle(\bra{0^{b}}\otimes\bm{I})\cdot\bm{U}\cdot(\ket{0^{c}}\otimes\bm{I})=\sum_{j\in J}\ket{j}\otimes\bm{L}_{j}\quad\text{for}\quad b\leq c\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

(F.25)

Theorem 9

(Linear-time Lindbladian simulation
[
42
]
)
.

Suppose the jumps
\bm{A}^{a}
can be block-encoded by a unitary
\bm{V}_{jump}
using
c\in\mathbb{Z}
ancillas qubits. Then, we can simulate the map
\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}}
for (
F.12
) to
\epsilon\leq 1/2
precision in the diamond norm using

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}((c+1))\quad

\displaystyle\text{resettable ancilla},

(F.26)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}((t+1)\tau)\quad

\displaystyle\text{controlled Hamiltonian simulation time},

(F.27)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}((t+1)(c+1))\quad

\displaystyle\text{other two-qubit gates},

(F.28)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(t+1)\quad

queries to
\bm{W}
,
\bm{prep}_{c_{\beta}(t)}
,
\bm{Prep}^{\prime}_{c_{\beta}(\bar{t})}
, and
\bm{V}_{jump}

(F.29)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
absorbed poly-logarithmic dependences on
t,\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert,\epsilon,\tau,\beta
.
Furthermore, a block-encoding of the purely irreversible Lindbladian
\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
with discretized frequency labels can be implemented efficiently.

The above uses the following circuit components required for implementation: the controlled Hamiltonian simulation

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{t}\in S_{t_{0}}}\lvert\bar{t}\rangle\!\langle\bar{t}\rvert\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\bar{t}\bm{H}},

(F.30)

the unitary gates for preparing the bath correlation function in superposition

\displaystyle\bm{Prep}_{c_{\beta}(\bar{t})}:\ket{\bar{0}}\rightarrow\sum_{\bar{t}\in S_{t_{0}}}\sqrt{\left|{c_{\beta}(\bar{t})}\right|}\ket{\bar{t}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{Prep}^{\prime}_{c_{\beta}(\bar{t})}:\ket{\bar{0}}\rightarrow\sum_{\bar{t}\in S_{t_{0}}}\frac{c_{\beta}(\bar{t})}{\sqrt{\left|{c_{\beta}(\bar{t})}\right|}}\ket{\bar{t}},

(F.31)

and the controlled rotation for transition weights

\displaystyle\bm{W}:=\sum_{\bar{\omega}\in S_{\omega_{0}}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{\gamma(\bar{\omega})}&-\sqrt{1-\gamma(\bar{\omega})}\\
\sqrt{1-\gamma(\bar{\omega})}&\sqrt{\gamma(\bar{\omega})}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\lvert\bar{\omega}\rangle\!\langle\bar{\omega}\rvert.

(F.32)

Indeed, the above implementation uses discrete labels for the time
\bar{t}\in S_{t_{0}}
and frequencies
\bar{\omega}\in S_{\omega_{0}}
corresponding to
\mathrm{d}t
and
\mathrm{d}\omega
; these dominate the ancilla use. For conceptual simplicity, we focus on the continuous integral everywhere else and emphasize that the discretization is merely for implementation and introduces a negligible error; see
[
42
]
.

The controlled Hamiltonian simulation can be implemented efficiently for any
n
-qubit local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}

[
73
,
74
,
72
]
.
The other operations
\bm{W}
,
\bm{prep}_{c_{\beta}(t)}
,
\bm{Prep}^{\prime}_{c_{\beta}(\bar{t})}
can all be implemented efficiently
[
42
]
with the physically-motivated choice considered in Appendix
F.1
.

Proposition F.8

(Gradient of an observable under Lindbladian evolution; adapted from
[
42
]
)
.

Given block-encodings
\bm{U}
for a purely irreversible Lindbladian (Def.
13
) and
\bm{U}_{\bm{O}}
for a Hermitian observable
\bm{O}
, we get a block-encoding of

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J}\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\bm{O}\bm{L}_{j}\quad\text{via}\quad\bm{V}:=(\bm{Y}_{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\bm{U}^{\dagger}\otimes\bm{I}_{d})\cdot\left(2\lvert 0^{b+1}\rangle\!\langle 0^{b+1}\rvert\otimes\bm{I}-\bm{I}\right)\otimes\bm{U}_{\bm{O}}\cdot(\bm{Y}_{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\bm{U}\otimes\bm{I}_{d}),

(F.33)

where
\ket{\pm}:=(\ket{0}\pm\ket{1})/\sqrt{2}
and
\bm{Y}_{\frac{1}{2}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

We calculate

\displaystyle(\bra{0^{c+1}}\otimes\bm{I}\otimes\bra{0^{d}})\cdot\bm{V}\cdot(\ket{0^{c+1}}\otimes\bm{I}\otimes\ket{0^{d}})

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bra{-}\otimes(\bra{0^{c}}\otimes\bm{I})\bm{U}^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{0^{d}}\bigg)\cdot\left(2\lvert 0^{b+1}\rangle\!\langle 0^{b+1}\rvert\otimes\bm{I}-\bm{I}\right)\otimes\bm{U}_{\bm{O}}\cdot\bigg(\ket{+}\otimes\bm{U}(\ket{0^{c}}\otimes\bm{I})\otimes\ket{0^{d}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bra{-}\otimes(\bra{0^{c}}\otimes\bm{I})\bm{U}^{\dagger}\otimes\bra{0^{d}}\bigg)\cdot\left(2\lvert 0^{b+1}\rangle\!\langle 0^{b+1}\rvert\otimes\bm{I}\right)\otimes\bm{U}_{\bm{O}}\cdot\bigg(\ket{+}\otimes\bm{U}(\ket{0^{c}}\otimes\bm{I})\otimes\ket{0^{d}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=(\bra{0^{c}}\otimes\bm{I})\cdot\bm{U}^{\dagger}\cdot(\lvert 0^{b}\rangle\!\langle 0^{b}\rvert\otimes\bm{I})\otimes\bm{O}\cdot(\ket{0^{c}}\otimes\bm{I})

(F.34)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j\in J}\bra{j}\otimes\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\right)\bm{I}\otimes\bm{O}\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J}\ket{j^{\prime}}\otimes\bm{L}_{j^{\prime}}\right)=\sum_{j\in J}\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\bm{O}\bm{L}_{j}.

∎

Corollary F.1

(Block-encoding the gradient of the Hamiltonian)
.

Given a block-encoding for a purely irreversible Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
and a Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
, there is an efficient block-encoding for

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}],

(F.35)

which is a Hermitian operator corresponding to the gradient of
\bm{H}
under
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
F.8
for Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
, Hermitian observables
\bm{H}
and
\bm{I}
to obtain block-encodings for
\sum_{j\in J}\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\bm{H}\bm{L}_{j}
and
\sum_{j\in J}\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\bm{L}_{j}
. Then, use quantum singular value transform (QSVT) for products and sums of block-encoding to obtain the block-encoding for

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in J}\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\bm{H}\bm{L}_{j}-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{j\in J}\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\bm{L}_{j}\bm{H}-\frac{1}{4}\bm{H}\sum_{j\in J}\bm{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\bm{L}_{j}

(F.36)

at high precision.
∎

From all of the above propositions, corollaries, and theorems, we can obtain the following.

Lemma F.1

(Measuring energy gradient)
.

Given an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
, inverse temperature
\beta\geq 0,
time scale
\tau\geq 0
, samples of an
n
-qubit state
{\bm{\rho}}
, a thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}
from Eq. (
F.12
).
The energy gradient

\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}({\bm{\rho}}))=\Tr(\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}})

(F.37)

can be estimated to error
\epsilon
using time and samples of
{\bm{\rho}}
polynomial in
n,1/\epsilon,\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert,\beta,\tau
.

Proof.

From the form of thermal Lindbladians (
F.12
) and dropping the scripts
\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}=\mathcal{L},\bm{H}_{LS}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}=\bm{H}_{LS},\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}=\mathcal{D}
we have

\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}(\bm{H})=\mathrm{i}[\bm{H}_{LS},\bm{H}]+\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}}(\bm{H}).

(F.38)

Our goal is to create the block-encoding for
\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}(\bm{H})
.
First, we use quantum singular value transform (QSVT) for products and sums of block-encoding to obtain the block-encoding for
\mathrm{i}[\bm{H}_{LS},\bm{H}]
from block-encoding for
\bm{H}
and
\bm{H}_{LS}
in Propositions
F.6
and
F.7
.
Next, using the block-encoding for the purely irreversible Lindbladian
\mathcal{D}
from Theorem
9
and the block-encoding for
\bm{H}
, we can apply Corollary
F.1
to obtain efficient block-encoding for
\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}}(\bm{H})
.
To obtain the block-encoding for
\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}(\bm{H})
, we use QSVT for sums of block-encoding to add
\mathrm{i}[\bm{H}_{LS},\bm{H}]
and
\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}}(\bm{H})
.
Finally, using Prop.
F.5
, we can estimate
\Tr(\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}})
efficiently. All the above QSVT manipulations operate at high precision, and the discrete Fourier transform well-approximates the continuum at poly-logarithmic costs
[
42
]
.
∎

Appendix G
A polynomial-time quantum algorithm for finding a local minimum under thermal perturbations (Proof of Theorem
6
)

In this appendix, we present the proof of Theorem
6
by giving a polynomial-time quantum algorithm for finding local minima under thermal perturbations.
We refer to the efficient quantum algorithm as
Quantum thermal gradient descent
as the algorithm performs gradient descent using thermal Lindbladians induced by a heat bath.
The algorithm uses the properties of thermal Lindbladians presented in Appendix
F
.

G.1
Cooling by gradient descent

The central idea of quantum thermal gradient descent is the following.
When we are not at a local minimum under thermal perturbations, the negative energy gradient will be sufficiently large, and we can decrease the energy by following a direction with a negative energy gradient.
This is characterized by the following lemma.
We will use this lemma to design the gradient descent algorithm for finding a local minimum.

Lemma G.1

(Cooling by gradient descent)
.

Given parameters
0<\tilde{\epsilon}<0.5,B\geq 1
,
\beta,\tau\geq 0
, an
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
with
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq B
, and
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
.
Consider
a=1,\ldots,m
with an approximate energy gradient
g_{a}
satisfying

\left|g_{a}-\Tr\left(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}[{\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}]\right)\right|<0.01\tilde{\epsilon}

(G.1)

Suppose there exist
a^{*}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
with sufficiently negative approximate energy gradient,

g_{a^{*}}<-0.99\tilde{\epsilon}.

(G.2)

The state after evolving
{\bm{\rho}}
along the direction
\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a^{*}}
for a small step
s=|g_{a^{*}}|/(9B^{2})>0
,

{\bm{\rho}}^{(\mathrm{next})}:=\exp_{{\bm{\rho}}}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H},\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}\left(s\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a^{*}}\right)

(G.3)

guarantees the following energy decrease,

\Tr\left(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(\mathrm{next})}\right)<\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})-\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}}{20B^{2}}.

(G.4)

Proof.

From Prop.
D.2
on Taylor’s theorem, we have the following identity

\Tr\left(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(\mathrm{next})}\right)=\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})+s\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{*}}[{\bm{\rho}}])+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{*}}[\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{*}}[\bm{\sigma}]])

(G.5)

for some
n
-qubit state
\bm{\sigma}
.
We will separately control the linear term and the quadratic term.

Linear term.

From the definition of the energy gradient vector
{\bm{\nabla}}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}})
, we have

\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{*}}[{\bm{\rho}}])<g_{a^{*}}+0.01\tilde{\epsilon}<\frac{98}{99}g_{a^{*}}=-\frac{98}{99}|g_{a^{*}}|.

(G.6)

The second inequality follows from
g_{a^{*}}<-0.99\tilde{\epsilon}
, hence
0.01\tilde{\epsilon}<-(1/99)g_{a^{*}}
.

Quadratic term.

We can bound the quadratic term as follows,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Tr(\bm{H}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{*}}[\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{*}}[\bm{\sigma}]])\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{*}}}\right\|_{\infty-\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{a^{*}}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}(\bm{H})\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(G.7)

From Prop.
D.3
and Prop.
F.4
that bounds the norm of these objects, we have

\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}}\right\|_{\infty-\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{a^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}(\bm{H})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 4.5\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leq 4.5B^{2}.

(G.8)

Combining the linear and quadratic terms with
s=|g_{a^{*}}|/(9B^{2})>0
, we have

\Tr\left(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(\mathrm{next})}\right)\leq\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})-\left(\frac{98}{99\times 9}-\frac{1}{18}\right)\frac{|g_{a^{*}}|^{2}}{B^{2}}<\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}})-0.054\frac{|g_{a^{*}}|^{2}}{B^{2}}.

(G.9)

We can use
|g_{a^{*}}|^{2}>0.99^{2}\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}
to obtain the desired claim.
∎

G.2
Quantum thermal gradient descent

Given error
\epsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, norm bound
B=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, inverse temperature
0\leq\beta\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)
, time scale
\tau=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, an
n
-qubit local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
with
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq B
,
m
local jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
with
m=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, and a local observable
\bm{O}
with
\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
.

We consider a coordinate-wise gradient descent algorithm that implements the following.
The initial state
{\bm{\rho}}^{(0)}
is arbitrary as long as copies of the state can be prepared on the quantum computer. For example, we can set
{\bm{\rho}}^{(0)}
to be the maximally mixed state
\frac{\bm{I}}{2^{n}}
.
The total number of steps is

T:=\frac{42B^{3}}{\epsilon^{2}}.

(G.10)

For each time step
t
from
1
to
T
, the algorithm does the following.

1.

For each direction
a=1,\ldots,m
, estimate an approximate energy gradient
g_{a}^{(t)}
satisfying

\left|g_{a}^{(t)}-\Tr\left(\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}\right)\right|<0.0099\epsilon.

(G.11)

The energy gradient can be estimated efficiently using Lemma
F.1
given copies of
{\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}
prepared through Eq. (
G.12
) and Theorem
9
.
From the bound on energy gradients in Prop.
D.3
, we have
|g_{a}^{(t)}|\leq 3B+0.0099\epsilon
.
If
g_{a}^{(t)}<-0.99\epsilon
, set
a^{(t)}:=a
and
terminate
the for-loop over
a
.

2.

If
a^{(t)}
is not found, set
{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}:={\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}
and
terminate
the for-loop over
t
. Otherwise evolve
{\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}
under the direction
\hat{{\bm{e}}}_{a^{(t)}}
for a small step
s^{(t)}:=|g_{a}^{(t)}|/(9B^{2})
,

{\bm{\rho}}^{(t)}:=\exp\left(s^{(t)}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{(t)}}\right)\left({\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}\right)=\prod_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t}\exp\left(s^{(t)}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a^{(t)}}\right)\left({\bm{\rho}}^{(0)}\right).

(G.12)

Because
0\leq s^{(t)}\leq 1/(2B)
, a single copy of
{\bm{\rho}}^{(t)}
can be prepared in polynomial-time using the thermal Lindbladian simulation algorithm in
[
42
]
; see Theorem
9
.

We will show that the state
{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}
created by the gradient descent algorithm is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations.
Furthermore, using the thermal Lindbladian simulation algorithm, a quantum machine can efficiently create many copies of
{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}
.

G.3
Proof of Theorem
6

The central idea in the proof of Theorem
6
is the following lemma.
The lemma combines the key results characterizing local minima in Appendix
D
.

Lemma G.2

(Gradient descent finds a local minimum)
.

{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}
from Eq. (
G.12
) is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations with inverse temperature
\beta
, time scale
\tau
, and system-bath interactions generated by
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
.

Proof.

Suppose the algorithm terminates at some time step
t<T
, then
g^{(t)}_{a}\geq-0.99\epsilon
.
From Eq. (
G.11
), we have
\Tr\left(\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}\right)\geq-0.9999\epsilon
.
Hence,

\left\lVert{\bm{\nabla}}_{\beta,\tau,\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}}^{-}(\bm{H},{\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 0.9999\epsilon<\epsilon.

(G.13)

From the sufficient condition for local minima given in Lemma
D.1
, we have
{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}={\bm{\rho}}^{(t-1)}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{{\bm{\rho}}}
of the
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations.

We now show by contradiction that the algorithm must terminate early.
Assume that the algorithm did not terminate early.
Then, we can use Lemma
G.1
with
\tilde{\epsilon}=0.99\epsilon
for cooling by gradient descent to obtain

\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)})\leq\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(T-1)})-\frac{0.99^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{20B^{2}}\leq\ldots\leq\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(0)})-\frac{0.99^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{20B^{2}}T\leq\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}-\frac{0.99^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{20B^{2}}T.

(G.14)

From the definition of
T
in Eq. (
G.10
) and
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq B
, we have

\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)})\leq\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}-\frac{0.99^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{20B^{2}}\frac{42B^{3}}{\epsilon^{2}}\leq\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}-2.05B\leq-1.05B.

(G.15)

At the same time, because
\left\lVert{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}\right\rVert_{1}=1
,

\Tr(\bm{H}{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)})\geq-\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq-B.

(G.16)

This is a contradiction. Hence the algorithm must terminate early.
∎

The polynomial-time quantum algorithm for establishing Theorem
6
is as follows.
The algorithm runs quantum thermal gradient descent to find a local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}
of
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations.
Recall that
B
is the upper bound on
\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert_{\infty}
, and is equal to
\mathrm{poly}(n)
, and
1/\epsilon=\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
Because every step can be done in polynomial time, and there are at most
T=42B^{3}/\epsilon^{2}=\mathrm{poly}(n)
time steps, quantum thermal gradient descent runs in time polynomial in
n
.

Now, given any observable
\bm{O}
. The quantum algorithm prepares
\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon^{2})=\mathrm{poly}(n)
copies of
{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}
in
\mathrm{poly}(n)
time, then measures
\bm{O}
on the
\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon^{2})
copies of
{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)}
to estimate
\Tr(\bm{O}{\bm{\rho}}^{(T)})
to
\epsilon
error.
This concludes the proof of Theorem
6
.

Appendix H
Characterizing energy gradients in low-temperature heat bath

Recall from Appendix
D.3
on certifying Hamiltonians without suboptimal local minima, if there exists
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
with
\left\lVert{\bm{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{1}=1
, such that the negative gradient condition holds,

-\sum_{a}\alpha_{a}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]\succeq\frac{2\epsilon}{\delta}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}))-\epsilon\bm{I},

(H.1)

then any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of the
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
under thermal perturbations is an exact global minimum of
\bm{H}
with failure probability
\leq\delta
, i.e.,
\Tr(\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\delta
, where
\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H})
is the projection onto the ground state space.
To understand when the above condition holds, it is imperative to characterize the energy gradients,
\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]
.

In this appendix, we present various lemmas and theorems characterizing the energy gradients, which will be used in our proof of
Theorem

7
in
Appendix

J
for showing that a certain family of Hamiltonians has no suboptimal local minima.
We remark that the proofs of many formal statements in this appendix require concepts and results that won’t be shown till later in Appendices
K
and
L
, and we recommend the first-time reader to freely skip the proofs and return later.

For simplicity, we will focus on the nonnegative vector
{\bm{\alpha}}
being uniform over a subset
S
for the remaining appendices. We will show that this is sufficient for our purposes even though having the ability to choose
{\bm{\alpha}}
is more powerful. We define the following Lindbladian with uniform weights over a subset
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
,

\mathcal{L}:=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}.

(H.2)

Recall from Appendix
F
that each
\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
corresponds to a jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}
satisfying the normalization condition
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
. If we let
r:=2m\epsilon/\delta,\epsilon^{\prime}=m\epsilon
and
S=\{1,\ldots,m\}
, then the negative gradient condition becomes

\text{(negative gradient condition)}:\quad-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\succeq r(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{G})-\epsilon^{\prime}\bm{I},

(H.3)

This will be the central inequality we would like to establish for the remaining appendices.
Throughout the proofs, we will consider different subsets
S
and show a relation similar to Eq. (
H.3
) for subset
S
.

H.1
Basic properties of energy gradients in low-temperature bath
