We show a few basic properties of energy gradients under a low-temperature, long-time-scale bath. First, we show that the energy gradient, at large
\beta
(i.e., low temperatures), is negative semi-definite up to controllable error. Intuitively, this can be seen from the KMS condition in Eq. (
F.2
),
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)=\gamma_{\beta}(-\omega)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega}
: the heating transition is suppressed by the Boltzmann weight, allowing energy to increase by
\omega\sim\beta^{-1}
. Another source of error is the uncertainty in energy
\tau^{-1}
.

Lemma H.1

(Almost negative gradients)
.

Consider the thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
with jump operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a\in S}
where
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\leq 1
, and
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
satisfying Eq. (
F.2
). Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\preceq\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\right)\right)\cdot\bm{I}.

(H.4)

Proof.

Rewrite the energy gradient with an error controlled by
Proposition

F.3
and Lemma
L.1
gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{0}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega+\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

(H.5)

and bound the positive operator

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\int_{0}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left|{S}\right|\max_{\omega\geq 0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\leq\frac{|S|}{\beta}.

(H.6)

The second inequality uses the tail bound in Eq. (
F.3
) with
\Delta=0
.
∎

Second, we show that the energy gradient operator is nearly diagonal in the energy basis. The intuition is that for any operator
\bm{A}
, the product

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{A}}^{{\dagger}}(\omega)\hat{\bm{A}}(\omega)

(H.7)

is nearly diagonal in the energy basis for large
\tau
.

Lemma H.2

(Energy gradient is almost diagonal)
.

In the setting of Lemma
H.1
, assume that for any two well-isolated energy eigensubspaces
\bm{P}_{1}
and
\bm{P}_{2}
such that the two sets of eigenvalues have at least distance
\delta
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{P}_{1}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\bm{P}_{2}\right\rVert\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\theta_{\beta}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\sqrt{\delta\tau}}\right)\right).

(H.8)

where
\theta_{\beta}(\omega):=\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega
.

Proof.

Formally, approximate the energy gradient by dropping the Lamb-shift term (
Proposition

F.3
) and applying Lemma
L.1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\approx\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega.

(H.9)

We then apply the secular approximation for
\mu=\delta/2
(Corollary
K.3
) such that the transition amplitudes vanishes between the subspaces

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{1}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\mu}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\mu}(\omega)\bm{P}_{2}=0\quad\text{for each}\quad\omega\in\mathbb{R}\quad\text{and}\quad a\in A.

(H.10)

Combining the errors in each of the approximations leads to the claimed result.
∎

Next, we show that the finite-
\tau
Lindbladian can be approximated by the infinite-
\tau
version under certain conditions. The latter, known as the Davies’ generator
[
75
]
, has a simpler form that is more amenable for analysis in some situations.

Lemma H.3

(Recovering Davies’ generator)
.

Consider the dissipative part of the thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{D}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
with the jump operators
\bm{A}^{a}
where
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\leq 1
, and any
\gamma_{\beta}
such that
\left\lVert\gamma_{\beta}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
. Suppose the Bohr-frequency gap is
\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H})
, then

\left\lVert\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}-\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}}\right\rVert_{\infty-\infty}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\max_{\nu}\left|{\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\left|\hat{f}(\omega-\nu)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega}\right|+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H})\tau}}\right).

(H.11)

Therefore, the Bohr-frequency gap sets a timescale
\sim\Delta_{\nu}^{-1}
such that the map
\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
stabilized.

Proof.

It suffices to consider
\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{O}]
acting on arbitrary operator
\bm{O}
such that
\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert=1
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{O}]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\Big[\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{O}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega),\bm{O}\}\Big]\mathrm{d}\omega

(H.12)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{1}}}{{\approx}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\Big[\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{O}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega)-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega),\bm{O}\}\Big]\mathrm{d}\omega

(secular approximation: Corollary
K.2
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in B(\bm{H})}\Big(\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu^{\prime}}\bm{O}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\{\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu^{\prime}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu},\bm{O}\}\Big)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\hat{f}_{\mu}^{*}(\omega-\nu^{\prime})\hat{f}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)\mathrm{d}\omega

(truncated at frequency
\mu=\frac{\Delta_{\nu}}{2}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\Big(\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{O}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\{\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu},\bm{O}\}\Big)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\left|\hat{f}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

(different blocks
\nu\neq\nu^{\prime}
decohere)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{2}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\Big(\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{O}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\{\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu},\bm{O}\}\Big)\gamma_{\beta}(\nu).

(Lemma
K.1
)

The approximation errors are bounded by

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert\left\lVert\gamma_{\beta}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\left\lVert f_{\tau}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu\tau}}\right),

(H.13)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\max_{\nu}\left|{\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\left|\hat{f}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\max_{\nu}\left|{\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\left|\hat{f}(\omega-\nu)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega}\right|+\frac{\left\lVert\gamma_{\beta}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\mu\tau}\right).

(H.14)

Combine the error bounds to conclude the proof.
Note that since the bound becomes vacuous at
\mu\tau=\Omega(1)
, we have that
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\mu\tau}+1/(\mu\tau))=\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\mu\tau})
.
∎

Lemma H.4
.

For
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
defined in Eq. (
F.4
) with
\Lambda_{0}=\Theta(1)
, we have that

\displaystyle\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{R}}\left|{\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\left|\hat{f}(\omega-\nu)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega}\right|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1+\beta}{\tau}\ln\tau\right).

(H.15)

Proof.

Recall the integration-by-part trick for expectation integral

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)p(x)\mathrm{d}x=-[f(x)P(x)]^{x=\infty}_{x=0}+\int_{0}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(x)P(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{where}\quad P(x):=\int_{x}^{\infty}p(y)\mathrm{d}y.

(H.16)

Then,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\left|\hat{f}(\omega-\nu)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\nu+x)\left|\hat{f}(x)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma_{\beta}(\nu+x)+\gamma_{\beta}(\nu-x)}{2}\underset{=:p(x)}{\underbrace{2\left|\hat{f}(x)\right|^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\nu+x)+\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(x-\nu)}{2}P(x)\mathrm{d}x,

(H.17)

where in the last line we applied the integration-by-part and used
\gamma(\pm\infty)=0
and
P(0)=1
.
The error term can be bounded as follows

\displaystyle\left|{\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\nu+x)+\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(x-\nu)}{2}P(x)\mathrm{d}x}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\int_{0}^{1/\tau}\frac{\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\nu+x)+\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\nu-x)}{2}P(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{1/\tau}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\nu+x)+\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\nu-x)}{2}P(x)\mathrm{d}x}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1+\beta}{\tau})+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1+\beta}{\tau}\ln\tau).

(H.18)

In the last line, we control the first term by
P(x)\leq 1
and noting by the product rule we have

\displaystyle\left|{\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\omega)}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}\right)}\right|\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\omega/\Lambda_{0}^{2}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\beta\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}{(1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega})^{2}}}\right|\right)

(H.19)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{0}}+\beta).

(with change of variable
x=\beta\omega
and
y=\omega/\Lambda_{0}
)

The second term uses the tail bound
P(x)\leq\frac{4}{\pi x\tau}
from Eq. (
K.21
) and that
\gamma^{\prime}_{\beta}(\nu\pm x)

are each rapidly decaying outside an
x\in[\mp\nu-\Lambda_{0},\mp\nu+\Lambda_{0}]
window so that the integral over
\frac{1}{x}\mathrm{d}x
only contributes at most
\mathcal{O}(\int^{\Lambda_{0}}_{1/\tau}\frac{1}{x}\mathrm{d}x)=\mathcal{O}(\log(\tau\Lambda_{0}))
.
∎

H.2
Relating subspace and local gradients to global gradients

As a method of proof, we will often analyze a Lindbladian by its constituents, and here we present a few useful relations.
First, when studying gradients, the gradient acting on a subspace is often conceptually simpler. The following lemma relates the energy gradient in a subspace and the full energy gradient. This is a direct consequence of Lemma
H.1
and Lemma
H.2
above.

Lemma H.5

(Subspace gradient and global gradient)
.

In the setting of Lemma
H.2
, suppose
\bm{P}
projects onto a set of eigenstates of
\bm{H}
separated by the rest by a gap of at least
\delta
.
Then,

-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\succeq-\bm{P}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\bm{P}-\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\theta_{\beta}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\sqrt{\delta\tau}}\right)\right)\cdot\bm{I}.

(H.20)

Proof.

Let
\bm{L}=\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]
. We have,

\displaystyle\bm{L}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{P}+\bm{P}^{\perp}\bm{L}\bm{P}^{\perp}+\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{P}^{\perp}+\bm{P}^{\perp}\bm{L}\bm{P}.

(H.21)

Using
Lemma

H.1
establishing the almost negativity of the energy gradient,

\displaystyle-\bm{L}

\displaystyle\succeq-\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\right)\right)\bm{I},

(H.22)

we have

\displaystyle-\bm{P}^{\perp}\bm{L}\bm{P}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\succeq-\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\right)\right)\bm{P}^{\perp}.

(Lemma
H.1
)

\displaystyle\|\bm{P}\bm{L}\bm{P}^{\perp}+\bm{P}^{\perp}\bm{L}\bm{P}\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\theta_{\beta}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\sqrt{\delta\tau}}\right)\right).

(Lemma
H.2
)

Putting the bounds together yields the advertised result.
∎

Next, we provide a lemma that gives a simplified expression of the energy gradient operator when restricted to a subspace of low-energy eigenstates.

Lemma H.6

(Gradient in a subspace)
.

In the setting of Lemma
H.1
, suppose
\bm{H}
has a subspace of low-energy eigenstates with corresponding projector
\bm{Q}
that is separated from the higher energy eigenstates by an excitation gap
\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
.
Let
\Delta_{\nu}=\min_{\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}\in B(\bm{H}|_{\bm{Q}})}|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|
be the Bohr-frequency gap of
\bm{H}
restricted to the subspace. Assuming
\Delta_{\nu}/2<\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
, then the energy gradient operator in the subspace can be approximated using

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\bm{Q}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\bm{Q}-\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H}|_{\bm{Q}})}\bm{Q}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{Q}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\bm{Q}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega|\hat{f}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega\Bigg\|\leq\epsilon

(H.23)

where
\mu=\Delta_{\nu}/2
and

\displaystyle\epsilon\leq|S|\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{\|\omega\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}\right).

(H.24)

Proof.

We invoke a series of approximations to rewrite in terms of the exact Bohr frequencies on the subspace
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{1}}}{{\approx}}\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]

(Proposition
F.3
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{2}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{A}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{A}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

(Lemma
L.1
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{3}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{A}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{A}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

(Operator norms: Corollary
K.1
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{4}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{S}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{S}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

(secular approximation: Corollary
K.3
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu^{\prime},\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu^{\prime}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\hat{f_{\mu}}^{*}(\omega-\nu^{\prime})\hat{f_{\mu}}(\omega-\nu)\mathrm{d}\omega=:\bm{X}.

(H.25)

The errors are
E_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\left|{S}\right|\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}/\tau^{1/4})
,
E_{2}=\mathcal{O}(\left|{S}\right|/\tau)
,
E_{3}=\mathcal{O}(\left|{S}\right|/\beta)
, and
E_{4}=\mathcal{O}(\left|{S}\right|\times\|\omega\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\|_{\infty}/\sqrt{\mu\tau})
.
In particular,
{E_{3}}
arises from dropping the positive integral range, with error bounded by
\max_{\omega\geq 0}\omega\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\leq 1/\beta
.

Sandwiching Eq. (
H.25
) with
\bm{Q}
further simplifies the expression as it restricts to transitions in the subspace.
Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\bm{Q}\bm{X}\bm{Q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu^{\prime},\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{Q}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu^{\prime}}\bm{Q}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\bm{Q}\hat{f_{\mu}}^{*}(\omega-\nu^{\prime})\hat{f_{\mu}}(\omega-\nu)\mathrm{d}\omega

(no heating transitions)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H}|_{\bm{Q}})}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{Q}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{Q}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\bm{Q}\left|{\hat{f}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega.

(different Bohr-frequencies decohere)

The first line inserts an additional projector
\bm{Q}
between
\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu^{\prime}}
and
\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}
because any transition to excited states require
\nu,\nu^{\prime}>\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
, but this is forbidden by the restrictions that
\omega\leq 0
(from the integral) and that
|\nu-\omega|,|\nu^{\prime}-\omega|<\mu<\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
(from the secular approximation).
In the second line, since the Bohr frequencies in
B(\bm{H}|_{\bm{Q}})
are at least
\Delta_{\nu}=2\mu
apart, we must have that

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{*}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu^{\prime})\hat{f}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)=0\quad\text{for all}\quad\omega\in\mathbb{R},\quad\text{unless}\quad\nu^{\prime}=\nu.

(H.26)

Combining the above with Eq. (
H.25
) to conclude the proof.
∎

When the Hamiltonian is local, thinking about the gradient “locally” is sometimes useful. The following lemma gives a sufficient condition that guarantees a global gradient. Since the consequence is strong, the premise is also more stringent; it is only helpful when the Hamiltonian is frustration-free.

Lemma H.7

(Local-to-global gradient condition)
.

Suppose
\bm{H}=\sum_{i}\bm{h}_{i}
, where each term
\bm{h}_{i}\succeq 0
. Then for any (not necessarily thermal) Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
,

-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{h}_{i}]\succeq r_{i}\bm{h}_{i}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\succeq r\bm{H},

(H.27)

where
r=\min_{i}r_{i}
.

Proof.

By linearity, we have
-\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]=\sum_{i}-\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{h}_{i}]\succeq\sum_{i}r_{i}\bm{h}_{i}
.
Since
r_{i}\bm{h}_{i}\succeq r\bm{h}_{i}
, we have
-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\succeq r\sum_{i}\bm{h}_{i}=r\bm{H}
,
concluding the proof.
∎

H.3
Gradients for commuting Hamiltonians

When we are given a commuting Hamiltonian, the energy gradient induced by any local jump operator can be understood by restricting the system to its neighborhood.
In this situation, the negative gradient condition for the overall Hamiltonian can be decomposed into conditions that can be checked locally.
This gives an efficient method to show a commuting Hamiltonian has a negative gradient for all its excited states, which we elucidate in this section of the appendix.

Recall the thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
defined in Eq. (
F.1
) for a local jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}
, whose Heisenberg picture is

\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{O}]=\mathrm{i}[\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a},\bm{O}]+\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{O}],

(H.28)

where

\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{O}]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\Big[\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{O}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{\dagger}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega),\bm{O}\}\Big]\mathrm{d}\omega.

(H.29)

Note
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)
is the operator Fourier transform of
\bm{A}^{a}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}
, and
\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a}
is a Lamb-shift term defined in Eq. (
F.7
).

When
\bm{H}
is a commuting Hamiltonian (e.g.,
[
76
,
77
]
), an important observation is that
\bm{A}^{a}(t)
only depends on the part of
\bm{H}
that does not commute with
\bm{A}^{a}
. In particular, the energy gradient for each jump operator only depends on the neighborhood of
\bm{A}^{a}
.

Lemma H.8

(Commuting Hamiltonian and localized Lindblad operators)
.

Suppose
\bm{H}=\sum_{e}\bm{h}_{e}
is a commuting Hamiltonian. For any jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}
, the associated energy gradient simplifies to

\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]=\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\ni a}}[\bm{H}_{\ni a}]

(H.30)

where
\bm{H}_{\ni a}=\sum_{e:\,[\bm{h}_{e},\bm{A}^{a}]\neq 0}\bm{h}_{e}
is the part of
\bm{H}
does not commute with
\bm{A}^{a}
.

Proof.

When
\bm{H}
is commuting, we have
\bm{A}^{a}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}_{\ni a}t}\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}_{\ni a}t}
, so the Lindbladian superoperator only depends on
\bm{H}_{\ni a}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}=\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\ni a}}
.

Let
\bm{H}_{\not\ni a}=\bm{H}-\bm{H}_{\ni a}
be the part of
\bm{H}
that commutes with
\bm{A}^{a}
.
Since
[\bm{H}_{\not\ni a},\bm{H}_{\ni a}]=0
, we have
[\bm{A}^{a}(t),\bm{H}_{\not\ni a}]=0
for each
t
, which implies
[\bm{H}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{LS,a},\bm{H}_{\not\ni a}]=[\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega),\bm{H}_{\not\ni a}]=0
.
Thus we have
\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}_{\not\ni a}]=0
.
∎

H.4
Negative gradient condition under perturbations to Hamiltonians

We next look at how the negative energy gradient condition changes under perturbations to the
n
-qubit Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
.
See Appendix
L
for the proof of the following theorem.

Theorem 10

(Monotonicity of gradient under level splitting)
.

Consider a highly degenerate Hamiltonian
\bm{H}=\sum_{\bar{E}}\bar{E}\bm{P}_{\bar{E}}
with Bohr-frequency gap
\Delta_{\nu}:=\min_{\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}\in B(\bm{H})}|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|
of
\bm{H}
, and add a perturbation
\bm{H}^{\prime}:=\bm{H}+\bm{V}
. Let
\bm{P}=\bm{P}_{\bar{E}}
be a projector to an energy subspace and
\bm{P}^{\prime}
the corresponding perturbed subspace.
Suppose the perturbation is weaker than the Bohr-frequency gap,

\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{8}\Delta_{\nu}
.
For any
\beta,\tau>0
, let
\mathcal{L}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}}_{a}
be thermal Lindbladians with jumps
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a\in S}
, where
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\leq 1
and the transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
is given by Eq. (
F.4
).
Then we have the monotone property that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]\succeq r(\bm{I}-\bm{P})-\epsilon\bm{I}\quad\text{implies}\quad-\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\succeq r(\bm{I}-\bm{P}^{\prime})-\epsilon^{\prime}\bm{I}

(H.31)

where

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon+\left|{S}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\big(1+\frac{\Lambda_{0}+r}{\Delta_{\nu}}\big)\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\right).

(H.32)

Finally, we look at how the negative energy gradient condition changes when restricted to a subspace.
See Appendix
L.6
for proofs of the following two corollaries.

Corollary H.1

(Monotonicity of gradient on a subspace)
.

Consider a Hamiltonian
\bm{H}=\sum_{\bar{E}}\bar{E}\bm{P}_{\bar{E}}
and its perturbation
\bm{H}^{\prime}:=\bm{H}+\bm{V}
. Let
\bm{P}
be the ground space projector for
\bm{H}
and
\bm{P}^{\prime}
be the corresponding perturbed eigensubspace of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
.
Let
\bm{Q}
be a low-energy eigensubspace projector of
\bm{H}
(i.e.,
\bm{Q}=\sum_{E\leq E_{\bm{Q}}}\bm{P}_{E}
for
E_{\bm{Q}}\in\text{Spec}(\bm{H})
) with excitation gap
\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
.
Assume
\frac{\|\bm{V}\|\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}\leq\frac{1}{144}\Delta_{\nu}
where
\Delta_{\nu}:=\min_{\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}\in B(\bm{H}|_{\bm{Q}})}|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|
is the Bohr-frequency gap of
\bm{H}
within the subspace
\bm{Q}
.
For any
\beta,\tau>0
, let
\mathcal{L}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}}_{a}
be thermal Lindbladians with jumps
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a\in S}
, where
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\leq 1
and the transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
is given by Eq. (
F.4
).
Then we have the monotone property that

\displaystyle-\bm{Q}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\bm{Q}\succeq r\bm{Q}(\bm{I}-\bm{P})-\epsilon\bm{I}\quad\text{implies}\quad-\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}^{\prime{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\bm{Q}^{\prime}\succeq r\bm{Q}^{\prime}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}^{\prime})-\epsilon^{\prime}\bm{I}

(H.33)

where
\bm{Q}^{\prime}
projects onto the perturbed eigensubspace of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
identified with
\bm{Q}
, and

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon+\left|{S}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\bm{Q}}/4}}{\beta}

\displaystyle+\left(1+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\Delta_{\nu}}\right)\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+r\Big(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}}\Big)\bigg).

(H.34)

Corollary H.2

(Monotonicity of gradient on a subspace under off-block-diagonal perturbation)
.

In the setting of Corollary
H.1
, instead assume
\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}},\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}\leq(const.)
, and that the perturbation is off-block-diagonal, i.e.,
\bm{Q}\bm{V}\bm{Q}=(\bm{I}-\bm{Q})\bm{V}(\bm{I}-\bm{Q})=0
. Then,

\displaystyle-\bm{Q}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\bm{Q}\succeq r\bm{Q}(\bm{I}-\bm{P})-\epsilon\bm{I}\quad\text{implies}\quad-\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}^{\prime{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\bm{Q}^{\prime}\succeq r\bm{Q}^{\prime}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}^{\prime})-\epsilon^{\prime}\bm{I}

(H.35)

where

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\left|{S}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\bm{Q}}/4}}{\beta}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\bm{Q}}\right\rVert\cdot\Big(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\bm{Q}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\Delta_{\nu}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\Delta_{\nu}}\Big)+r\Big(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\Delta_{\nu}}\Big)\bigg).

(H.36)

Appendix I
Energy landscape of an Ising chain

In this appendix, we take a brief aside to characterize the energy landscape of the one-dimensional ferromagnetic Ising chain under thermal perturbations.
This provides a basic example on how the definition of local minima under thermal perturbations is related to the physical picture.
We will see that this system has many suboptimal local minima in the absence of an external field with a lifetime polynomial in the system size.
Once an external field is added, however, the system essentially has no suboptimal local minima and can quickly cool to the ground state where all spins are aligned.
This observation corresponds to the following physical phenomena: When there is no external magnetic field, a ferromagnetic system will often be stuck in a configuration with many domain walls, and an externally applied magnetic field can quickly magnetize the system.

The Hamiltonian for the ferromagnetic Ising chain on a periodic boundary condition is

\bm{H}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{Z}_{j}\bm{Z}_{j+1}-h\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{Z}_{j},

(I.1)

where we identify
\bm{Z}_{n+1}\equiv\bm{Z}_{1}
.
Intuitively, this system energetically favors configurations where adjacent spins are aligned.
When
h=0
, we have two degenerate ground states,
\ket{00\cdots 0}
and
\ket{11\cdots 1}
, which are the global minima.
This degeneracy is broken when
h\neq 0
, and these two states split by energy
2nh
.
The system also has many excited states with
domain walls
, i.e., locations where adjacent spins are anti-aligned such as
\ket{01}
and
\ket{10}
.
In what follows, we study the energy landscape of the above system under thermal perturbations with jump operator
\{\bm{A}^{j}=\bm{X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, setting
\tau=\infty
for simplicity.
We analyze three cases.

Case 1: no external field (
h=0
).

In this case, we will see that any bit string state with domain walls sufficiently far from each other, e.g.
\ket{\cdots 0001111000\cdots}
is a suboptimal local minimum.
Indeed, there is no local operation to strictly decrease the energy of such states; the jump operators
\{\bm{X}_{j}\}
can only displace the domain walls by one site, which does not change the energy.

We can see this more formally by computing the energy gradient operator.
Since
\bm{H}
is a commuting Hamiltonian, we may apply
Lemma

H.8
and study the gradient induced by a single jump operator
\bm{X}_{j}
by restricting the Hamiltonian to its neighborhood, i.e.,

\bm{H}_{\ni j}=-\bm{Z}_{j-1}\bm{Z}_{j}-\bm{Z}_{j}\bm{Z}_{j+1}.

(I.2)

Observe
\bm{H}_{\ni j}
has three degenerate eigenspaces
\bm{P}^{E}_{j}
with energy
E
as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{P}^{-2}_{j}=\sum_{{\bm{s}}\in\{000,111\}}\lvert{\bm{s}}\rangle\!\langle{\bm{s}}\rvert_{j-1,j,j+1},\qquad\qquad\bm{P}^{2}_{j}=\sum_{{\bm{s}}\in\{010,101\}}\lvert{\bm{s}}\rangle\!\langle{\bm{s}}\rvert_{j-1,j,j+1},

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\bm{P}^{0}_{j}=\sum_{{\bm{s}}\in\{001,100,011,110\}}\lvert{\bm{s}}\rangle\!\langle{\bm{s}}\rvert_{j-1,j,j+1}.

(I.3)

Then the negative Bohr-frequencies and the associated jumps are

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{j}_{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{j}^{-2}\bm{X}_{j}\bm{P}_{j}^{2}=(\lvert 000\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert+\lvert 111\rangle\!\langle 101\rvert)_{j-1,j,j+1},

\displaystyle\nu_{1}

\displaystyle=-4,

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{j}_{\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{j}^{0}\bm{X}_{j}\bm{P}_{j}^{2}+\bm{P}_{j}^{-2}\bm{X}_{j}\bm{P}_{j}^{0}=0,

\displaystyle\nu_{2}

\displaystyle=-2.

(I.4)

Hence, the energy gradient operator associated with jump
\bm{X}_{j}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{j}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{j}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}_{\ni j}}[\bm{H}_{\ni j}]={\textstyle\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H}_{\ni j})}\nu\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)\bm{A}_{\nu}^{j{\dagger}}\bm{A}_{\nu}^{j}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{0}\cdot(\lvert 010\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert+\lvert 101\rangle\!\langle 101\rvert)_{j-1,j,j+1}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-4\beta})

(I.5)

where
\theta_{0}=-4\gamma_{\beta}(-4)=-\Omega(1)
.
As we can see, the energy gradient is essentially 0 when the domain walls are more than distance 1 apart, and only becomes significant when two domain walls are next to each other, as in
\ket{\cdots 010\cdots}
or
\ket{\cdots 101\cdots}
.
This implies the presence of exponentially many suboptimal local minima; for example, choose whether or not to have a domain wall every 2 sites.

Despite the presence of many suboptimal local minima, we now argue that they have a lifetime polynomial in the system size
n
when the system evolves under thermal perturbations.
We may understand the dynamics of the system as a random walk of domain walls, and two domain walls annihilate each other when they meet.
Since two domain walls at distance
\ell
apart moving under diffusive dynamics take
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2})
time to meet, a suboptimal local minimum with
k
domain walls decays to a lower energy state after approximately
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}/k^{2})
time.

Case 2: weak external field (
0<h<2
).

In this case, the ground state of
\bm{H}
is uniquely
\ket{0^{n}}
, as all spins are slightly favored to be in the
\ket{0}
state instead of the
\ket{1}
state.
When the domain walls are far apart, e.g.
\ket{\cdots 0001111000\cdots}
, the applied external field causes an attraction across the domain of
1
’s, which energetically favors the domain walls to move closer together.
The presence of the field
h
removes all the suboptimal local minima that were in the previous case.
The state
\ket{1^{n}}
, which was a ground state in the previous case, becomes now the only suboptimal local minimum.

We now more formally characterize the energy landscape of
\bm{H}
using the energy gradient operator.
Again applying
Lemma

H.8
, we may consider the gradient induced by a single jump operator by focusing on its neighborhood.
The relevant neighborhood Hamiltonian is

\bm{H}_{\ni j}=-\bm{Z}_{j-1}\bm{Z}_{j}-\bm{Z}_{j}\bm{Z}_{j+1}-h\bm{Z}_{j}.

(I.6)

Turning the crank, we see that the negative Bohr-frequencies and the associated jumps are:

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{j}_{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\lvert 000\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert_{j-1,j,j+1},

\displaystyle\nu_{1}

\displaystyle=-4-2h,

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{j}_{\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\lvert 111\rangle\!\langle 101\rvert_{j-1,j,j+1},

\displaystyle\nu_{2}

\displaystyle=-4+2h,

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{j}_{\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle=(\lvert 001\rangle\!\langle 011\rvert+\lvert 100\rangle\!\langle 110\rvert)_{j-1,j,j+1},

\displaystyle\nu_{3}

\displaystyle=-2h.

(I.7)

Then the energy gradient operator associated with jump
\bm{X}_{j}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{j}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]={\textstyle\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H}_{\ni j})}\nu\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)\bm{A}_{\nu}^{j{\dagger}}\bm{A}_{\nu}^{j}}

\displaystyle\qquad=(\theta_{1}\lvert 010\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert+\theta_{2}\lvert 101\rangle\!\langle 101\rvert+\theta_{3}\lvert 011\rangle\!\langle 011\rvert+\theta_{3}\lvert 110\rangle\!\langle 110\rvert)_{j-1,j,j+1}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-2\beta h}),

(I.8)

where
\theta_{j}=\nu_{j}\gamma_{\beta}(\nu_{j})
.
As we can see, any configuration with a domain wall now has a significant gradient from at least one of the jumps.
The only configurations without a significant energy gradient are
\ket{0^{n}}
, the ground state, and
\ket{1^{n}}
, a metastable local minimum.

Case 3: strong external field (
h>2
).

In this case, the external field is sufficiently strong that the state
\ket{1^{n}}
is no longer a local minimum, and
\bm{H}
has no suboptimal local minima.
To see this, we note that
h>2
implies that
\nu_{2}>0
in Eq. (
I.7
), which means the energetically favored jump operator is actually
\bm{A}^{j}_{-\nu_{2}}=\bm{A}^{j{\dagger}}_{\nu_{2}}
.
This implies the energy gradient operator induced by the jump
\bm{X}_{j}
in this case is

\mathcal{D}_{j}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}}[\bm{H}]=(\theta_{1}\lvert 010\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert+\theta^{\prime}_{2}\lvert 111\rangle\!\langle 111\rvert+\theta_{3}\lvert 011\rangle\!\langle 011\rvert+\theta_{3}\lvert 110\rangle\!\langle 110\rvert)_{j-1,j,j+1}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-2\beta(h-2)}),

(I.9)

where
\theta^{\prime}_{2}=-\nu_{2}\gamma_{\beta}(-\nu_{2})
.
This gives the state
\ket{1^{n}}
a significant energy gradient, and thus the ground state
\ket{0^{n}}
is the only local minimum of
\bm{H}
.

Appendix J
All local minima are global in
BQP
-hard Hamiltonians (Proof of Theorem
7
)

A main result of our work is that the task of finding a local minimum for
\bm{H}_{C}
under thermal perturbation is universal for quantum computation and hence classically hard.
As we have seen in the main text and Appendix
E.2
, this main result follows from Theorem
7
, which we prove in this appendix.

We start by defining
\bm{H}_{C}
in detail.
Given a 2D
n
-qubit circuit
\bm{U}_{C}=\bm{U}_{T}\cdots\bm{U}_{2}\bm{U}_{1}
with
T=2t_{0}+L=\mathrm{poly}(n)
gates as constructed in Fig. 1 of Ref.
[
43
]
, where the first and last
t_{0}
gates are identity gates and each gate of the 2D circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
is geometrically adjacent to the subsequent gate. We consider a geometrically local Hamiltonian on a 2D lattice with
n+T
qubits defined as follows.

Definition 14

(Modified circuit-to-Hamiltonian construction)
.

Consider a 2D circuit

\bm{U}_{C}=\bm{U}_{T}\cdots\bm{U}_{2}\bm{U}_{1}

on
n
qubits with
T=2t_{0}+L
gates, where the first and last
t_{0}=cL^{2}
gates are identity gates with
c=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and each consecutive gates are geometrically adjacent.
We define a geometrically-local Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
on a 2D lattice with
n+T
qubits as follows,

\bm{H}_{C}:=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\quad\text{acting on}\quad(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes T},

(J.1)

where each individual term is given by

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}

\displaystyle:=J_{\textnormal{clock}}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}f_{t}\bm{I}\otimes\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{t,t+1},

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}

\displaystyle:=J_{\textnormal{in}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{j}\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert_{j}\otimes\lvert 10\rangle\!\langle 10\rvert_{t_{j}-1,t_{j}},

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}J_{\textnormal{prop}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}(t),

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}(1)

\displaystyle:=\bm{I}-h_{1}(\bm{U}_{1}\otimes\lvert 10\rangle\!\langle 00\rvert_{1,2}+\bm{U}_{1}^{\dagger}\otimes\lvert 00\rangle\!\langle 10\rvert_{1,2}),

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\bm{I}-h_{t}(\bm{U}_{t}\otimes\lvert 110\rangle\!\langle 100\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1}+\bm{U}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\lvert 100\rangle\!\langle 110\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1})\qquad\text{for each}\quad 1<t<T,

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}(T)

\displaystyle:=\bm{I}-h_{T}(\bm{U}_{T}\otimes\lvert 11\rangle\!\langle 10\rvert_{T-1,T}+\bm{U}_{T}^{\dagger}\otimes\lvert 10\rangle\!\langle 11\rvert_{T-1,T}).

The
T
qubits correspond to the
T
geometrically-local gates and are placed next to each gate to ensure
\bm{H}_{C}
is geometrically local.
The couplings are chosen as

J_{\textnormal{clock}}=1,\quad f_{t}=(T-t)/T,\quad g_{j}=1/\xi_{t_{j}-1},\quad h_{t}=\sqrt{t(T-t+1)}.

(J.2)

We will set the other parameters
J_{\textnormal{in}},J_{\textnormal{prop}}
later. The time
t_{j}
is the first time qubit
j
is acted on.

We will show later in Appendix
J.1
that
\bm{H}_{C}
has a unique ground state given by

\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\sqrt{\xi_{t}}\big(\bm{U}_{t}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}\ket{0^{n}}\big)\otimes\ket{0^{t}1^{T-t}}\qquad\text{where}\quad\xi_{t}:=\frac{1}{2^{T}}\binom{T}{t}.

(J.3)

Note this state encodes the computational history of the circuit
\bm{U}_{C}
.
By choosing
t_{0}=L^{2}
, we ensure that each time in the interesting part of the computational history (i.e., the intermediate
L
gates) can be observed with
\Omega(1/T)
probability as we will show later in
Proposition

J.1
.
This also implies
g_{j}=\mathcal{O}(T)
.

We now state a detailed version of Theorem
7
based on the definition of
\bm{H}_{C}
in the following.

Theorem 11

(All local minima are global in
\bm{H}_{C}
)
.

Let
\bm{P}_{G}
be the ground-space projector for the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{C}
in Eq. (
J.1
).
For any failure probability
0<\delta<1
,
there is a parameter choice
J_{\textnormal{in}},J_{\textnormal{prop}}=\poly(n,T,\delta^{-1})
and a choice of
m
two-qubit jump operators

S_{0}=\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{m}:=\{\bm{I}\otimes\bm{X}_{t},\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}\cup\{\bm{X}_{j}\otimes\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert_{t_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}

(J.4)

with
m=2T+n
satisfying the following:

For a sufficiently small
\epsilon=1/\poly(n,T,\delta^{-1})
, any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of
\bm{H}_{C}
under thermal perturbations with sufficiently large
\beta=\poly(n,T,\delta^{-1})
,
\tau=\poly(n,T,\delta^{-1})
, and system-bath interactions generated by
S_{0}
is an exact global minimum with probability
\Tr(\bm{P}_{G}(\bm{H}_{C}){\bm{\rho}})\geq 1-\delta
.

We remind the reader that the thermal Lindbladians that generate the perturbations are defined in Eq. (
F.1
). The transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
is chosen to be Glauber dynamics as defined in Eq. (
F.4
), with energy cut-off
\Lambda_{0}=1
as a convenient choice so that
\|\omega\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. We do not expect our result to change with other reasonable choices of
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
.

Remark 2
.

Our
\bm{H}_{C}
is similar to previous circuit-Hamiltonian constructions (see e.g.,
[
43
,
44
,
20
]
), but there are some significant differences.
One key change is that
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}
is no longer frustration-free, and its couplings
h_{t}
are not uniform;
consequently, this revised
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}
has better spectral properties that enable us to lower bound its Bohr-frequency gap.
Furthermore,
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}
is given non-uniform couplings
f_{t}
so that any local excitation has an incentive to move rightwards (e.g.
\ket{0011}\to\ket{0001}
), ensuring
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}
has no local minima except its ground states.
These modifications allow us to prove that all excited states of
\bm{H}_{C}
have significant negative gradients, so that they will all flow to the ground state under thermal perturbations.

J.1
Characterizing low energy states of
\bm{H}_{C}

We will start by characterizing the low energy states of the circuit Hamiltonian.
We define the following sequence of Hamiltonians

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}

\displaystyle=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}

\displaystyle=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}

\displaystyle=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}=\bm{H}_{C}

with the ground space projectors
\bm{P}_{j}
such that

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\supset\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\supset\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}.

(J.5)

Equivalently, we have

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
and
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}
.
Our approach to calculating the gradient for
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{H}_{C}
is
perturbative
: we start with the simple Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
and gradually add perturbations (which will split the spectrum, Figure
3
). Remarkably, the gradient is
stable
as long as the perturbation is weak enough. That is, it suffices to analyze the gradient of the simpler, unperturbed Hamiltonians on suitable subspaces.

Now we describe explicitly the ground subspaces
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
,
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
and
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}
. Let

\ket{C_{t}}=\ket{1^{t}0^{T-t}}\quad\text{for each}\quad t=0,1,\ldots,T

(J.6)

and

\ket{\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}}=\big(\bm{U}_{t}\cdots\bm{U}_{1}\ket{\bm{x}}\big)\otimes\ket{C_{t}}\quad\text{for each}\quad\bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq t\leq T.

(J.7)

The set of
\ket{\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}}
forms an orthonormal basis for the ground space of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}
, with energy 0 and a spectral gap of
J_{\textnormal{clock}}/T
.
The ground space projector is

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}=\sum_{{\bm{x}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\lvert\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}\rangle\!\langle\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}\rvert\quad\text{and}\quad\braket{\eta_{{\bm{y}},t^{\prime}}|\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}}=\delta_{{\bm{y}}{\bm{x}}}\delta_{tt^{\prime}}.

(J.8)

Observe that
[\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}},\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}]=0
, so the ground states of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}
are given as the ground states of

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}=\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}[\bm{I}-h_{t}(\bm{U}_{t}\otimes\lvert C_{t}\rangle\!\langle C_{t-1}\rvert+\bm{U}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\lvert C_{t-1}\rangle\!\langle C_{t}\rvert)].

(J.9)

Furthermore, observe the orthogonality relations

\braket{\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}|\eta_{{\bm{y}},t^{\prime}}}=0\qquad\text{ when}\quad{\bm{x}}\neq{\bm{y}}\quad\text{for each}\quad t,t^{\prime}.

(J.10)

That is,
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
is block diagonal with blocks labeled by
{\bm{x}}
.
Moreover, for any
\bm{x}
, in the basis of
\ket{\eta_{\bm{x},0}},\ket{\eta_{\bm{x},1}},\ldots,\ket{\eta_{\bm{x},T}}
, we can explicitly write down the effective
(T+1)\times(T+1)
Hamiltonian

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}=\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T&-h_{1}&&&\\
-h_{1}&T&-h_{2}&&\\
&-h_{2}&T&-h_{3}&\\
&&-h_{3}&\ddots&\ddots\\
&&&\ddots&T&-h_{T}\\
&&&&-h_{T}&T\end{pmatrix}=J_{\textnormal{prop}}\Big(\frac{T}{2}\bm{I}-\bm{L}_{x}\Big),

(J.11)

where
\bm{L}_{x}
is the matrix representation of the spin-
T/2
angular momentum operator whose spectrum is well known. In particular, the unique ground state is

\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}:=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\sqrt{\xi_{t}}\ket{\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}}\quad\text{with energy}\quad 0\quad\text{and spectral gap}\quad J_{\textnormal{prop}}.

(J.12)

We will call
\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}
the
history state
with respect to input
\ket{{\bm{x}}}
.
The ground space projector of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
is then given as

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}=\sum_{{\bm{x}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\lvert\eta_{{\bm{x}}}\rangle\!\langle\eta_{{\bm{x}}}\rvert.

(J.13)

Finally, note
\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}
for
\bm{0}=(0,0,\cdots,0)
is the unique ground state of
\bm{H}_{C}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
and so

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}=\lvert\eta_{\bm{0}}\rangle\!\langle\eta_{\bm{0}}\rvert.

(J.14)

This is because
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}
is positive semi-definite, and
\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}
is the only state in
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
with zero eigenvalue with respect to
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}
.

Figure 3:
The degenerate levels of
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}
split under perturbations
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}
and
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}
. In particular, the ground state splitting is tracked in blue shades. The careful choice of energy scales ensures that the levels can be identified with the original degenerate blocks.

J.2
Proof of Theorem
11

To prove Theorem
11
, we show that all excited states in
\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}
have significant gradient relative to
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
.
Our analysis for the gradient will be carried out in three subspaces

\displaystyle\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}=\underset{\text{studying }\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}}{\underbrace{(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}})}}+\underset{\text{studying }\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}}{\underbrace{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})}}+\underset{\text{studying }\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}}{\underbrace{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})}}

(J.15)

Let
\mathcal{L}_{j}:=\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{j}}
be the thermal Lindbladian with uniform weights as in Eq. (
H.2
), defined with respect to
\bm{H}_{j}
and the jump operators in Eq. (
J.4
) .

Case 1: Gradients for
\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
from
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
.

We first show excited states of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
have good energy gradient:

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{I}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]\succeq r_{1}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}})-\epsilon_{1a}\bm{I}.

(J.16)

Because
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}
is a commuting Hamiltonian, the global gradient can be lower bounded by checking the local gradient from individual local jumps. We carry out this computation in
Section

J.3.1
, where we show

r_{1}=\Omega(1/T\ln\beta)
and
\epsilon_{1a}=\mathcal{O}(T^{7/4}/\tau^{1/4}+T/\beta+T(1+\beta)\ln\tau/\tau)
in
Lemma

J.2
.

We then apply
Theorem

10
(with
\bm{H}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
and
\bm{H}^{\prime}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
) to show excited states of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
have large gradient with respect to
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
.
In other words,

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\succeq r_{1}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}})-\epsilon_{1}\bm{I}.

(J.17)

To do this, we only need to check that the conditions of
Theorem

10
are satisfied.
Note
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
also projects onto eigenstates of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
since
[\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}},\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]=0.

Note
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
has a discrete spectrum with a minimum Bohr-frequency gap of at least
\Delta_{\nu}\geq 1/T
.
We can choose sufficiently small
J_{\textnormal{prop}},J_{\textnormal{in}}
such that
\|\bm{V}_{\mathtt{I}}\|:=\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\|\leq J_{\textnormal{prop}}T^{2}+J_{\textnormal{in}}ng_{\max}\ll\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}})=1/T
, where
g_{max}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq n}g_{j}=\mathcal{O}(T)
. And plugging in
\Lambda_{0}=1
and other parameters into the error bound (
H.32
)

\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{1a}+|S_{0}|\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\Big(1+\frac{1+r_{1}}{\Delta_{\nu}}\Big)\left\lVert\bm{V}_{\mathtt{I}}\right\rVert\right).

(J.18)

Noting that
|S_{0}|,\|\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}\|,g_{\max}=\mathcal{O}(T)
, we can make
\epsilon_{1}/r_{1}\leq\delta/6
by choosing appropriate powers

\tau\geq\tilde{\Omega}(T^{11}/\delta^{4}),\quad\beta\geq\tilde{\Omega}(T^{2}/\delta),\quad J_{\textnormal{prop}}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\delta/T^{5}),\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\textnormal{in}}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\delta/nT^{4}).

(J.19)

Case 2: Gradients for
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})
from
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
.

We next restrict our attention to the action of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}]
inside the
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
subspace, which conveniently is also an eigensubspace of both
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
and
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
since
[\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}},\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}]=[\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}},\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]=0
.
Explicit computation in
Section

J.3.2
shows that

-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\succeq r_{2}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})-\epsilon_{2a}\bm{I},

(J.20)

with the bounds from
Lemma

J.4
promising

r_{2}=\Omega(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{T\ln\beta})\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon_{2a}=|S_{0}|\cdot\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau J_{\textnormal{prop}}}}\Big).

(J.21)

We then invoke Corollary
H.1
with
\bm{Q}=\bm{Q}^{\prime}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
,
\bm{H}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
and
\bm{H}^{\prime}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
to show monotonicity of energy gradient on a subspace under perturbation

-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\succeq r_{2}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime})-\epsilon_{2b}\bm{I},

(J.22)

where
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}
is the perturbed eigensubspace of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
that is identified with
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
. To justify the application of
Corollary

H.1
, we note
in
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
, the eigensubspace
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
has an excitation gap of
\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\geq 1/T-2\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\|=\Omega(1/T)
, where extra
2\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\|
term is due to shifts in eigenvalues of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
bounded by Weyl’s inequality (see
Proposition

L.3
).
The perturbation on
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
has strength
\|\bm{V}_{\mathtt{II}}\|:=\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\|\leq J_{\textnormal{in}}ng_{\max}
, and
\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}|_{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}})=J_{\textnormal{prop}}
.
Then noting
\Delta_{\nu}\ll\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
, we keep the dominant terms in the error bound (
H.34
) and get

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2b}\leq\epsilon_{2a}+|S_{0}|\cdot\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\|\bm{V}_{\mathtt{II}}\|\|\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}\|}{\Delta_{\nu}\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+r_{2}\frac{\|\bm{V}_{\mathtt{II}}\|}{\Delta_{\nu}}\Big).

(J.23)

Furthermore, the eigensubspace
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
in
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
is separated by a spectral gap of
1/T-2\|\bm{V}_{\mathtt{I}}\|=\Omega(1/T)
from the other eigenstates, we may apply
Lemma

H.5
to show

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\succeq r_{2}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime})-\epsilon_{2}\bm{I}\quad\text{where}\quad\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon_{2b}+|S_{0}|\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\sqrt{\frac{T}{\tau}}).

(J.24)

Since
|S_{0}|,\penalty\ g_{\max},\penalty\ \|\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}\|,\penalty\ \|\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}\|=\mathcal{O}(T)
, we can make
\epsilon_{2}/r_{2}\leq\delta/6
by choosing

\tau\geq\tilde{\Omega}\Big(\frac{T^{11}}{J_{\textnormal{prop}}^{2}\delta^{4}}\Big),\quad\beta\geq\tilde{\Omega}\Big(\frac{T^{2}}{J_{\textnormal{prop}}\delta}\Big),\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\textnormal{in}}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}^{2}\delta}{nT^{5}}\Big).

(J.25)

Case 3: Gradients for
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})
from
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
.

Now, we restrict our attention to
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}
, the perturbed eigensubspace in
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
that correspond to
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
.
We can show by explicit computation (deferred to
Section

J.3.3
) that

-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}\succeq r_{3}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})-\epsilon_{3a}\bm{I}.

(J.26)

This computation shows that all valid history states
\ket{\eta_{{\bm{x}}}}
except for
{\bm{x}}=\bm{0}
have nonzero gradient with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}
.
The derivation uses a more fine-grained version of subspace gradient monotonicity (
Corollary

H.2
) since the standard version yields insufficient bounds. Roughly, we need to capture the fact that off-diagonal perturbations induce only
second-order
perturbation on the eigenvalues. The final calculated bounds in Eqs. (
J.92
) and (
J.95
) give us

r_{3}=\Omega\Big(\frac{J_{\textnormal{in}}}{T^{2}\ln\beta}\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon_{3a}\leq T\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{T^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{J_{\textnormal{in}}\tau}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta J_{\textnormal{in}}}+n\frac{(nTJ_{\textnormal{in}})^{2}}{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}\bigg).

(J.27)

Using the fact that
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}
is separated by energy of at least

J_{\textnormal{prop}}-2\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\|
from the other eigenstates in
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
, we can apply
Lemma

H.5
to get

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\succeq r_{3}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})-\epsilon_{3}\bm{I}\quad\text{where}\quad\epsilon_{3}=\epsilon_{3a}+\left|{S_{0}}\right|\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{J_{\textnormal{prop}}\tau}}\Big).

(J.28)

We may ensure
\epsilon_{3}/r_{3}\leq\delta/6
by choosing

\tau\geq\tilde{\Omega}\Big(\frac{T^{15}}{J_{\textnormal{in}}^{4}\delta^{4}}\Big),\quad\beta\geq\tilde{\Omega}\Big(\frac{T^{3}}{J_{\textnormal{in}}\delta}\Big),\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\textnormal{in}}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}\delta}{n^{3}T^{5}}\Big).

(J.29)

Altogether.

Based on the conditions in Eqs. (
J.19
) (
J.25
) (
J.29
) and the fact that
T=\Omega(n)
, a consistent choice of parameters that satisfies all the bounds and ensures
\epsilon_{j}/r_{j}\leq\delta/6
are

\tau=\tilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{T^{79}}{\delta^{16}}\Big),\quad\beta=\tilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{T^{19}}{\delta^{4}}\Big),\quad J_{\textnormal{prop}}=\tilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{\delta}{T^{5}}\Big),\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\textnormal{in}}=\tilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{\delta^{3}}{T^{16}}\Big).

(J.30)

Then combining Eqs. (
J.17
), (
J.24
), and (
J.28
) implies that

\displaystyle\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}

\displaystyle\preceq\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{r_{1}}\bm{I}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathtt{III}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}],

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\preceq\frac{\epsilon_{2}}{r_{2}}\bm{I}-\frac{1}{r_{2}}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathtt{III}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}],

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}

\displaystyle\preceq\frac{\epsilon_{3}}{r_{3}}\bm{I}-\frac{1}{r_{3}}\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\mathtt{III}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}].

(J.31)

Note we have used
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}
and
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{C}}_{a}
.
Adding all three inequalities together and normalizing suitably by the number of jumps
m=\left|{S_{0}}\right|
, we have

\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}\preceq-\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{m}{r_{j}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{a=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{C}}_{a}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\right)+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\epsilon_{j}}{r_{j}}\right)\bm{I}.

(J.32)

The above provides the desired negative gradient condition on the full Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{H}_{C}
.
From Lemma
D.2
, any
\epsilon
-approximate local minimum
{\bm{\rho}}
of
\bm{H}_{C}
under thermal perturbation satisfies

1-\Tr(\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}{\bm{\rho}})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\epsilon_{j}+m\epsilon}{r_{j}}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon m\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{1}{r_{j}}.

(J.33)

By choosing
\epsilon\leq\frac{\delta}{2m}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{3}1/r_{j}\big)^{-1}=1/\poly(n,T,\delta^{-1})
, we guarantee that
1-\Tr(\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}{\bm{\rho}})\leq\delta
.
This concludes our proof of
Theorem

11
.

J.3
Explicit calculations for energy gradients

In this section of the appendix, we provide the missing calculations supporting the claims that were asserted in Eqs. (
J.16
), (
J.20
), and (
J.26
) in the above proof of Theorem
11
.

J.3.1
Gradient from
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}

Note that
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}
is a commuting Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}=\bm{H}_{\textnormal{clock}}=J_{\textnormal{clock}}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}f_{t}\bm{h}_{t,t+1}\quad

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\quad\bm{h}_{t,t+1}=\bm{I}\otimes\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{t,t+1}

(J.34)

where we set
f_{t}=(T-t)/T
and
J_{\textnormal{clock}}=1
.
We start by computing the gradient from a single jump operator, using the simplification from
Lemma

H.8
:

Lemma J.1
.

Let the jump operator
\bm{A}^{t}=\bm{I}\otimes\bm{X}_{t}
for each
t\in[T]
.
For all
t=2,\ldots,T
, we have

-\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]\succeq r_{1}\bm{h}_{t-1,t}-\epsilon_{0}\bm{I}

(J.35)

where
r_{1}=\Omega(\frac{1}{T\ln\beta})
and
\epsilon_{0}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T/\tau}+[(1+\beta)\ln\tau]/\tau+1/\beta)
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

H.8
, we have
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]=\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\ni t}}[\bm{H}_{\ni t}]
.
We then proceed in two cases.

Case 1:
2\leq t\leq T-1
.

In this case, the relevant part of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
that does not commute with
\bm{A}^{t}
is

\bm{H}_{\ni t}=f_{t-1}\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{t-1,t}+f_{t}\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{t,t+1}.

(J.36)

Observe that
\bm{H}_{\ni t}
has three degenerate eigenspaces with corresponding energies as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{P}^{0}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{s}}\in\{000,100,110,111\}}\lvert{\bm{s}}\rangle\!\langle{\bm{s}}\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1},\quad

\displaystyle E_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\bm{P}^{L}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{s}}\in\{010,011\}}\lvert{\bm{s}}\rangle\!\langle{\bm{s}}\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1},\quad

\displaystyle E_{L}

\displaystyle=f_{t-1},

\displaystyle\bm{P}^{R}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{s}}\in\{001,101\}}\lvert{\bm{s}}\rangle\!\langle{\bm{s}}\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1},\quad

\displaystyle E_{R}

\displaystyle=f_{t}.

(J.37)

The possible negative Bohr frequencies and the associated jumps are

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{t}_{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{t}^{0}\bm{X}_{t}\bm{P}_{t}^{L}=\lvert 000\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1},

\displaystyle\nu_{1}

\displaystyle=-f_{t-1},

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{t}_{\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{t}^{0}\bm{X}_{t}\bm{P}_{t}^{R}=\lvert 111\rangle\!\langle 101\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1},

\displaystyle\nu_{2}

\displaystyle=-f_{t},

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{t}_{\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{t}^{R}\bm{X}_{t}\bm{P}_{t}^{L}=\lvert 001\rangle\!\langle 011\rvert_{t-1,t,t+1},

\displaystyle\nu_{3}

\displaystyle=f_{t}-f_{t-1}=-1/T.

(J.38)

Furthermore, observe that the Bohr frequencies are exactly integer multiples of
1/T
, so we can lower bound the Bohr-frequency gap
\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H}_{\ni t})\geq 1/T
.
Then by
Lemma

H.3
and
H.4
, we can replace
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\ni t}}
with
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}_{\ni t}}
up to an
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T/\tau}+[(1+\beta)\ln\tau]/\tau)
error.

Letting
\theta_{j}=\nu_{j}\gamma_{\beta}(\nu_{j})
for
j=1,2,3
(recall
\gamma_{\beta}
is given in Eq. (
F.4
) with
\Lambda_{0}=1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}_{\ni t}}[\bm{H}_{\ni t}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H}_{\ni t})}\nu\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\big(\theta_{1}\lvert 010\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert+\theta_{2}\lvert 101\rangle\!\langle 101\rvert+\theta_{3}\lvert 011\rangle\!\langle 011\rvert\big)_{t-1,t,t+1}+\mathcal{O}(1/\beta),

(J.39)

where the last error term is due to heating transitions (positive Bohr frequencies), which incur errors of at most
\|\omega\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\mathds{1}(\omega>0)\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(1/\beta)
.
Note that
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}<0
, and furthermore we have

\displaystyle\min\{|\theta_{1}|,|\theta_{3}|\}\geq\min_{\omega\in[-1,-1/T]}|\omega|\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)=:r_{1}=\Omega\big(\frac{1}{T\ln\beta}\big).

(J.40)

Hence,

-\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}_{\ni t}}[\bm{H}_{\ni t}]\succeq r_{1}\big(\lvert 010\rangle\!\langle 010\rvert+\lvert 011\rangle\!\langle 011\rvert\big)_{t-1,t,t+1}-\mathcal{O}(1/\beta)\bm{I}.

(J.41)

Note the first term combines to make
\bm{h}_{t-1,t}
.
We then return to the finite-
\tau
Lindbladian up to the aforementioned error:

-\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\ni t}}[\bm{H}_{\ni t}]\succeq r_{1}\bm{h}_{t-1,t}-\mathcal{O}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{T}{\tau}}+\frac{(1+\beta)\ln\tau}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\Big)\bm{I}

(J.42)

Case 2:
t=T
.

The relevant part of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}
in this case is

\bm{H}_{\ni T}=f_{T-1}\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{T-1,T},

(J.43)

which has two eigenspaces.
There is only one negative Bohr frequency with a corresponding jump operator filtered at
\nu

\bm{A}^{T}_{\nu}=(\bm{I}-\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{T-1,T})\bm{X}_{T}\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{T-1,T}=\lvert 00\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{T-1,T}\quad\text{where}\quad\nu=-f_{T-1}=-\frac{1}{T}.

(J.44)

Then,

\textstyle\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\infty,\bm{H}_{\ni T}}[\bm{H}_{\ni T}]=-\frac{1}{T}\gamma_{\beta}(-\frac{1}{T})\lvert 01\rangle\!\langle 01\rvert_{T-1,T}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\beta})\bm{I}.

(J.45)

Note that
\frac{1}{T}\gamma_{\beta}(-\frac{1}{T})\geq r_{1}
. Applying
Lemma

H.3
and
H.4
to return to the finite
\tau
expression,

-\mathcal{D}_{t}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\ni T}}[\bm{H}_{\ni T}]\succeq r_{1}\bm{h}_{T-1,T}-\mathcal{O}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{T}{\tau}}+\frac{(1+\beta)\ln\tau}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\Big)\bm{I},

(J.46)

which is the advertised result.
∎

We are now ready to prove Eq. (
J.16
), which we state as the following lemma:

Lemma J.2
.

Assume
1\leq T\leq\tau
. We have

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{I}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]\succeq r_{1}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}})-\epsilon_{1a}\bm{I}

(J.47)

where

r_{1}=\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{T\ln\beta}\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon_{1a}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{T^{7/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{T}{\beta}+\frac{T(1+\beta)\ln\tau}{\tau}\Big).

(J.48)

Proof.

Note by linearity, we have

\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{I}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}].

(J.49)

Let
S_{\mathtt{I}}=\{\bm{I}\otimes\bm{X}_{t}:2\leq t\leq T\}
be a subset of the jump operators.
Then

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{I}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]\succeq-\sum_{a\in S_{\mathtt{I}}}\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]-\mathcal{O}\bigg(\left|{S_{0}}\right|\Big(\frac{\|\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}\|^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\Big)\bigg)\bm{I},

(J.50)

where the error contribution from neglecting the Lamb-shift term and the other jump operators in
S_{0}\setminus S_{\mathtt{I}}
are bounded by

Proposition

F.3
and
Lemma

H.1
.

Applying
Lemma

J.1
to the sum on the right hand side above, we get

-\sum_{a\in S_{\mathtt{I}}}\mathcal{D}_{a}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]\succeq r_{1}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\bm{h}_{t,t+1}-T\epsilon_{0}\bm{I}.

(J.51)

It is not difficult to see that

\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\bm{h}_{t,t+1}\succeq\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}},

(J.52)

that is, the smallest excitation has energy
1
. Hence,

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{I}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}]\succeq r_{1}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}})-\epsilon_{1a}\bm{I},

(J.53)

where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1a}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\bigg(\left|{S_{0}}\right|\Big(\frac{\|\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}\|^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\Big)\bigg)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{T^{3/2}}{\tau^{1/2}}+\frac{T(1+\beta)\ln\tau}{\tau}+\frac{T}{\beta}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{T^{7/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{T}{\tau}+\frac{T}{\beta}+\frac{T^{3/2}}{\tau^{1/2}}+\frac{T(1+\beta)\ln\tau}{\tau}\Big).

(J.54)

The last equality uses that
\left|{S_{0}}\right|,\|\bm{H}_{\mathtt{I}}\|=\mathcal{O}(T)
.
Since
1\leq T\leq\tau
, we have
T^{7/4}/\tau^{1/4}\geq T/\tau
and
T^{7/4}/\tau^{1/4}\geq T^{3/2}/\tau^{1/2}
, so we drop the latter two terms for the final error estimate in the lemma statement.
∎

J.3.2
Gradient from
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}

In this subsection, we prove

\displaystyle-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\succeq r_{2}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})-\epsilon_{2a}\bm{I}.

(J.55)

Denote
\ket{t}:=\ket{\eta_{\bm{x},t}}
in what follows. Let
\bm{L}_{+}
be the raising operator whose only non-trivial action is

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{+}\ket{t}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(t+1)(T-t)}\ket{t+1}\quad\text{for each}\quad 0\leq t\leq T-1

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\bm{L}_{-}

\displaystyle=:\bm{L}_{+}^{\dagger}.

(J.56)

Furthermore, let
\bm{L}_{x}=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{L}_{+}+\bm{L}_{-})
,
\bm{L}_{y}=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}(\bm{L}_{+}-\bm{L}_{-})
,
\bm{L}_{z}=\sum_{t=0}^{T}(t-T/2)\lvert t\rangle\!\langle t\rvert
.
These operators form a set of angular momentum operators as
[\bm{L}_{a},\bm{L}_{b}]=\mathrm{i}\epsilon_{abc}\bm{L}_{c}
for
a,b,c\in\{x,y,z\}
.
As noted earlier, we have

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}=J_{\textnormal{prop}}(\frac{T}{2}-\bm{L}_{x}).

(J.57)

The eigenstates are known to be

\displaystyle\ket{v_{k}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi\bm{L}_{y}/2}\ket{k}\quad\text{with eigenvalues}\quad\lambda_{k}=kJ_{\textnormal{prop}}\quad\text{for }\quad k=0,1,\ldots,T.

(J.58)

This integer spectrum means the minimum Bohr-frequency gap in the subspace
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
is
\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}|_{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}})=J_{\textnormal{prop}}
.

Next, we give jump operators with nontrivial gradient on any excited state of
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
. These will be the 1-local jumps acting on the clock register

\displaystyle\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{\ell}\quad\text{for each}\quad 1\leq\ell\leq T

(J.59)

which nicely respects the block-diagonal structure of
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}
such that

\braket{\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}|\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{\ell}|\eta_{{\bm{y}},t^{\prime}}}=0\quad\text{if}\quad{\bm{y}}\neq{\bm{x}}.

(J.60)

Thus, fixing
\bm{x}
, we merely need to consider effective jump operators

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{\ell})\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\equiv\bm{\sigma}_{\ell}\quad\text{such that}\quad\bm{\sigma}_{\ell}\ket{t}=(-1)^{\mathds{1}_{t\geq\ell}}\ket{t}.

(J.61)

Lemma J.3

(Good transition rates)
.

For the operators
\bm{\sigma}_{\ell}
in Eq. (
J.61
) and any
0\leq k<T
, we have that

\max_{\ell\in[T]}\left|\braket{v_{k}|\bm{\sigma}_{\ell}|v_{k+1}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}.

(J.62)

Proof.

Fix any
0\leq k<T
.
Observe that

\braket{v_{k}|\bm{L}_{z}|v_{k+1}}=\braket{k|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\bm{L}_{y}/2}\bm{L}_{z}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi\bm{L}_{y}/2}|k+1}=\braket{k|\bm{L}_{x}|k+1}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(k+1)(T-k)}.

(J.63)

Then,

\displaystyle\max_{t}\left|\braket{v_{k}|\bm{\sigma}_{t}|v_{k+1}}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|\braket{v_{k}|\bm{\sigma}_{t}|v_{k+1}}\right|

(maximum is at least the mean)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{T}\left|\braket{v_{k}|\bm{L}_{z}|v_{k+1}}\right|

(triangle inequality for
\bm{L}_{z}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{\sigma}_{t}
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}

(Since
\sqrt{(k+1)(T-k)}\geq\sqrt{T}
when
k<T
)

as advertised.
∎

Now that we understand the connectivity between the
t
labels, we may restore the
2^{n}
many labels
{\bm{x}}

\ket{v_{k}}\rightarrow\ket{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}\quad\text{such that}\quad\braket{v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{y}}}|v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{{\bm{x}}{\bm{y}}}.

(J.64)

Fortunately, we do not need to address the explicit labels
{\bm{x}}
due to the orthogonality properties in Eq. (
J.60
).
We may now calculate the gradient operator
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
.

Lemma J.4
.

Consider the thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{II}}=\sum_{a\in S_{0}}\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}}
.
Then,

\displaystyle-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\succeq r_{2}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})-\epsilon_{2}\bm{I}

(J.65)

for

\displaystyle r_{2}=\Omega(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{T\ln\beta})\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon_{2}=|S_{0}|\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau J_{\textnormal{prop}}}}\right).

(J.66)

Proof.

Observe that
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
projects onto a low-energy subspace of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
with an excitation gap of at least
J_{\textnormal{clock}}/T-2\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\|
from Weyl’s inequality.
Furthermore,
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
restricted to
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
has eigenvalues that are integer multiples of
J_{\textnormal{prop}}
, so the Bohr-frequency gap in the subspace is
\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}|_{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}})=J_{\textnormal{prop}}
.
Assuming
J_{\textnormal{prop}}/2<J_{\textnormal{clock}}/T-2\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{prop}}\|
, we may apply
Lemma

H.6
with
\bm{H}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}
,
\bm{Q}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}
to get

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S_{0}}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}|_{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}})}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\left|{\hat{f_{\mu}}(\omega-\nu)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle\preceq\sum_{a\in S_{\mathtt{II}}}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}|_{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}})}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\left|{\hat{f_{\mu}}(\omega-\nu)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

(J.67)

where
\mu=\Delta_{\nu}/2
and

\displaystyle E=\left|{S_{0}}\right|\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}\right).

(J.68)

The second line uses the negativity of the half-integral to reduce to the following subset of jump operators from Eq. (
J.4
)

S_{\mathtt{II}}=\{\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{\ell}:\ell\in[T]\}.

(J.69)

Let us now explicitly display the matrix elements of the above jump operators in the
\ket{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}
basis:

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{\ell})\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime},{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\ket{v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{y}}}}\bra{v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{y}}}}\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{\ell}\ket{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}\bra{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}=\sum_{k,k^{\prime},{\bm{x}}}\ket{v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{x}}}}\bra{v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{x}}}}\bm{I}\otimes\bm{Z}_{\ell}\ket{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}\bra{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}},

where we applied Eq. (
J.60
) to drop the sum on
{\bm{y}}
.
Thus we can rewrite the RHS of Eq. (
J.67
) as

\displaystyle(cont.)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell,k^{\prime},k,{\bm{x}}}\lvert v_{k,{\bm{x}}}\rangle\!\langle v_{k,{\bm{x}}}\rvert\bm{\sigma}_{\ell}\lvert v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{x}}}\rangle\!\langle v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{x}}}\rvert\bm{\sigma}_{\ell}\lvert v_{k,{\bm{x}}}\rangle\!\langle v_{k,{\bm{x}}}\rvert\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\left|{\hat{f_{\mu}}(\omega-J_{\textnormal{prop}}(k^{\prime}-k))}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle\preceq-\Omega\left(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{\ln\beta}\right)\sum_{k,{\bm{x}}}\ket{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}\bra{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}\max_{\ell\in[T]}\left|{\bra{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}\bm{\sigma}_{\ell}\ket{v_{k-1,{\bm{x}}}}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\preceq-\Omega\left(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{T\ln\beta}\right)\sum_{k\geq 1,{\bm{x}}}\ket{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}\bra{v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}

(applying Lemma
J.3
)

\displaystyle\preceq-\Omega\left(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{T\ln\beta}\right)\Big(\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}-\sum_{{\bm{x}}}\ket{v_{0,{\bm{x}}}}\bra{v_{0,{\bm{x}}}}\Big)=-\Omega(\frac{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}{T\ln\beta})\cdot\bm{P}_{\mathtt{I}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})

(J.70)

The first line uses the orthogonality condition
\braket{v_{k^{\prime},{\bm{y}}}|v_{k,{\bm{x}}}}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{{\bm{x}}{\bm{y}}}
, and the fact that the identical
\nu
labels on
\bm{A}_{\nu}^{a{\dagger}}
and
\bm{A}_{\nu}^{a}
enforce that transitions from
k^{\prime}
need to be to the same
k
on both sides.
The second line uses the negativity of the half-integral to focus on cooling transitions (which includes
k\rightarrow k-1
) and evaluates the integral (which concentrates near
\omega\approx J_{\textnormal{prop}}
). Lastly, we combine the above with the error bound on
E
to conclude the proof.
∎

J.3.3
Gradient from
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}

The goal of this subsection is to prove

-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}\succeq r_{3}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})-\epsilon_{3a}\bm{I}.

(J.71)

Here
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}
is the projector onto perturbed low-energy eigenstates of
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}+\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}
, corresponding to

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}=\sum_{{\bm{x}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\lvert\eta_{\bm{x}}\rangle\!\langle\eta_{\bm{x}}\rvert,

(J.72)

where

\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\sqrt{\xi_{t}}\ket{\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}}=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\sqrt{\xi_{t}}U_{t}\cdots U_{1}\ket{{\bm{x}}}\otimes\ket{C_{t}}\quad\text{and}\quad\qquad\xi_{t}=\frac{1}{2^{T}}\binom{T}{t}.

(J.73)

Recall our
Definition

14
where given a circuit with
L
computational gates, we pad it in the beginning and the end with
t_{0}
identity gates to make a total
T=2t_{0}+L
gates.
We can understand
\xi_{t}
as the probability from a symmetric binomial distribution
\text{Binom}(T,\frac{1}{2})
, which has substantial weight near the center where the interesting computation takes place.

Proposition J.1

(Lower bound
\xi_{t}
in the center)
.

Suppose
T=2t_{0}+L
and
t_{0}=cL^{2}
are positive integers. Then we have

\xi_{t}\geq\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-c/4}}{T+1}\qquad\text{for each}\quad t\in[t_{0},T-t_{0}].

(J.74)

Proof.

As a property of the binomial distribution
\text{Binom}(T,\frac{1}{2})
, we have
\xi_{t}\geq\xi_{t_{0}}
for all
t\in[t_{0},T-t_{0}]
.
Observe that

\binom{T}{t}^{-1}=(T+1)\int_{0}^{1}x^{t}(1-x)^{T-t}\mathrm{d}x\leq(T+1)\Big(\frac{t}{T}\Big)^{t}\Big(1-\frac{t}{T}\Big)^{T-t},

(J.75)

where the inequality comes from the fact that
\arg\max_{x\in[0,1]}x^{t}(1-x)^{T-t}=t/T
.
Then

\displaystyle\xi_{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{T}}\binom{T}{t_{0}}\geq\frac{f(L)}{T+1}

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad f(L)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{T}}\Big(\frac{T}{t_{0}}\Big)^{t_{0}}\Big(\frac{T}{T-t_{0}}\Big)^{T-t_{0}}=\Big(1+\frac{1}{2cL}\Big)^{cL^{2}}\Big(1-\frac{1}{2(cL+1)}\Big)^{cL^{2}+L}.

(J.76)

The last equality is obtained after plugging in
T=2t_{0}+L
,
t_{0}=cL^{2}
and simplifying.
We can use the first-derivative test to check that
f(L)
is monotonically decreasing, and so
f(L)\geq\lim_{L\to\infty}f(L)=\mathrm{e}^{-c/4}
.
Hence,
\xi_{t}\geq\xi_{t_{0}}\geq f(L)/(T+1)\geq\mathrm{e}^{-c/4}/(T+1)
.
∎

Using the fact that
U_{t_{j}-1}\cdots U_{1}
acts trivially on the
j
-th qubit (by definition of
t_{j}
), we see that
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
is diagonal in the
\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}
basis:

\displaystyle\braket{\eta_{\bm{x}}|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}|\eta_{\bm{y}}}

\displaystyle=J_{\textnormal{in}}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}=0}^{T}\sqrt{\xi_{t}\xi_{t^{\prime}}}\braket{\eta_{{\bm{x}},t}|\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{j}\lvert 1\rangle\!\langle 1\rvert_{j}\otimes\lvert C_{t_{j}-1}\rangle\!\langle C_{t_{j}-1}\rvert\Big)|\eta_{{\bm{y}},t^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}}\cdot J_{\textnormal{in}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}g_{j}\xi_{t_{j}-1}.

Since
g_{j}=1/\xi_{t_{j}-1}
(see
Definition

14
), then the above implies that that
\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}
are eigenstates of
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
with eigenvalue
J_{\textnormal{in}}\cdot\wt({\bm{x}})
, where
\wt({\bm{x}})
is the Hamming weight of bit string
{\bm{x}}
. While
\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}
are eigenstates of
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
, unfortunately, only
\ket{\eta_{\bm{0}}}
is an eigenstate of
\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}
; this will require an additional perturbation step to handle this off-block-diagonal effect.

Let
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}=\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}+\bm{V}_{\mathtt{III}}
where

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{II}}+\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}+\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\perp}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\perp}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{V}_{\mathtt{III}}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\perp}+\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\perp}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}.

(J.77)

We will also denote
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{III}}
as the thermal Lindbladian with respect to
\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}
.

We start by studying the gradient on
\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}
and
showing all its excited states have negative energy gradients.
It suffices to focus on the states in
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
, which is a low-energy subspace of eigenstates of
\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}
with excitation gap of at least
J_{\textnormal{prop}}-2\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\|
from Weyl’s inequality.
The effective Hamiltonian in this subspace has a simple form of decoupled qubits, which we write as

\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}}:=\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}|_{\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}=J_{\textnormal{in}}\sum_{{\bm{x}}}\wt({\bm{x}})\lvert\eta_{{\bm{x}}}\rangle\!\langle\eta_{{\bm{x}}}\rvert\equiv J_{\textnormal{in}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\bm{I}-\bm{Z}_{j}^{\textnormal{eff}})/2,

(J.78)

where
\bm{Z}_{j}^{\textnormal{eff}}
is the Pauli Z operator of a virtual qubit defined as
\bm{Z}_{j}^{\textnormal{eff}}\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}=(-1)^{x_{j}}\ket{\eta_{\bm{x}}}
.

Observe that
\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}}
has eigenvalues that are integer multiples of
J_{\textnormal{in}}
, and thus its Bohr-frequency gap is
\Delta_{\nu}(\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}})=J_{\textnormal{in}}
.
Hence, assuming
J_{\textnormal{in}}/2<J_{\textnormal{prop}}-2\|\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\|
, we can apply
Lemma

H.6
to see that the gradient operator sandwiched by
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}
can be understood by fully restricting to the subspace:
