\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{E}_{a}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S_{0}}\sum_{\nu\in B(\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}})}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{A}_{\nu}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{A}_{\nu}^{a}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega|\hat{f}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

(J.79)

where
\mu=J_{\textnormal{in}}/2
and

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{a}=|S_{0}|\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\|\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}\|^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{J_{\textnormal{in}}\tau}}\right).

(J.80)

To show that this has good gradients for all states in
\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})
, it is sufficient to consider the following subset of the jump operators from Eq. (
J.4
):

S_{\mathtt{III}}=\left\{\bm{X}_{j}\otimes\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert_{t_{j}}\right\}_{j=1}^{n}.

(J.81)

These jump operators from
S_{\mathtt{III}}
effectively flip the individual virtual qubits by

\textstyle\braket{\eta_{{\bm{y}}}|(\bm{X}_{j}\otimes\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert_{t_{j}})|\eta_{\bm{x}}}=\braket{{\bm{y}}|\bm{X}_{j}|{\bm{x}}}\sum_{t=0}^{t_{j}-1}\xi_{t}=:\braket{{\bm{y}}|\bm{X}_{j}|{\bm{x}}}\sqrt{\alpha_{j}}

(J.82)

where we have denoted
\alpha_{j}=(\sum_{t<t_{j}}\xi_{t})^{2}
.
Let

\displaystyle\bm{X}^{\textnormal{eff}}_{j}=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}(\bm{X}_{j}\otimes\lvert 0\rangle\!\langle 0\rvert_{t_{j}})\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}/\sqrt{\alpha_{j}}\quad\text{such that}\quad\left\lVert\bm{X}^{\textnormal{eff}}_{j}\right\rVert=1\quad\text{for each}\quad j=1,\ldots,n.

(J.83)

Note
\bm{X}^{\textnormal{eff}}_{j}
is effectively the Pauli X operator for the
j
-th virtual qubit.
Furthermore, note since
t_{j}\in[t_{0},T-t_{0}]
by our circuit construction in
Definition

14
, we have
\alpha_{j}\geq\xi_{t_{0}}^{2}\geq\Omega(1/T^{2})
by
Proposition

J.1
.

Next, we replace
\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}
by
\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}}
so we only need to talk about the virtual qubits.
We first restore the RHS of Eq. (
J.79
) to the thermal Lindbladian form by undoing the approximation in
Lemma

H.6
, which incurs another error bounded by
\tilde{E}_{a}
:

\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2\tilde{E}_{a}}}{{\approx}}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\textnormal{eff}}}[\bm{H}_{\textnormal{eff}}].

(J.84)

We then focus on the subset
S_{\mathtt{III}}
of jump operators and write

\textstyle\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}_{\textnormal{eff}}}[\bm{H}_{\textnormal{eff}}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{E}_{b}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{j}^{\dagger}[\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}}],

(J.85)

where we denoted
\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{j}}:=\mathcal{L}_{j}^{\beta,\tau,\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}}}
to be the thermal Lindbladian associated with effective jump operator
\bm{X}^{\textnormal{eff}}_{j}
and pulled out the normalization factor
\alpha_{j}
.
The error

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{b}=|S_{0}|\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\|\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}}\|^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}\right)

(J.86)

comes from neglecting the other jump operators
S_{0}\setminus S_{\mathtt{III}}
, and is bounded using
Lemma

H.1
.

Since the effective operators on different virtual qubits commute, we can treat them independently.
More formally, by
Lemma

H.8
we have
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{j}^{\dagger}[\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\textnormal{eff}}]=\mathcal{L}_{j}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{h}_{j}}[\bm{h}_{j}]
, where
\bm{h}_{j}=J_{\textnormal{in}}(\bm{I}-\bm{Z}_{\textnormal{eff}}^{j})/2
.
To bound the global gradient in Eq. (
J.85
), we first consider cooling a single qubit.

Lemma J.5

(Cooling a qubit)
.

On a qubit, consider the thermal Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{a}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
with the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}=J_{\textnormal{in}}(\bm{I}-\bm{Z})/2
and one jump operator
\bm{A}=\bm{X}
.
Then,

-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\succeq r_{\textnormal{in}}(\bm{I}-\bm{Z})-\epsilon_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{I}

(J.87)

where

r_{\textnormal{in}}=\Omega\Big(\frac{J_{\textnormal{in}}}{\ln\beta}\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon_{\textnormal{in}}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{J_{\textnormal{in}}^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau J_{\textnormal{in}}}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta J_{\textnormal{in}}}\Big).

(J.88)

Proof.

Again, we invoke a series of approximations

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle\approx\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]

(Proposition
F.3
)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{A}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{A}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

(Lemma
L.1
)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\bm{S}(\omega)^{\dagger}\bm{S}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

(secular approximation (
\mu=J_{\textnormal{in}}/2
): Corollary
K.3
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\bm{A}^{{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{A}_{\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega\left|{\hat{f_{\mu}}(\omega-\nu)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

(different blocks
\nu\neq\nu^{\prime}
decohere)

\displaystyle\preceq-\Omega(J_{\textnormal{in}}/\ln\beta)\ket{1}\bra{1}+\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta J_{\textnormal{in}}})\ket{0}\bra{0}.

(J.89)

The last line uses the transition matrix elements for the two Bohr frequencies
\nu=\pm J_{\textnormal{in}}
:

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{+1}=\ket{1}\bra{0},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{A}_{-1}=\ket{0}\bra{1}.

(J.90)

Combine the error bound to conclude the proof.
∎

Summing up over the contributions from the individual qubits, the global gradient satisfies

\displaystyle-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}

\displaystyle\succeq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\left[r_{\textnormal{in}}(\bm{I}-\bm{Z}_{j}^{\textnormal{eff}})-\epsilon_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{I}\right]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}-(2\tilde{E}_{a}+\tilde{E}_{b})\bm{I}

\displaystyle\succeq r_{3}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})-\tilde{\epsilon}_{3}\bm{I}

(J.91)

where we used the fact that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\bm{I}-\bm{Z}_{j}^{\textnormal{eff}})\succeq\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}}
, and denoted
r_{3}=r_{\textnormal{in}}\min_{j}\alpha_{j}
, and
\tilde{\epsilon}_{3}=2\tilde{E}_{a}+\tilde{E}_{b}+\epsilon_{\textnormal{in}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}
.
Since
\alpha_{j}=\Omega(1/T^{2})
and
\alpha_{j}\leq 1
, we have that

r_{3}=\Omega\Big(\frac{J_{\textnormal{in}}}{T^{2}\ln\beta}\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\epsilon}_{3}\leq 2\tilde{E}_{a}+\tilde{E}_{b}+n\epsilon_{\textnormal{in}}=|S_{0}|\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{\|\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}\|^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{J_{\textnormal{in}}\tau}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta J_{\textnormal{in}}}\Big).

(J.92)

Lastly, to obtain the gradient for the final Hamiltonian
\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
, we need to add the off-block-diagonal perturbation and show that the gradient persists on the subspace.
Directly applying subspace monotonicity (
Corollary

H.1
) yields loose bounds; we will need to invoke a finer-grained subspace monotonicity (
Corollary

H.2
) that exploits the fact that
\bm{V}
is off-block-diagonal and contribute to eigenvalue change at
second order

\mathcal{O}(\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2})
. We apply
Corollary

H.2
with
\bm{H}=\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}
and
\bm{H}^{\prime}=\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}
, and parameters

\displaystyle\bm{Q}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}

(low-energy eigensubspace of
\bm{H}=\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}
)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bm{Q}}

\displaystyle=J_{\textnormal{prop}}-2\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\right\rVert

(excitation gap)

\displaystyle\bm{V}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})+(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}})\bm{H}_{\textnormal{in}}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}

(off-block-diagonal perturbation)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\nu}

\displaystyle=J_{\textnormal{in}}

(subspace Bohr-frequency gap)

Therefore, by Corollary
H.2
,

\displaystyle-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}_{\mathtt{III}}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}_{\mathtt{III}}]\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}\succeq r_{3}\bm{P}_{\mathtt{II}}^{\prime}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{\mathtt{III}})-\epsilon_{3a}\bm{I}

(J.93)

where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{3a}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\epsilon_{3}}+\left|{S_{0}}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\|\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}\|^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\bm{Q}}/4}}{\beta}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\bm{Q}}\right\rVert\cdot\Big(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}_{\bm{Q}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\Delta_{\nu}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\Delta_{\nu}}\Big)+r_{3}\Big(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta_{\nu}\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}\Big)\bigg).

(J.94)

Noting that
|S_{0}|,\|\tilde{\bm{H}}_{\mathtt{III}}\|=\mathcal{O}(T)
,
\|\bm{V}\|=\mathcal{O}(nTJ_{\textnormal{in}})
,
\|\bm{H}_{\bm{Q}}\|=nJ_{\textnormal{in}}
, and

J_{\textnormal{in}}\ll J_{\textnormal{prop}}
, we simplify the error bound above by keeping the dominant term to get

\epsilon_{3a}\leq T\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{T^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{J_{\textnormal{in}}\tau}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta J_{\textnormal{in}}}+n\frac{(nTJ_{\textnormal{in}})^{2}}{J_{\textnormal{prop}}}\bigg)

(J.95)

as advertised earlier in Eq. (
J.26
).

Appendix K
Operator Fourier Transform

Recall that the exact form of thermal Lindbladians in Appendix
F
involves the operator Fourier transform (OFT)
[
42
]
for a set of jump operators
\bm{A}^{a}
.
In this appendix, we provide the key properties of OFT which are used in the proofs of many statements in Appendices
H
and
L
.
For any operator
\bm{A}
(the jump operator), and Hermitian operator
\bm{H}
(the Hamiltonian), and a weight function
f
, the
operator Fourier Transform
(OFT) is an integral over time-evolution of the operator
\bm{A}
,

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{A}}_{\hat{f}}(\omega)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}f(t)\mathrm{d}t.

(K.1)

Often, we will also write
\hat{\bm{A}}(\omega)
when we choose
f
to be the default
normalized window function

\displaystyle f_{\tau}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau}}\cdot\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\quad t\in[-\tau/2,\tau/2]\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

(K.2)

It is usually helpful to consider the energy eigenspaces
\bm{H}=\sum_{i}E_{i}\ket{\psi_{i}}\bra{\psi_{i}}=\sum_{E\in\text{Spec}(\bm{H})}E\bm{P}_{E}
and and write
\bm{A}
as the following decomposition

\bm{A}=\sum_{E_{2},E_{1}\in\text{Spec}(\bm{H})}\bm{P}_{E_{2}}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{E_{1}}=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\bm{A}_{\nu}\quad\text{where}\quad\bm{A}_{\nu}:=\sum_{E_{2}-E_{1}=\nu}\bm{P}_{E_{2}}\bm{A}\bm{P}_{E_{1}}.

(K.3)

Formally, these energy differences
\nu\in B(\bm{H}):=\{E_{i}-E_{j}\,|\,E_{i},E_{j}\in\mathrm{Spec}(\bm{H})\}
are called the
Bohr frequencies
(Figure
2
), and
\bm{A}_{\nu}
collects the matrix elements
\bra{\psi_{i}}\bm{A}\ket{\psi_{j}}
that changes the energy by
\nu=E_{i}-E_{j}
.
The Bohr frequencies are natural for the Heisenberg-picture evolution of
\bm{A}
since

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu t}\bm{A}_{\nu}.

(K.4)

Then, executing the Fourier integral yields the frequency domain representation

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{A}}_{f}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in B}\bm{A}_{\nu}\hat{f}(\omega-\nu)\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{f}(\omega):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathrm{d}t,

(K.5)

which contains a collection of Bohr frequencies
\nu
near
\omega
. Conceptually, we can think of the operator Fourier transform
\hat{\bm{A}}_{f}(\omega)
as the
smooth
probe of
\bm{A}_{\nu}
with exact Bohr frequency
\nu
, which generally requires resolving arbitrarily close eigenvalues.

K.1
Useful properties

We instantiate some useful properties of the Operator Fourier Transform.

Proposition K.1

(Operator Parseval’s identity
[
42
]
)
.

Consider a set of operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a}
and its operator Fourier transform with weight
f\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
. Then, we have a certain symmetry

(\bm{A}^{a}_{f}(\omega))^{{\dagger}}=\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{f^{*}}(-\omega)
and
certain Parseval-type identity

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\left|{f(t)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\preceq\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\bm{I}

(K.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\left|{f(t)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\preceq\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\right\rVert\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\bm{I}.

(K.7)

Intuitively, the above tells us that the Fourier Transforms
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)
with different frequencies
\omega
are “orthogonal” to each other and that the average of squares of strengths is bounded (reminiscent of a probability). Without using this norm sum constraint from Fourier transforms, one easily gets loose bounds. An alternative view of the above (as a natural purification) will prove useful for manipulating norms of expression involving
\bm{A}^{a}_{f}(\omega)
.

Corollary K.1

(Purification of Operator Fourier Transform)
.

In the prevailing notation, the abstract operator Fourier Transform has a norm bound,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes\ket{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}\sqrt{\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert},

(K.8)

where the continuous basis vectors satisfy the normalization

\displaystyle\braket{\omega^{\prime}|\omega}=\delta(\omega^{\prime}-\omega).

(K.9)

Proof.

We multiply the conjugate

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{a^{\prime}\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a^{\prime}}_{f}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\otimes\bra{a^{\prime}}\otimes\bra{\omega^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes\ket{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega.

(K.10)

Take the operator norm and use Proposition
K.1
to conclude the proof.
∎

The above “purification” trick applies to other quantities with a liberal choice of summation indices, whether they are
a,\omega,
or
\nu
.

Lemma K.1

(Norm inequalities from operator purification)
.

For any operator
\bm{O}
and any set of operators
\bm{A}_{i},\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}
acting on the same Hilbert space, we have that

\displaystyle\textstyle\left\|\sum_{i,j}\bm{A}_{i}^{\dagger}\bm{O}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}G_{ij}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{G}\|\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert\sqrt{\left\|\sum_{i}\bm{A}_{i}^{\dagger}\bm{A}_{i}\right\|\left\|\sum_{j}\bm{A}_{j}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}\right\|}

(K.11)

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i,j}\bm{A}_{i}^{\dagger}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}G_{ij}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{G}\|\sqrt{\left\|\sum_{i}\bm{A}_{i}^{\dagger}\bm{A}_{i}\right\|\left\|\sum_{j}\bm{A}_{j}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}\right\|}

(K.12)

Proof.

By homogeneity, it suffices to set normalization to be
\left\lVert\sum_{j}\bm{A}_{j}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\sum_{i}\bm{A}_{i}^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}_{i}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\bm{G}\right\rVert=1
. Introduce purifications

\displaystyle\bm{G}^{\prime}:=\bm{I}\otimes\sum_{i,j}G_{ij}\ket{i}\bra{j},\qquad\bm{O}^{\prime}:=\bm{O}\otimes\bm{I},

(K.13)

\displaystyle\bm{V}^{\prime}:=\sum_{j}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}\otimes\ket{j},\qquad\bm{V}:=\sum_{i}\bm{A}_{i}\otimes\ket{i},

(K.14)

which are all bounded by
\left\lVert\bm{G}^{\prime}\right\rVert,\left\lVert\bm{O}^{\prime}\right\rVert,\left\lVert\bm{V}^{\prime}\right\rVert,\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq 1
. Then,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{i,j}\bm{A}_{i}^{\dagger}\bm{O}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}G_{ij}\bigg\|

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{V}^{\prime}\bm{O}^{\prime}\bm{G}^{\prime}\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq 1

(K.15)

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{i,j}\bm{A}_{i}^{\dagger}\bm{A}^{\prime}_{j}G_{ij}\bigg\|

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\bm{V}^{\prime}\bm{G}^{\prime}\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq 1.

(K.16)

Rescale to obtain the advertised result.
∎

K.2
Secular approximation

Due to the energy-time uncertainty principle, the energies
\omega
that are accessed by finite-time quantum algorithms always inherit an uncertainty.
Indeed, when we choose our weight function
f_{\tau}(t)
in Eq. (
K.2
) for our operator Fourier transform in the frequency domain, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{A}}_{f}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in B}\bm{A}_{\nu}\hat{f}(\omega-\nu)\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{f}(\omega)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}}{\mathrm{i}\omega\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\quad\text{when}\quad f(t)=f_{\tau}(t).

(K.17)

Note with this choice,
\hat{f}(\omega)
has a heavy tail
\sim 1/\omega
, which is reminiscent of unamplified phase estimation. Therefore, even when restricting to jumps with
\omega<0
, there is a decent chance that
\hat{\bm{A}}_{f}(\omega)
mistakenly activates a heating transition (
\nu>0
) instead of a cooling transition
(\nu<0)
, unintentionally heating up the system instead of cooling it.

To control the resulting error, in this section, we introduce the
secular approximation

[
42
]
of the Fourier transformed operators
\bm{A}_{f}(\omega)
. The secular approximation applies truncation to the Fourier-transformed operators in the frequency domain by truncating Bohr frequencies
E\in B
that deviate substantially from the frequency label
\omega
. Truncation at energy difference
\mu
can be achieved by setting a step function and defining the following secular-approximated operators as follows

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{S}}_{f,\mu}(\omega):=\sum_{\nu\in B}\bm{A}_{\nu}\hat{f}(\omega-\nu)\cdot\hat{s}_{\mu}(\omega-\nu)\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{s}_{\mu}(\omega):=\mathds{1}(\left|{\omega}\right|<\mu).

(K.18)

We often drop subscript
f,\mu
for simplicity. The truncation error is an operator whose norm can be bounded by the following (as a variant of Corollary
K.1
).

Corollary K.2

(Secular approximation)
.

In the prevailing notation,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)-\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega))\otimes\ket{a}\otimes\ket{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\hat{f}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\sqrt{\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert}.

(K.19)

The error is controlled by the 2-norm for the truncated tail

\left\lVert\hat{f}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\int_{\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\mu}|\hat{f}(\omega)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

(K.20)

noting that conveniently, the Fourier transform preserves the 2-norm of functions.
For our bump function (
K.2
) in particular, we can integrate the tail-bound

\left\lVert\hat{f}_{\tau}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{\pi\mu\tau}.

(K.21)

This conveniently leads to bounds on other quantities involving operator Fourier Transforms
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)
.

Corollary K.3

(Error from secular approximation)
.

For any real function
\theta:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{A}}_{f}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}_{f}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}-\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{S}}_{f,\mu}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{S}}_{f,\mu}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\hat{f}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}\sqrt{\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert}.

(K.22)

Proof.

It suffices to set normalization
\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert=1
. Introduce purifications

\displaystyle\bm{F}

\displaystyle:=\bm{I}\otimes\bm{I}\otimes\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega)\ket{\omega}\bra{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega,

(K.23)

\displaystyle\bm{V}

\displaystyle:=\left(\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes\ket{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega\right),

(K.24)

\displaystyle\bm{V}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\left(\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{f}(\omega)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes\ket{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega\right).

(K.25)

Then, by a telescoping sum,

\displaystyle(LHS)=\left\lVert\bm{V}^{{\dagger}}\bm{F}\bm{V}-\bm{V}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}\bm{F}\bm{V}^{\prime}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\bm{V}^{{\dagger}}-\bm{V}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}})\bm{F}\bm{V}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\bm{V}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}\bm{F}(\bm{V}-\bm{V}^{\prime})\right\rVert.

(K.26)

We conclude the proof using the individual bounds on the operator norm

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}\quad

(Corollary
K.1
)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{V}^{\prime}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{f}\cdot\hat{s}_{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}\quad

(Corollary
K.1
)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{F}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}

(K.27)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{V}^{\prime}-\bm{V}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{f}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\quad.

(Corollary
K.2
)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix L
Proving monotonicity of energy gradient under level splitting

It will often be helpful to understand how the energy gradients of a Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
change when a perturbation
\bm{V}
is added to yield
\bm{H}^{\prime}=\bm{H}+\bm{V}
.
This allows us to characterize the energy gradient of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
by analyzing the unperturbed spectrum of
\bm{H}
, which is usually much simpler than that of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
.
Indeed, this is an important part of our proof strategy for showing our key result that
\bm{H}_{C}
has no suboptimal local minima (
Theorem

11
in
Appendix

J
).

The relationship we can prove, which was previously stated in
Theorem

10
in
Appendix

H
, takes the form of
monotonicity
. As the name implies, the result only holds in one direction; it fails when the
\bm{H}^{\prime}
and
\bm{H}
are switched. It is imperative that
\bm{H}
have a highly degenerate spectrum with the
Bohr-frequency gap

\displaystyle\Delta_{\nu}(\bm{H}):=\min_{\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}\in B(\bm{H})}\left|{\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}}\right|,

(L.1)

which sets an energy scale for which
\bm{V}
is perturbative. Note that the Bohr-frequency gap is upper bounded by the gap in the spectrum
11
11

11

The Bohr-frequency gap can be much smaller than the eigenvalue gap. For example, consider the energies
\{-0.99,0,1\}
, which has an eigenvalue gap of
0.99
and a Bohr-frequency gap of
0.01
.

\displaystyle\Delta_{\nu}\leq\Delta_{E}\quad\text{where}\quad\Delta_{E}=\min_{E_{1}\neq E_{2}\in\text{Spec}(\bm{H})}\left|{E_{1}-E_{2}}\right|.

(L.2)

We now state a more general version of
Theorem

10
, which we prove in the remainder of this appendix.

Theorem 12

(Monotonicity of gradient under level splitting, expanded version)
.

Consider a Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
with a highly degenerate spectrum and Bohr-frequency gap
\Delta_{\nu}:=\min_{\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}\in B(\bm{H})}|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|
, and a perturbed Hamiltonian
\bm{H}^{\prime}=\bm{H}+\bm{V}
.
Suppose the perturbation is weaker than the Bohr-frequency gap,
\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{8}\Delta_{\nu}
.
For any
\beta,\tau>0
, let
\mathcal{L}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}}_{a}
be thermal Lindbladians with jumps
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a\in S}
, where
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\leq 1
and the transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
is given by Eq. (
F.4
).
Let
\delta_{\lambda}=\max_{j}|\lambda_{j}(\bm{H})-\lambda_{j}(\bm{H}^{\prime})|
,
where
\lambda_{j}(\bm{X})
is the
j
-th largest eigenvalue of
\bm{X}
, and let

\theta_{\max}=\max_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}|\nu\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)\mathds{1}(\nu\leq\Delta_{\nu}/2)|
.
For any two operators
\bm{O}
and
\bm{O}^{\prime}
, where
[\bm{O}^{\prime},\bm{H}^{\prime}]=0
, we have the monotone property that

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\bm{H}]\succeq r\bm{O}-\epsilon\bm{I}\quad\text{implies}\quad-\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\succeq r\bm{O}^{\prime}-\epsilon^{\prime}\bm{I}

(L.3)

where

\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon+\left|{S}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\delta_{\lambda}+\theta_{\max}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}}+r\|\bm{O}-\bm{O}^{\prime}\|\right).

(L.4)

For the special case of
\bm{O}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}
and
\bm{O}^{\prime}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}^{\prime}
, where
\bm{P}
projects onto an eigensubspace of
\bm{H}
, and
\bm{P}^{\prime}
projects onto the corresponding perturbed eigensubspace in
\bm{H}^{\prime}
, then we have the following simpler error bound

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon+\left|{S}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\big(1+\frac{\Lambda_{0}+r}{\Delta_{\nu}}\big)\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\right).

(L.5)

The above result is non-trivial because naïve perturbation theory fails: the Lindbladian depends sensitively on the perturbation
\bm{V}
(as it uses a long Hamiltonian simulation time
\tau\lVert\bm{V}\rVert\gg 1
). In fact, it drastically fails if the energy spectrum of
\bm{H}
has a (nearly) continuous spectrum (as the opposite of the premise of gapped degenerate subspaces). We can understand the energy scale associated with the minimum Bohr-frequency gap
\Delta_{\nu}
as the meaningful quantity for which
\bm{V}
is a perturbation

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau}\ll\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\ll\Delta_{\nu}.

(L.6)

Otherwise, the
1/\tau
energy resolution is too small compared to the intended perturbation
\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert
.

The proof of
Theorem

12
will be quite involved. Technically, we heavily utilize the manipulations using the operator Fourier Transform (Appendix
K
). The key subroutines of the proof are discussed separately as follows.
First, in Appendix
L.1
, we will simplify the intimidating expression for energy gradient
\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]
.
Secondly, in Appendix
L.2
, we isolate the key non-perturbative argument, which roughly says level splitting only improves the gradient.
We then provide some results from perturbation theory in
Section

L.3
.
The altogether proof is presented in Appendix
L.4
, with minor supporting calculations in
Section

L.5
.
We also prove two corollaries of
Theorem

12
that apply to subspace gradients in Appendix
L.6
.

Since this appendix only consider thermal Lindbladians, in what follows we will drop the superscripts
\beta,\tau,\bm{H}
, i.e.
\mathcal{L}\equiv\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{D}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}
, etc.

L.1
Expressing the energy gradient

The thermal Lindbladian is quite cumbersome to manipulate. Nicely, the energy gradient operator associated with the dissipative part
\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]
permits a much simpler approximate form up to a controllable error. Combining with error bounds on the Lamb-shift term
[\bm{H}_{LS},\bm{H}]
(Proposition
F.3
) allows us to approximate the full gradient operator
\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]
.

Lemma L.1

(Expression for energy gradient)
.

Consider the operator Fourier Transforms
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)
weighted by the bump function
f_{\tau}
in Eq. (
K.2
) with Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
. Then, for any Fourier transform pairs
\gamma(\omega)
and
c(t)
, the energy gradient associated with the purely dissipative Lindbladian

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\left(\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\bm{H}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega),\bm{H}\}\right)\mathrm{d}\omega

(L.7)

can be well approximated as a simpler form using

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]-\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\omega\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rVert\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}\tau}\cdot\left\lVert c\right\rVert_{1}\cdot\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert.

(L.8)

Intuitively, the following expression is the simplest proxy one can write down to capture the rate of energy change

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\omega\times\gamma(\omega)\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\sim\text{(energy difference)}\times\text{(rate)}.

(L.9)

Indeed, the Bohr frequency
\omega
is essentially the energy difference after jump operator
\bm{A}^{a}(\omega)
; but because of the energy uncertainty in the operator Fourier transform (i.e., because
\hat{f}(\omega)
is not a delta function), this interpretation must be corrected by an error scaling as the energy resolution
\sim 1/\tau
.
The starting point of the calculation is a certain integration-by-part trick that relates the Hamiltonian operator
\bm{H}
to the scalar
\omega
.

Proposition L.1

(Integration by parts)
.

In the setting of Lemma
L.1
,

\displaystyle[\bm{H},\hat{\bm{A}}(\omega)]=\omega\hat{\bm{A}}(\omega)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\left(\bm{A}(\tau/2)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}-\bm{A}(-\tau/2)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}\right).

(L.10)

Proof.

Integration of the derivative can be expanded by the product rule

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\left(\bm{A}(\tau/2)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}-\bm{A}(-\tau/2)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\int_{-\tau/2}^{\tau/2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\bm{A}(t)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\int_{-\tau/2}^{\tau/2}\left(\mathrm{i}[\bm{H},\bm{A}(t)]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}-\mathrm{i}\omega\bm{A}(t)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}[\bm{H},\hat{\bm{A}}(\omega)]-\mathrm{i}\omega\hat{\bm{A}}(\omega).

(L.11)

Rearrange to conclude the proof.
∎

Observe that taking the infinite time limit
\tau\rightarrow\infty
(i.e., perfect energy resolution) in the above proposition recovers the relation for the true Bohr frequencies
\nu

\displaystyle[\bm{H},\bm{A}_{\nu}]=\nu\bm{A}_{\nu}\quad\text{for each}\quad\nu\in B(\bm{H}).

(L.12)

At finite
\tau
, the above leads to simple bounds on the correction term.
We now present the proof of Lemma
L.1
.

Proof of Lemma
L.1
.

We calculate

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\left(\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\bm{H}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega),\bm{H}\}\right)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H},\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)]-[\bm{H},\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}]\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\right)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\omega\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega+\bm{E},

(L.13)

where the error term
\bm{E}
is given by Proposition
L.1
as

\displaystyle\bm{E}

\displaystyle:=\frac{-\mathrm{i}}{2\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\left(\bm{A}^{a}(\tau/2)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}-\bm{A}^{a}(-\tau/2)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}\right)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\sqrt{2\pi\tau}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\left(\bm{A}^{a}(\tau/2)^{\dagger}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}-\bm{A}^{a}(-\tau/2)^{\dagger}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}\right)\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega.

(L.14)

To bound this error term, let us calculate one of the four individual terms as an example

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma(\omega)\bm{A}^{a}(\tau/2)^{\dagger}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}\mathrm{d}\omega

(L.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\tau}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c(t_{1})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\int_{-\tau/2}^{\tau/2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tau/2}\bm{A}^{a}(\tau/2)^{\dagger}\bm{A}^{a}(t_{2})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t_{2}}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}\omega\quad

(Fourier Transforms)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\tau/2}^{\tau/2}c(\tau/2-t_{2})\bm{A}^{a}(\tau/2)^{\dagger}\bm{A}^{a}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{2}.\quad

(using
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathrm{d}\omega=2\pi\delta(t)
)

We can bound the operator norm of this term by
\|c\|_{1}/\sqrt{\tau}
after applying the triangle inequality
and using the fact that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a}(\tau/2)^{\dagger}\bm{A}^{a}(t_{2})\right\rVert=\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}(\tau/2)\bm{A}^{a}(t_{2})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\otimes\bra{a}\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a}\otimes\ket{a}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert.

(L.16)

Repeat a similar argument for the other three terms to conclude the proof.
∎

L.2
Monotonicty of rates

Thermal Lindbladians generally depend sensitively on the Hamiltonian as it uses Hamiltonian simulation for a long time
\tau

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}\tau}\quad\text{for}\quad\tau\gg 1.

(L.17)

Therefore, even adding a small perturbation to the Hamiltonian
\bm{H}^{\prime}=\bm{H}+\bm{V}
may have a non-perturbative effect on
\mathcal{L}
since

\displaystyle\tau\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\gg 1\quad\text{implies}\quad\left\lVert\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{1-1}\gg 0.

(L.18)

In other words, at a large
\tau
, it is not obvious at all why the Lindbladians
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are related. Indeed, the original Davies’ generator (
\tau\rightarrow\infty
) is unstable against arbitrarily small perturbations to the Hamiltonian; whenever energy degeneracy is broken, the Lindbladian can change substantially.

Nevertheless, what we can show as a compromise is that the rate only
increases
if the perturbation only introduces
level splitting
; this amounts to the assumption that the original Hamiltonian has highly degenerate subspaces with a certain Bohr frequencies gap
\Delta_{\nu}
as another large energy scale

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau}\ll\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\ll\Delta_{\nu}.

(L.19)

Intuitively, level splitting causes
decoherence
(and only decoherence) in the Bohr frequencies; for large
\tau
, the Lindblidan can indeed tell the transitions
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
apart if the Bohr frequencies are sufficiently different. Fortunately, even though decoherence can change the Lindbladian by a lot, we establish certain
monotonicity
of transition rates. That is,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
must have as good transition rates as
\mathcal{L}
.
A good example of
\bm{O}
would be an energy subspace projector. However, the argument works for general
\bm{O}
, which makes it more flexible to use.

Lemma L.2

(Decoherence increases the rates)
.

For any set of operators
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a\in S}
, suppose there exists an operator
\bm{O}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\succeq\bm{O}\quad\text{where}\quad[\bm{O},\bm{H}]=0.

(L.20)

Then, the operator Fourier Transforms
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)
(subscript
f
omitted) for some normalized weight
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=1
and Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\succeq\bm{O}.

(L.21)

Proof.

By Proposition
K.1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega-\bm{O}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\left(\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}|f(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-\bm{O}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\left(\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}-\bm{O}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}|f(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{X}^{{\dagger}}\cdot\bm{X}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}|f(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\succeq 0.

(L.22)

The second equality uses that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=1
and that
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{O}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}=\bm{O}
. The last line establishes PSD order using the assumption that there exists operator
\bm{X}
such that
\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}-\bm{O}=\bm{X}^{{\dagger}}\bm{X}
. Together, we establish the desired statement.
∎

L.3
Perturbation theory of eigenstates and eigenvalues

We state a few useful facts about perturbed eigenspace and eigenvalues that would be useful in the proofs.

Proposition L.2

(Davis-Kahan
\sin\Theta
theorem (see also Theorem VII.3.1 of
[
78
]
))
.

Let
\bm{H}
and
\tilde{\bm{H}}
be two equal-sized Hermitian matrices. Let
\bm{P}
be the projector onto eigenstates of
\bm{H}
with eigenvalue in an interval
[a,b]
.
Let
\tilde{\bm{P}}^{\perp}
be the projector onto eigenstates of
\tilde{\bm{H}}
with eigenvalues outside the interval
[a-\delta,b+\delta]
.
Then

\|\bm{P}\tilde{\bm{P}}^{\perp}\|\leq\|\bm{H}-\tilde{\bm{H}}\|/\delta.

(L.23)

Here
\|\cdot\|
is the spectral norm (or any unitarily invariant norm).

Furthermore, the following fact bounds errors on perturbed eigenvalues:

Proposition L.3

(Weyl’s inequality)
.

For any two equal-sized Hermitian matrices
\bm{H}
and
\tilde{\bm{H}}
, we have
|\lambda_{j}(\bm{H})-\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\bm{H}})|\leq\|\bm{H}-\tilde{\bm{H}}\|
for all
j
, where
\lambda_{j}(\bm{X})
is the
j
-th largest eigenvalue of matrix
\bm{X}
.

Together, these facts imply that

Lemma L.3
.

Let
\bm{H}
and
\tilde{\bm{H}}=\bm{H}+\bm{V}
be Hamiltonians.
Let
\bm{P}
be the projector onto eigenstates of
\bm{H}
with eigenvalues in some interval
[a,b]
, which are separated from the other eigenvalues by a gap of at least
\Delta
.
If
\|\bm{V}\|\leq\Delta/4
, then

1.

There exists a spectral projector
\tilde{\bm{P}}
onto eigenstates of
\tilde{\bm{H}}
with eigenvalues in
[a-\Delta/4,b+\Delta/4]
, which are separated from the other eigenvalues by a gap of at least
\Delta/2
.

2.

\|\bm{P}-\tilde{\bm{P}}\|\leq 8\|\bm{V}\|/\Delta
.

Proof.

The existence of
\tilde{\bm{P}}
(item 1) holds because of
Proposition

L.3
above.
Then observe that

\|\bm{P}-\tilde{\bm{P}}\|=\|\bm{P}-\bm{P}\tilde{\bm{P}}+\bm{P}\tilde{\bm{P}}-\tilde{\bm{P}}\|\leq\|\bm{P}\tilde{\bm{P}}^{\perp}\|+\|\bm{P}^{\perp}\tilde{\bm{P}}\|\leq 8\|\bm{V}\|/\Delta,

(L.24)

where the last inequality is obtained by applying
Proposition

L.2
with
\delta=\Delta/4
to bound
\|\bm{P}\tilde{\bm{P}}^{\perp}\|
and
\|\bm{P}^{\perp}\tilde{\bm{P}}\|
.
∎

Lemma L.4

(Off-block-diagonal perturbation)
.

Consider a block diagonal Hermitian matrix
\bm{D}=\bm{D}_{1}+\bm{D}_{2}
, where the two blocks correspond to orthogonal subspace projectors
\bm{P}_{1}
and
\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{1}=\bm{P}_{2}
and are separated by eigenvalue gap at least
\Delta
. Add an off-block-diagonal Hermitian perturbation
\bm{V}=\bm{V}_{12}+\bm{V}_{21}
such that
\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq\Delta/4
.
Then, there is an anti-Hermitian operator
\bm{B}
and an absolute constant
C_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\bm{D}+\bm{V}=\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}\bm{D}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}}+(\bm{D}+\bm{V}-\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}\bm{D}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}})

\displaystyle\text{where }\qquad\left\lVert\bm{B}\right\rVert\leq C_{0}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left\lVert\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}}(\bm{D}+\bm{V})\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}-\bm{D}\right\rVert\leq C_{0}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta}.

(L.25)

This implies
the sorted eigenvalues of
\bm{D}
are perturbed by
C_{0}\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}/\Delta
.

We remark that the scaling with respect to
\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert
is consistent with perturbation theory: the
angle
change is first-order
\sim\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta}
, and the
eigenvalue
change is second-order
\sim\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert^{2}}{\Delta}
. Note that for diagonal perturbation, the eigenvalue change is only bounded by
\sim\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert
.

Proof.

Observe that

\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}}(\bm{D}+\bm{V})\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}=\bm{D}+(\bm{V}+[\bm{B},\bm{D}])+[\bm{B},\bm{V}]+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\ad_{\bm{B}}^{k}(\bm{D}+\bm{V}),

(L.26)

Let us choose
\bm{B}
to cancel the first order term in
\bm{V}
, i.e.,

\bm{V}=-[\bm{B},\bm{D}].

(L.27)

We can solve for
\bm{B}
by working in the eigenbasis of
\bm{D}=\sum_{i}D_{i}\lvert\psi_{i}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{i}\rvert
.
Then denoting
O_{ij}=\braket{\psi_{i}|\bm{O}|\psi_{j}}
, we can rewrite Eq. (
L.27
) as

V_{ij}=B_{ij}(D_{i}-D_{j})\qquad\text{or}\qquad B_{ij}=\frac{V_{ij}}{D_{i}-D_{j}}.

(L.28)

Note
B_{ij}=V_{ij}=0
whenever
|D_{i}-D_{j}|\geq\Delta
by assumption. Hence, we can solve for
\bm{B}
using the Heisenberg picture Fourier transform:

\displaystyle B_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(D_{i}-D_{j})t}V_{ij}\mathrm{d}t=V_{ij}\hat{f}(D_{j}-D_{i})\quad\text{for each}\quad i,j

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\bm{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\underset{=:\bm{V}(t)}{\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{D}t}\bm{V}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{D}t}}}\mathrm{d}t,

(L.29)

where we choose the function
f(t)
whose Fourier transform matches the reciprocal at sufficiently large values,

\hat{f}(\omega)=\frac{1}{-\omega}\quad\text{when}\quad\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\Delta,

(L.30)

but remain “nice” near
\omega=0
. One example is to use a smooth bump function

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\omega}\cdot b(\frac{\omega}{\Delta})\quad\text{where}\quad b(x)=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{if}\quad\left|{x}\right|\geq 1\\
\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\quad&\text{if}\quad\left|{x}\right|\approx 0.\end{cases}

(L.31)

For concreteness, we take

\displaystyle b(x)=\begin{cases}1-\exp(\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{x^{2}}})\quad&\text{if}\quad\left|{x}\right|<1\\
1&\text{else}\end{cases}

(L.32)

Then, taking triangle inequality and using the unitary invariance of the operator norm,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{B}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1}\cdot\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq C_{0}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta}.

(L.33)

The last inequality bounds the Fourier transform by change-of-variable
x=\omega/\Delta
and leaves a constant
C_{0}
that depends on the inverse Fourier transform of the “dimensionless function”
b(x)/x
.
This bound on
\bm{B}
then allows us to control the higher-order errors

\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}}(\bm{D}+\bm{V})\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}=\bm{D}+\bm{V}+\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}s}[\bm{B},\bm{V}]\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}s}\mathrm{d}s+[\bm{B},\bm{D}]+\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}s}[\bm{B},[\bm{B},\bm{D}]]\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}s}(1-s)\mathrm{d}s.

Substitute
[\bm{B},\bm{D}]=-\bm{V}
and rearranging, we get

\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}}(\bm{D}+\bm{V})\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}-\bm{D}=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}s}[\bm{B},\bm{V}]\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}s}s\mathrm{d}s.

(L.34)

Apply the triangle inequality, we get

\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}}(\bm{D}+\bm{V})\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}-\bm{D}\|\leq\|[\bm{B},\bm{V}]\|\int_{0}^{1}s\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{0}\frac{\|\bm{V}\|^{2}}{\Delta}.

(L.35)

Finally, to obtain sorted eigenvalues of
\bm{D}
, use the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}
is unitary and apply Weyl’s inequality for
\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{B}}\bm{D}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{B}}
.
∎

Figure 4:
The energy scales
\frac{1}{\tau}\ll\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\ll\Delta_{\nu}
in one plot. The Hamiltonian perturbation
\bm{V}
causes eigenvalues of
\bm{H}
to change by at most
\delta_{\lambda}\leq\|\bm{V}\|
, which splits the Bohr frequencies such that
|\nu-\nu^{\prime}|\leq 2\delta_{\lambda}
.
Here
\mu
is the cut-off frequency for the secular approximation
\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{f,\mu}(\omega^{\prime})=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}}\bm{A}_{\nu^{\prime}}^{a}\hat{f}(\omega^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime})\hat{s}_{\mu}(\omega^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime})
. As long as we choose
\mu<\frac{\Delta_{\nu}-4\delta_{\lambda}}{2}
small enough, the secular approximation
\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})
can only contain Bohr frequencies
\bm{A}_{\nu^{\prime}}
from at most one block
\nu
, i.e., different blocks decohere.

L.4
Proof of
Theorem

12

We combine the above ingredients for the proof of Theorem
12
.
In what follows, let
E_{j}
,
\bm{P}_{E_{j}}
,
\nu
be the eigenvalues, eigenspace projectors, and Bohr frequencies of the unperturbed Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
; furthermore let
E_{j}^{\prime}
,
\bm{P}_{E_{j}^{\prime}}

\nu^{\prime}
, be their counterpart for the perturbed Hamiltonian
\bm{H}^{\prime}
.

It will be helpful to display the structure of the Bohr frequencies and the energy eigenspaces under perturbation by

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\sum_{E_{1}-E_{2}=\nu}\bm{P}_{E_{1}}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{P}_{E_{2}}=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\in B(\bm{H}^{\prime})}\sum_{E^{\prime}_{1}-E^{\prime}_{2}=\nu^{\prime}}\bm{P}_{E^{\prime}_{1}}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{P}_{E^{\prime}_{2}}=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\in B(\bm{H}^{\prime})}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu^{\prime}}=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}

(L.36)

where we defined

\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}:=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\in B(\bm{H}^{\prime}),\penalty\ \nu^{\prime}\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu^{\prime}}\qquad\text{and}\quad\nu^{\prime}\approx\nu\iff\left|{\nu^{\prime}-\nu}\right|\leq 2\delta_{\lambda}.

(L.37)

In other words, the perturbed set of Bohr frequencies can be identified with the original degenerate blocks according to eigenvalue perturbation
12
12

12

Note that we always have
\delta_{\lambda}\leq\|\bm{V}\|
by
Proposition

L.3
, but we keep
\delta_{\lambda}
as an separate parameter which helps yield better bounds when
\delta_{\lambda}\ll\|\bm{V}\|
, such as the case when the
\bm{V}
is an off-diagonal perturbation (see
Lemma

L.4
).

bounded by
\delta_{\lambda}
, under the assumption that the perturbation is weaker than the Bohr frequency differences
\Delta_{\nu}>4\delta_{\lambda}
.
This structure is crucial for proving the monotonicity of gradients; see Figure
4
.
For later use, we also define
\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})
to be the operator Fourier transform of
\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}
with respect to the perturbed Hamiltonian
\bm{H}^{\prime}
, and consider its secular approximation
\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})
at truncation scale
\mu
, i.e.,

\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu^{\prime}}\hat{f}_{\tau}(\omega^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}),\qquad\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu^{\prime}}\hat{f}_{\tau}(\omega^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime})\mathds{1}(|\omega^{\prime}-\nu^{\prime}|<\mu).

(L.38)

In what follows, we will denote
\theta(\omega)=\gamma(\omega)\omega
.
It is worth recalling the following bounds,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert,\penalty\ \left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2},\penalty\ \frac{\left\lVert c_{\beta}\right\rVert_{1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\leq 1,\penalty\ \qquad\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(\Lambda_{0}),\qquad\Lambda_{0}=\Theta(1).

(L.39)

Our strategy for proving
Theorem

10
is to rewrite the energy gradients
\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]
and
\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]
in a form amenable to Lemma
L.2
between a set of operators and their Fourier Transforms.

Step 1.

For the perturbed Hamiltonian
\bm{H}^{\prime}
, we apply a sequence of approximations to establish

\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta_{-}(\nu)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}-\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]}\Bigg\|\leq\epsilon_{A}

(L.40)

for some
\epsilon_{A}>0
and a function
\theta_{-}
to be soon specified.
Recall we write
\bm{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E}}{{\approx}}\bm{B}
if
\|\bm{A}-\bm{B}\|\leq E
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{1}}}{{\approx}}\mathcal{D}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]

(bounds on the Lamb-shift: Prop.
F.3
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{2}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(simplify: Lemma
L.1
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{3}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}\quad

(secular approximation: Corollary
K.3
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(different blocks
\nu
s decohere)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{4}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(undoing secular approximation: Corollary
K.3
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{5}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta(\nu)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(rounding
\theta
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{6}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta_{-}(\nu)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}.

(dropping positive values of
\theta
)

The approximations
E_{1},E_{2}
are bounded by

\displaystyle{E_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}\left\lVert c_{\beta}\right\rVert_{1}\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\right)=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}})

(L.41)

\displaystyle{E_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert}{\sqrt{2\pi}\tau}\left\lVert c_{\beta}\right\rVert_{1}=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\tau}).

(L.42)

In the approximations
E_{3}
and
E_{4}
, we choose the secular approximation parameter
\mu
such that

\mu<(\Delta_{\nu}-4\delta_{\lambda})/2

(L.43)

with associated errors given by
Corollary

K.3
as

\displaystyle{E_{3}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\left\lVert\hat{f}_{\tau}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2}=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}(\Lambda_{0}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\mu\tau}}),

(L.44)

\displaystyle{E_{4}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\sum_{a,\nu}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}\right\rVert\left\lVert\hat{f}_{\tau}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2}=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}(\Lambda_{0}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\mu\tau}}),

(L.45)

where we applied Eq. (
K.21
) to bound
\left\lVert\hat{f}_{\tau}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{4/{\pi\mu\tau}}
, and used Proposition
L.5
to bound the spectral norm of the sum of jump operators in the second line.

To justify the equality on the fourth line (different blocks
\nu
s decohere), observe the choice of the parameter
\mu
in Eq. (
L.43
) implies

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathds{1}(\left|{\omega^{\prime}-\nu}\right|<\mu+2\delta_{\lambda})=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathds{1}(\left|{\omega^{\prime}-\nu}\right|<\Delta_{\nu}/2),

(L.46)

where
\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})
is given in Eq. (
L.38
).
This ensures that for any given
\omega^{\prime}
,
\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})
can activate at most
one
block of transitions with Bohr frequencies closest to
\nu
(see Figure
4
).
Consequently,

\hat{\bm{S}}^{a{\dagger}}(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})=\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a{\dagger}}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime}).

(L.47)

Next, for the approximation
E_{5}
, we define the following “rounded” function
\bar{\theta}(\omega^{\prime})
where an input
\omega^{\prime}
close to
\nu\in B(\bm{H})
is assigned the same value
\theta(\nu)
, with uniqueness of
\nu
guaranteed by Eq. (
L.46
),

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}(\omega^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}\theta(\nu)\quad&\text{if}\quad\left|{\omega^{\prime}-\nu}\right|\leq\mu+2\delta_{\lambda}\quad\text{for}\quad\nu\in B(\bm{H})\\
\theta(\omega^{\prime})\quad&\text{else}\end{cases}.

(L.48)

This lets us formally pull
\theta(\omega^{\prime})
out of the integral. Of course, this rounding introduces an error scaling with the energy spread multiplied with the derivative

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{\theta}-\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq(2\mu+4\delta_{\lambda})\cdot\left\lVert{\mathrm{d}\theta}/{\mathrm{d}\omega}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

(L.49)

Roughly, this error quantifies how the energy gradient (i.e.,
\theta(\omega)=\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\omega
) changes due to perturbation in Bohr frequency. Thus,

{E_{5}}\leq\left\lVert\theta-\bar{\theta}\right\rVert_{\infty}\bigg\|{\sum_{a,\nu}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}}\bigg\|\left\lVert\hat{f}_{\tau}\cdot\hat{s}_{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}\left(2\mu+4\delta_{\lambda}\right)

(L.50)

where we applied Propositions
L.4
and
L.5
(deferred to
Section

L.5
), and used the fact that
\|\hat{f}_{\tau}\hat{s}_{\mu}\|_{2}\leq\|\hat{f}_{\tau}\|_{2}\leq 1
.

Finally, in the last approximation
E_{6}
, we define the truncated weight

\theta_{-}(\nu)=\theta(\nu)\mathds{1}(\nu\leq\Delta_{\nu}/2).

(L.51)

This truncation has the property that
\theta_{-}(\nu)\leq 0
for each
\nu\in B(\bm{H})
, which ensures the last line is negative semidefinite.
Thus,

\displaystyle{E_{6}}\leq\left\lVert\theta-\theta_{-}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\sum_{a,\nu}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}\right\rVert\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leq\left|{S}\right|\left\lVert\theta-\theta_{-}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad\left\lVert\theta-\theta_{-}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\max_{\omega\geq\Delta_{\nu}/2}\omega\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)\leq\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta},

(L.52)

using the tail bound in Eq. (
F.3
).
Altogether,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{A}

\displaystyle={E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}+E_{4}+E_{5}+E_{6}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\mu+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\mu\tau}}+\delta_{\lambda}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}\right)\right).

(L.53)

We then choose
\mu=\min(\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}/\tau^{1/3},(\Delta_{\nu}-4\delta_{\lambda})/4)
so as to optimize the error
\mathcal{O}(\mu+\Lambda_{0}/\sqrt{\mu\tau})
while subject to the constraint that
\mu<(\Delta_{\nu}-4\delta_{\lambda})/2
.
This choice implies
\Lambda_{0}/\sqrt{\mu\tau}\leq\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}/\tau^{1/3}+2\Lambda_{0}/\sqrt{(\Delta_{\nu}-4\delta_{\lambda})\tau}\leq\mathcal{O}(\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}/\tau^{1/3}+\Lambda_{0}/\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau})
, where we used
\delta_{\lambda}\leq\|\bm{V}\|\leq\Delta_{\nu}/8
which is a combination of
Proposition

L.3
and the assumption in the theorem statement.
This yields the following error bound

\epsilon_{A}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\delta_{\lambda}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}\right)\right).

(L.54)

Step 2.

For the original Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
, we may repeat the above argument with trivial perturbation (
\bm{V}=0
) to get

\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta_{-}(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}_{\approx\nu}-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\right\rVert\leq\epsilon_{B}

(L.55)

for some
\epsilon_{B}>0
.
More detailedly, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{7}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta_{-}(\nu)\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\nu}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\nu}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

(setting
\bm{V}=0
from above)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta_{-}(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}

(Proposition
K.1
and
[\bm{A}_{\nu}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu},\bm{H}]=0
for each
\nu\in B(\bm{H})
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{8}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta_{-}(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}.

(L.56)

The second line is operator Parseval’s identity, where the time evolution simplifies due to commutativity
[\bm{A}_{\nu}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu},\bm{H}]=0
.
The error
E_{7}
can be bounded by the same bounds for
\epsilon_{A}
in Eq. (
L.54
) by setting
\bm{V}=0
(i.e.,
\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}\rightarrow\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}
).

The last line is a brute-force rewriting of
\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}
into
\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}
, which acts on eigenstates of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
instead of
\bm{H}
, with error
E_{8}
bounded by perturbation theory.
This rewriting allows us to prove
Theorem

12
by directly applying Lemma
L.2
between the following set of operators and their Fourier transforms

\displaystyle\{\sqrt{\left|{\theta_{-}(\nu)}\right|}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}\}_{a,\nu}\quad\text{and}\quad\{\sqrt{\left|{\theta_{-}(\nu)}\right|}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\}_{a,\nu,\omega^{\prime}}\quad\text{for the perturbed Hamiltonian}\quad\bm{H}^{\prime}.

We give an explicit error bound on
E_{8}
in Proposition
L.6
(deferred to
Section

L.5
), which yields

\displaystyle{E_{8}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\bigg\|\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\bigg\|\theta_{\max}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\left|{S}\right|\theta_{\max}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}}\right),

(L.57)

where we used the bound
\max_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}|\theta_{-}(\nu)|=\theta_{\max}
provided in the theorem statement.
Collect the errors to bound
\epsilon_{B}=E_{7}+E_{8}\leq\epsilon_{A}+E_{8}
.

Step 3.

Now we may finish the proof of
Theorem

12
by applying Lemma
L.2
.
First, Eq. (
L.55
) implies

\displaystyle-\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta_{-}(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}_{\approx\nu}+\epsilon_{B}\bm{I}\succeq-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\succeq r\bm{O}-\epsilon\bm{I}

(by assumption)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\succeq r\bm{O}^{\prime}-(r\|\bm{O}^{\prime}-\bm{O}\|+\epsilon)\bm{I}.

(L.58)

Similarly, Eq. (
L.40
) implies

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]+\epsilon_{A}\bm{I}

\displaystyle\succeq-\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\theta_{-}(\nu)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{\approx\nu}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle\succeq r\bm{O}^{\prime}-(\epsilon+\epsilon_{B}+r\left\lVert\bm{O}-\bm{O}^{\prime}\right\rVert)\bm{I}.

(L.59)

Note in the last step we used the assumption that
[\bm{O}^{\prime},\bm{H}^{\prime}]=0
and applied
Lemma

L.2
.
Hence, we have shown that
-\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\succeq r\bm{O}^{\prime}-\epsilon^{\prime}\bm{I}
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon+\epsilon_{A}+\epsilon_{B}+r\|\bm{O}-\bm{O}^{\prime}\|
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\left|{S}\right|\Big(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\delta_{\lambda}+\theta_{\max}\frac{\|\bm{V}\|}{\Delta_{\nu}}+r\|\bm{O}-\bm{O}^{\prime}\|\Big)\bigg).

(L.60)

A simpler bound.

We now consider the special case of
\bm{O}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}
,
\bm{O}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}^{\prime}
to derive a simpler bound as in the theorem statement.
Note we have
\|\bm{O}-\bm{O}^{\prime}\|=\|\bm{P}-\bm{P}^{\prime}\|\leq 8\|\bm{V}\|/\Delta_{E}\leq 8\|\bm{V}\|/\Delta_{\nu}
, using
Lemma

L.3
and the fact the spectral gap is lower bounded by the Bohr-frequency gap,
\Delta_{E}\geq\Delta_{\nu}
.
Furthermore generally
\theta_{\max}=\|\theta_{-}\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(\Lambda_{0})
for our choice of
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
in
Eq.

F.4
.
And we always have
\delta_{\lambda}\leq\|\bm{V}\|
by
Proposition

L.3
.
Plugging these into
Eq.

L.60
, we have

\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\left|{S}\right|\Big(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert+(\Lambda_{0}+r)\frac{\|\bm{V}\|}{\Delta_{\nu}}\Big)\bigg).

(L.61)

This concludes our proof of
Theorem

12
.

L.5
Supplementary calculations

In this section, we provide some missing calculations that prove some propositions used in the proof in the previous section.

Proposition L.4

(Bounds on the derivative)
.

There exists an absolute constant
C
such that for any
\beta,\Lambda_{0}
,

\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\theta(\omega)\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}\left(\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}\omega\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)\leq C.

(L.62)

Proof.

By the product rule,

\displaystyle\left|{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\left(\frac{\omega\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\omega^{2}/\Lambda_{0}^{2}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\omega^{2}/2\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\beta\omega\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega}}{(1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega})^{2}}}\right|\leq(const.)

(L.63)

using change of variable
x=\beta\omega
and
y=\omega/\Lambda_{0}
to obtain the absolute constant bound.
∎

Proposition L.5
.

In the prevailing notation,

\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}}\Bigg\|,\quad\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}}\Bigg\|\leq\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}}\Bigg\|.

(L.64)

Proof.

We focus on
\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\approx\nu}
since the case of
\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\nu}
is a special case. Resolve the identity by nearby energy subspaces

\bm{I}=\sum_{E}\bm{P}_{E}=\sum_{\bar{E}}\underset{=:\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}}}{\underbrace{\sum_{E\approx\bar{E}}\bm{P}_{E}}}\quad\text{such that}\quad\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}=\sum_{\bar{E}_{2}-\bar{E}_{1}=\nu}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{2}}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{1}}.

(L.65)

Now, we calculate

\displaystyle\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}_{\approx\nu}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}}\Bigg\|

\displaystyle=\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S,\bar{E}_{2},\bar{E}_{1}}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{1}}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{2}}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{1}}}\Bigg\|=\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S,\bar{E}_{1}}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{1}}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{1}}}\Bigg\|

\displaystyle=\max_{\bar{E}}\bigg\|{\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{1}}\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{P}_{\approx\bar{E}_{1}}}\bigg\|\leq\bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}}\bigg\|.

(L.66)

The last line uses that the operator norm of block-diagonal matrices equals the maximum among the blocks.
∎

Proposition L.6

(Jumps with perturbed Hamiltonian)
.

In the prevailing notation, and for any function
h(\nu)
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}h(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}-\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}h(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\mathcal{O}\Bigg(\Bigg\|{\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}}\Bigg\|\cdot\max_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}\left|{h(\nu)}\right|\cdot\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}}\Bigg).

(L.67)

Proof.

It suffices to set
\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert=1
. Consider the operator Fourier transform with a smooth bump weight
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{2}=1
,

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{a}_{g,\bm{H}}(\omega)\quad\text{for}\quad\hat{g}(\omega)\propto\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{if}\quad\left|{\omega}\right|\geq\frac{\Delta_{\nu}}{2}\\
\mathcal{O}(1)\quad&\text{else}.\end{cases}

(L.68)

which automatically decohere different Bohr frequency blocks (i.e., no need to apply secular approximation). Also, extend the function locally

\displaystyle h(\omega):=\begin{cases}h(\nu)\quad&\text{if}\quad\left|{\omega-\nu}\right|\leq 2\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\end{cases}.

(L.69)

Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(\omega)\bm{A}^{a}_{g,\bm{H}}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}_{g,\bm{H}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in B(\bm{H})}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(\omega)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu^{\prime}})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\hat{g}^{*}(\omega-\nu^{\prime})\hat{g}(\omega-\nu)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu,\in B(\bm{H})}h(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{\hat{g}(\omega-\nu)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bm{H})}h(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\nu}.

(L.70)

Now, we add the perturbation
\bm{H}+\bm{V}
. The insight is that we can introduce an artificial Hamiltonian

\bar{\bm{H}}:=\sum_{E\in\text{spec}(\bm{H})}E\sum_{E^{\prime}\approx E}\bm{P}_{E^{\prime}}\quad\text{such that}\quad\left\lVert\bar{\bm{H}}-\bm{H}\right\rVert\leq 2\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert

(L.71)

with exactly the same spectrum of the original Hamiltonian
\bm{H}
, but with the basis according to the perturbed Hamiltonian
\bm{H}^{\prime}
. Then, the same argument with the artificial Hamiltonian implies

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h(\omega)\bm{A}^{a}_{g,\bar{\bm{H}}}(\omega)^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}_{g,\bar{\bm{H}}}^{a}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\sum_{\nu\in B(\bar{\bm{H}})}h(\nu)(\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu})^{{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}_{\approx\nu}.

(L.72)

Lastly, we may bound the difference by the purification

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{g,\bm{H}}(\omega)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes\ket{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega-\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}_{g,\bar{\bm{H}}}(\omega)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes\ket{\omega}\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bm{A}^{a}_{\bm{H}}(t)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes g(t)\ket{t}\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bm{A}^{a}_{\bar{\bm{H}}}(t)\otimes\ket{a}\otimes g(t)\ket{t}\mathrm{d}t\right\rVert

(Fourier Transform is unitary)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left|{2\left\lVert\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bar{\bm{H}}t}\right\rVert g(t)}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}t}=\mathcal{O}(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}}).

(L.73)

The factor of 2 is due to left and right Hamiltonian evolution

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bar{\bm{H}}t}\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bar{\bm{H}}t}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}\bm{A}^{a}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bar{\bm{H}}t})+(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bar{\bm{H}}t})\bm{A}^{a}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\bar{\bm{H}}t}.

(L.74)

To evaluate the integral, we use that
\left\lVert\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{H}t}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bar{\bm{H}}t}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\bm{H}-\bar{\bm{H}}\right\rVert t\leq 2\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert t
and that
g(t)
is rapidly decaying for large
\left|{t}\right|\geq\frac{1}{\Delta_{\nu}}
. To conclude the proof, use the purification tricks (
Lemma

K.1
).
∎

L.6
Monotonicity of gradient on a subspace

For our proof that
BQP
-hard Hamiltonians has no suboptimal local minima in
Appendix

J
, we will need the following refinements of
Theorem

12
where the gradient operator acts on a low-energy subspace with an excitation gap.
Intuitively, we care only about the Bohr-frequency gap restricted to the low-energy subspace
\bm{H}\bm{Q}
instead of the full Hilbert space; the gradient on that subspace should not be sensitive to the excited states above the excitation gap.

Corollary L.1

(Monotonicity of gradient on a subspace;
Corollary

H.1
restated)
.

Consider a Hamiltonian
\bm{H}=\sum_{\bar{E}}\bar{E}\bm{P}_{\bar{E}}
and its perturbation
\bm{H}^{\prime}:=\bm{H}+\bm{V}
. Let
\bm{P}
be the ground space projector for
\bm{H}
and
\bm{P}^{\prime}
be the corresponding perturbed eigensubspace of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
.
Let
\bm{Q}
be a low-energy eigensubspace projector of
\bm{H}
(i.e.,
\bm{Q}=\sum_{E\leq E_{\bm{Q}}}\bm{P}_{E}
for
E_{\bm{Q}}\in\text{Spec}(\bm{H})
) with excitation gap
\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
.
Assume
\frac{\|\bm{V}\|\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}\leq\frac{1}{144}\Delta_{\nu}
where
\Delta_{\nu}:=\min_{\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}\in B(\bm{H}|_{\bm{Q}})}|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|
is the Bohr-frequency gap of
\bm{H}
within the subspace
\bm{Q}
.
For any
\beta,\tau>0
, let
\mathcal{L}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}}_{a},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\sum_{a\in S}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}}_{a}
be thermal Lindbladians with jumps
\{\bm{A}^{a}\}_{a\in S}
, where
\left\lVert\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\leq 1
and the transition weight
\gamma_{\beta}(\omega)
is given by Eq. (
F.4
).
Then we have the monotone property that

\displaystyle-\bm{Q}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{H}]\bm{Q}\succeq r\bm{Q}(\bm{I}-\bm{P})-\epsilon\bm{I}\quad\text{implies}\quad-\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}^{\prime{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\bm{Q}^{\prime}\succeq r\bm{Q}^{\prime}(\bm{I}-\bm{P}^{\prime})-\epsilon^{\prime}\bm{I}

(L.75)

where
\bm{Q}^{\prime}
projects onto the perturbed eigensubspace of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
identified with
\bm{Q}
, and

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon+\left|{S}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}^{2/3}}{\tau^{1/3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\nu}\tau}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\nu}/4}}{\beta}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\bm{Q}}/4}}{\beta}

\displaystyle+\left(1+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\Delta_{\nu}}\right)\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+r\Big(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\nu}}\Big)\bigg).

(L.76)

Proof.

The idea is that
\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\bm{Q}^{\prime}
essentially depends only on the low energy subspace
\bm{Q}^{\prime}
and the corresponding restricted transition
\bm{Q}^{\prime}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{Q}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\bm{Q}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{1}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\bm{Q}^{\prime}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(Prop.
F.3
and Lemma
L.1
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{2}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\bm{Q}^{\prime}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\bm{Q}^{\prime}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(excitation gap
\Delta_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}
of
\bm{Q}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{R}}^{\prime a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{R}}^{\prime a}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(set
\bm{R}^{\prime a}:=\bm{Q}^{\prime}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{Q}^{\prime}
and use that
[\bm{Q}^{\prime},\bm{H}^{\prime}]=0
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{3}}}{{\approx}}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}}_{\{\bm{R}^{\prime a}\}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}]

(unsimplify: Prop.
F.3
and Lemma
L.1
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{4}}}{{\approx}}\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}}_{\{\bm{R}^{a}\}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}].

(change the jumps to
\bm{R}^{a}=\bm{Q}\bm{A}^{a}\bm{Q}
)

The approximation
E_{2}
inserts the low-energy projector
\bm{Q}^{\prime}
.
To do so, we resolves the identity by
\bm{I}=\bm{Q}^{\prime}+(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}^{\prime})
and uses that

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\bm{Q}^{\prime}\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}^{\prime})\hat{\bm{A}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(excitation gap of
\bm{Q}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{21}}}{{\approx}}\sum_{a\in S}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\theta(\omega^{\prime})\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})^{{\dagger}}\hat{\bm{S}}^{a}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}

(secular approximation for
(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}^{\prime})\bm{A}^{a}\bm{Q}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E_{22}}}{{\approx}}0

(
\mu\ll\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
,
\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\beta\gg 1
. )

That is, we need the excitation gap to be large so that
(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}^{\prime})\bm{A}^{a}\bm{Q}^{\prime}
have a vanishing contribution to the gradient. These error combines
E_{2}=E_{21}+E_{22}
where

\displaystyle{E_{21}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert f_{\tau}\cdot(1-\hat{s}_{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2}=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}(\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\mu\tau}})

(L.77)

\displaystyle{E_{22}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert\max_{\omega^{\prime}\geq\Delta_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}-\mu}{\left|{\theta(\omega^{\prime})}\right|}\left\lVert f_{\tau}\cdot\hat{s}_{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left|{S}\right|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\Delta_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}-\mu)/2}}{\beta}.

(L.78)

Thus, we consider a safe choice of
\mu=\Delta_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}/2
. Also, since the perturbation is small by assumption,
\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{144}\Delta_{\nu}\frac{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert}\leq\frac{1}{144}\Delta_{\bm{Q}}
, the excitation gap remains large
\Delta_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}\geq\Delta_{\bm{Q}}-2\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\geq\Delta_{\bm{Q}}-\frac{1}{72}\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\geq\Delta_{\bm{Q}}/2
.
The third line (after approximation
E_{2}
) formally disposes of the excited state of
\bm{H}^{\prime}
above
E^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}
by defining a modified Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}:=\bm{H}^{\prime}\bm{Q}^{\prime}+E^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}(\bm{I}-\bm{Q}^{\prime})

(L.79)

for the Fourier transform
\hat{\bm{R}^{\prime a}}(\omega^{\prime})
. This Hamiltonian is merely a proof artifact, and one can also set the energy of the excited subspace
\bm{I}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}
to be infinity. The error
E_{3}
is merely the error to put it back to the form of an energy gradient.

The approximation
E_{4}
changes the jumps with norm bounded by

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{R}^{\prime a}\otimes\ket{a}-\sum_{a\in S}\bm{R}^{a}\otimes\ket{a}\right\rVert\leq 2\sqrt{\left\lVert\sum_{a\in S}\bm{A}^{a{\dagger}}\bm{A}^{a}\right\rVert}\left\lVert\bm{Q}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}\right\rVert=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}})

(L.80)

using subspace perturbation bounds
\left\lVert\bm{Q}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq 8\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}
(Lemma
L.3
). Since the (suitably normalized) gradient operator
\frac{1}{2\left|{S}\right|\left\lVert\bm{O}\right\rVert}\cdot\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}}[\bm{O}]
can be block-encoded using
\mathcal{O}(1)
block-encodings of the jumps (
Theorem

9
,
Proposition

F.7
,
Proposition

F.8
), perturbation to the jumps propagates to the gradient operator by

{E_{4}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\bigg\|\sum_{a}\bm{R}^{\prime a}\ket{a}-\sum_{a}\bm{R}^{a}\ket{a}\bigg\|\cdot\left\lVert\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}\right\rVert\right)=\left|{S}\right|\mathcal{O}\Big(\left\lVert\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}\right\rVert\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}\Big).

(L.81)

To summarize, the above gives the bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{Q}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}]\bm{Q}^{\prime}-\mathcal{L}^{{\dagger}\beta,\tau,\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}}_{\{\bm{R}^{a}\}}[\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}]\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left|{S}\right|\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert^{3/4}}{\tau^{1/4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}\tau}}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\Delta_{\bm{Q}}/4}}{\beta}+\frac{\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert}{\Delta_{\bm{Q}}}\left\lVert\bm{H}^{\prime}_{\bm{Q}^{\prime}}\right\rVert\right)

(L.82)

using
\left\lVert\bm{H}^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\bm{V}\right\rVert\leq 2\left\lVert\bm{H}\right\rVert
and
